Variables

PREC_ → Precaution to observe before initiating or during treatment.
PROC_ → Initial tretament or procesure.
SUB_ → Subtretament or subprocesure inside the main treatment. It is like a detail with a lot of consideraton respect to the treatment.
REHAB_ → Stage of rehabilitation
HEAL_ → Stage of tissue healing
OBS_ → Observations that the PT has to notice in certain procedures of the treatment to take decisions.
DUE_ → Due to = sources of limitation
Next_/with another variable/ → Go there next
MSK1_ → Musculoskeletal 1 subject
Q_ → From Knowledge Ckeck/Quizzes
PL_ → Pantilles
REF_ → Refer to this for consultation/a reference, table, etc.

Things to consider when constructing the document:
	.Patient case style "Kin Case or Kin Case Descriptors txt" in which everything about the case is systematically organized.
	.Standard/initial variables that help us navigate the document or tell us where to go on it; i.e. rehab stage is one, immobilizated, recent surgery, age, etc.
	.Variables that are a condition to do or not to do, to start or to finish something.
	.Advantages/Disatvantages
	.When or when not
	.Precautions or Alerts
	.Division depending on type of treatment: Gate, Posture, Rehab, endurance, stregth, etc.
	.Expected results, efects, etc., of performing the thing.
	.patient Variables that have to be taken at all times or very often in which we can base our treatment.
	.How long on the therapy or after the surgery? All about times; a timeline; healing tissue, neurological changes, etc.
	.A list of priorities: elasticity, isometrics, concentric, excentric.
	.All the symptoms: weakness (needs isometrics), pain, swelling, rigidity (needs elasticity), like that etc.

Γ Advises to become a good investigator [MSK1_QuestToResearch] (Needs more elaboration (!!))
	For questions asked to the pt., we want to get to the cuases, so we can decide what to do.
		Ask yourself what I get from the question that help me
			to understand what structure/s are implicated
			to make decision on what to do
			to know what to change from the pt. side (increase, reduce, transform)
			Know first what are the feelings, types of pain, consequences, etc. of each condition
			What are you asking, why are you asking, and what are you going to do with that information 
	For all the subjects, specially tests, ask yourself:
		Recognize/Recall general purpose of each concept
			1. Why is this technique done?
			2. "Why is the purpose of doing something?"/"what information provides me?"
			3. "Can I picture it in my head?"/"If I wanted to do it, where/how would I start?"
				Where I have to be and the pt., or put my hands, or have rady, what is next, etc.

		meu.Stage of Healing
			Dense Connective Tissue
				Different interventions/treatments will be effective in different stages of healing process
				Could do harm if apply wrong treatment in wrong stage
				------------------------------------------
				PREC_DenseTissueHealingStage (bone healing)
			Synovial Joints & Connective Tissues (Osseous Tissue ¿?) (what connective tissue? we had dense before ¿?)
				Variables to take in account
					Synovial Joints = designed for mobility
					------------------------------------------------
					Mobility: A joint allows movement to occur between two limb segments.
					Stability: A joint must be stable to support body during weight-bearing.
						There may need to be stability at one joint so that another joint can perform a mobility task.
					------------------------------------------------
					Stress-Strain Response
						What is happening in tissue when tensile stresses are applied
						Stress
							The amount of tension across a cross-sectional area of tissue. Mechanical force applied to tissue when stretching.
							(N/m2) = Force (N) / Area (m2)
								Types of Stress
									Compressive stress
										Caused by forces that tend to shrink materials
									Tensile stress
										Caused by forces that tend to stretch materials
									Shear stress
										Caused by internal forces acting parallel to a plane
						Strain
							The amount of proportional elongation that occurs as the result of applying tensile stress
						----------------------
						Stress-Strain Curves (Chart) [☠.↓Ex].Repas.Kin
							Toe Region           Low stress, high strain
							Linear Region        Hi stress, high strain, slight tissue failure
							Progressive Failure  Internal failure with intact structure
							Major Failure        Shear & Rupture
							Complete Rupture     Structure breaks
						----------------------
						Different portions of the bone are better suited for handling different types of stresses
						According to the Stress-Strain Curves tissues respond to stress differently
							Bone connective tissues
								More stress with less Strain ¿?
							Dense connective tissues
								Less stress with less Strain ¿?
							Articular Cartilage
								Less stress with less Strain ¿?
								b/c type II collagen is weaker than type I, resistance is limited and may be easily damaged with too much force
						---------------------
						Unloaded (no tension)
						Tension
						Compression
						Bending
						Shear
						Torsion
						Combined
						loading
					How articular cartilage responds to loading with:
						Small increases in loading may increase tissue quality over time, but excessive loading will cause tissue damage.
						--------------------
						Sheer forces
							Articular Cartilage
								Helps protect against sheer forces that create friction parallel to the articular surface
								Results in stretch of collagen fibers
							Protection Against Sheer Forces
								The structure of articular cartilage helps protect against sheer forces that create friction parallel to the articular surface like in the following image
									Orientation of Surface Fibers
										-Sheer force results in stretch of collagen fibers.
										-Stress–Strain response in articular cartilage is similar to dense connective tissue.
										-But, remember, because type II collagen is weaker than type I, resistance is limited and may be easily damaged with too much force.
						Compressive forces
							Response to Excessive Compressive Loading
								Damage to the collagen fiber network results in:
								“Washing out” of proteoglycans
								Both result in loss of the ability to respond to compressive AND shear forces
						Impact forces
							Cartilage responds to impact loading
						Reduced loading

The point of all of the testings is:
	What are the imparements that resulted from the issue or
	What are the imparements that are causing the issue
		---------------------------
		Is it b/c
			weak mm.
			the mm. do not move enogh
			of the lack of flexibility on certain mm.
			etc.
				-----------------------
				This way we can find out
					What are the imparements we can treat/What can I do about it?
						Γ An imparement is what we can treat as a PTs	

GENERAL SECTION

	////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
	Tests and Measures (ROM, MMT, Stretch)

	Typically performed
		Before
			performing tests that provide more specific information about underlying pathology
		After
			history and observation

	ROM Assessment (AROM, AAROM, & PROM) [KIN_ROMAssess]
		Assessment of ROM vs. Measurement of ROM
			Assessment of ROM is (OBS_AssesQualityQuantity)
				observation of a patient’s overall ability to move a given body part
					Considers quality as well as quantity of movement and includes determination of:
						The patient’s willingness to move
						The effort required to move
						The presence or absence of pain during motion
						If the movement occurs strictly in the appropriate plane or drifts into other planes
							If drifts into other planes, this may: OBS_mPlane
								Occur due to activation of muscles other than the prime mover(s)
								Be due to pain and/or weakness
			Measurement of ROM refers only to
				quantification of the patient’s ability to move using a goniometer or other measurement device or system
		-----------------------------------------
		Examining a patient’s physiologic motion
			1st. Presentation
				Hello!, [name]
			2nd. Find out
				I believe you are here because... or ask what is about
				Depending of if it is the Knee, the Hip, etc we'll chose the kind of group of exercises
			3rd. Assesing ROM
				1 pt. AROM (Bilateraly alway it is appropiate) (take measure if needed) (ask for pain)
					If unable to perform movement agains gravity, gravity minimazed
					-----------------------------------------------------------------
					When assessing ROM, it helps to use the following systematic process: (this could contain repeats!)
						1. Assess Active Range of Motion (AROM) of the body segment 
						2. Compare bilateral motion simultaneously when it makes sense to do so 
						3. Ask the patient about pain or other symptoms during movement 
						4. If motion appears full and pain free, there USUALLY isn’t a need to do Passive Range of Motion (PROM) 
						5. If uncertain if patient has moved through full available range may use overpressure to be sure 
						6. If motion painful or (no serà &? ¿?) not full ROM:
							Measure the amount of motion 
							Right then and there! Don’t make them move in pain twice! 
						7. Have goniometer ready in hand 
						8. Assess PROM 
						9. In the patient’s most relaxed position (Most often supine) 
						10. Again, ask about presence of pain or other symptoms 
						11. If full and pain free no need to measure PROM
						------------------------------------------------- (Formal exposition of this point - REPEAT)
						Steps for assessing the ROM of a given joint in the correct order
							1. Have the patient perform AROM of the body segment
							2. Measure AROM deficits using a goniometer
							3. Therapist assesses PROM
							4. Measure PROM deficits using a goniometer
						Range of Motion Assessment
							Step 1 - Assess AROM of the body segment
								1. Ask the patient about pain or other symptoms during movement
								2. Compare bilaterally motion simultaneously when it makes sense to do so
								-------------------------------
								Scenario 1 – Full ROM & NO Pain (seems perfect)
									If motion appears full and pain free, you usually do not need to do PROM
										-In which of the following situations does the PT NOT need to "assess" (how that is?) (we are not mentioning "measure") shoulder ER PROM? [MSK1_L01]
											What you do not need to do is measure AROM
											---------------------------------------------
											✓The patient has full ER AROM but it is painful [AssessVSmeasure_dr6yhxxi]
												-You will still move them pasively because you want to compare to the other side too
												-You want to make sure if it is contractile or not pushing them to the limit
													If still painful
														-Non-contractile or
														-Could be that we are stretching the muscle to much; therefore, evoking the pain 
											✓The patient has limited ER AROM but it is not painful
												-You want to know if it is weakness due to oter than a neurological issue, so push (PROM) to see if they can go further
													If they can
														it is a neurological issue
													If not
														-It is a tigtness issue (needs elasticity)
														-They may be afraid to go farther b/c of the pain that they do nat have at that range but they may going further
											✓The patient has limited and painful ER AROM
												-Because it is limited on AROM and you want to test PROM to find out if contractile or non-contractile or both
											✓✓The PT should assess PROM in all of these situations
										Question 8
										    A patient has full hip flexion AROM but reports pain during the movement. Which of the following statements describes the best course of action by the physical therapist?
										        ✓The therapist should observe PROM hip flexion but does not need to measure it
										        	-See [AssessVSmeasure_dr6yhxxi]
										        	-(Γ)Push to (GM.II or III), if not painfull, probably non-contractile¿?, then if you push to (GM.IV) we elicit the pain again (not good, twice in pain), and we do not find more info by the way 
										        The therapist does not need to observe nor measure hip flexion PROM
										        The therapist should measure PROM but does not need to observe it
										        The therapist should observe and measure PROM hip flexion
									    -----------------------------------------------------------------------
										AROM of a joint motion is not limited or painful. Immediately following this situation, the PT should usually: [MSK1_L01]
											✗Measure the degrees of AROM through which the patient has moved
											✗Assess PROM of that joint motion
											✗Perform an MMT of that joint motion
											✓Defer measurement of AROM and move onto another motion
										An exception to deferring measurement of AROM that is not limited might be (When you still have to measure AROM even if it is not limited?): [MSK1_L01]
											✗When joint AROM motion is painful
												We want to see it pasively, but if it is obviouly the same, and not limited, we will not get more info from this measure
											✗When joint AROM motion is not painful
												Since it is not painful, we do not need to measure
											✗If PROM of the joint is greater than AROM
												This shouldn't be the case
													Do the exam again or the patient is not understanding what to do
											✓If AROM of the joint is excessive compared to the opposite side
												We want to now if the pt. is hypermobile (joint) in that area
													Imparement do to
														Instability (more muscle support need it there)
											✗All of these situations
									-If you are uncertain if the patient has moved through full available range of motion, you may use overpressure to be sure.
								Scenario 2 - Limited ROM with Pain
									Motion is painful or does not appear to be full ROM.
										Measure the amount of motion
											-You should measure motion right then and there! Don’t make them move in pain twice!  
											-Have goniometer ready in hand
							Step 2 -Assess PROM	
								Assess PROM in the patient’s most relaxed position (most often supine)
								Again, ask about presence of pain or other symptoms
								If full and pain free, there is no need to measure PROM
								If limited, measure the involved side
					-----------------------------------------------------------------
					AROM
						Theorethical Method
							We show the patient how to do he movement (do not touch the patient)
							We ask the patient to do the movement later (do not touch)
							We ask the patient to do the movement again and we asses the muscle touching it
						Realistic Method
							-If patient knows:
								-Make the patient to do it first (for that group of exercises); good side or bilateraly, to see the difference later
									Then, make the patient to do it first; bad side
								-Take the measure of the bad side
								-Go to PROM section
							-If patient does not knows (try everything on that group of exercises):
								Make the patient to do the movement (can be both sides at the same time)
								If good, do not use more time on that; do not measure
								If bad, then make the patient to do it first; bad side
								Take the measure of the bad side
								Go to PROM section
				2 PT. PROM (take measure) (ask for pain)
					We show the patient how to do the movement (do not touch the patient)
						We can skip this step. Once the pt. is at the end after AROM, ask to relax and "push farther & end feel"
					Then, patient does the movement
					Then, we do the movement on him (touch) and measure
		------------------------
		Question 1: 
			Are AROM and PROM the same, or different?
				(Γ)
					a.If the same and full, all Ok
					b.If the same and not full (how bad is it?), research more
						I.Is it normal?
						II.Is it due to pain?
						III.Is bilateral weakness?
					c.If different, research bad side taking as a reference the good one
		Question 2: 
			Are both AROM and PROM painful, or is only AROM painful?
				Γ Contractile, non-contractile, or both
		Question 3: 
			Is motion limited, but not painful actively nor passively?
				Γ Similar to Question 1::b.II. (see above)
		------------------------
		Pain and/or limitation Present? (Next_OBS_AssesQualityQuantity)
			✗PROM only → Not possible in theory
				Could be free of pain at AROM in non-involved structure and painful at PROM due to the stretching on the invoverd side.
			AROM only → Muscle and tendon injury
			AROM & PROM: → Injury to joint capsule, ligaments, articular cartilage; possibly also muscle/tendon injury
		Limitation & no pain
			AROM only → Weak muscles. → Neurological Origin
			------------------------------------------------
			Possible Source of ROM Limitation
				Muscle and tendon injury
				Injury to joint capsule, ligaments, articular
				Cartilage; possibly also muscle/tendon injury
				Not possible in theory
				Weak muscles
		------------------------
		Contractile
			Muscle
			Tendon
		Non-Contractile
			Γ From out to in
				ligaments
				capsule
				meniscus
				articular cartilage
		------------------------
		Interpretation of ROM Findings (!!E)
			Source of the ROM limitation
				-Once the source of the ROM limitation is determined, the PT can perform other follow up tests and measures to try and confirm if this is truly the source DUE_Source
					-If the limitation is due to pain: Question about location and intensity of pain
						OBS_mPlane
					-If the limitation is due to muscle weakness: Muscle performance testing
						OBS_mPlane
					-If the limitation is due to lack of flexibility in muscles/tendons (Non-Elastic/Non-Flexible (Rigidity): Flexibility tests. 
					-If the limitation is due to decreased accessory joint motion and joint mobilization is necessary we perform: Joint mobility assessment [MSK1_L4]
						Restrictions between joint surfaces 
						Hypomobile joint capsule
			Importance of Range of Motion Findings (I.e. Practic example)
				Contractile VS. Non-contractile tissue (or both?)
					Limitation of AROM without pain and normal PROM?
						Muscle weakness
					Limitation and/or pain with AROM but not PROM?
						Injury affecting contractile (muscle) tissue
					Limitation and/or pain with both AROM and PROM?
						Injury affecting non-contractile (capsular, ligamentous) tissue or both, contractile and non-contractile
				Capsular Pattern, or Non-Capsular Pattern? ¿?
					Limitation of x>y>z
			The Implications of Joint "End Feel" and Tissue Irritability [KIN_JointEndFeel]
				End Feel
					Can only be assessed with PROM
					Sensation PT detects at end of motion when over-pressure is applied
					Provides information concerning structure that is limiting motion
						PT should consider, is the end feel:
							Similar to expected end feel?
							Something unexpected?
							Occuring at expected end of motion or before?
							ΓSame side to side?
					------------------------------ [☠.1]
					Types (!!E.Grade & Doc).Kin
						.Normal
							Hard/Bony Γ(extexion/separation) ← Only?¿?
								An abrupt stop to movement with no give at the end of range
								I.e.: Elbow extenston
							Soft Γ(flexion/aproximation)
								-A gradual increase in restance at the end of the range as when soft tissue in adjacent limb segments approximate
									I.e.: Elbow flexion
							Firm Γ(extexion/separation)
								An abrupt increase in the resistance with some give at end of the range
								Tissue stretch, capsular stretch, ligamentous stretch
								I.e.: Ankle dorsiflexion
						.Pathological
							Muscular End Feel
								Abrupt end feel with some "rebound" due to a reflexive muscle spasm and pain
								Due to Acute joint imflamation or fracture? 
							Springy End Feel
								An abrupt end feel with "rebound" due to an intenal joint derangement
								I.e.: Springy & limited knee extension - Due to meniscus tear
							Empty End Feel
								No restriction is felt but the movement is stopped by the patient due to pain or apprehension
				Tissue Irritability
					Symptoms produced during ROM testing
					May be applied to any PT test performed
					Tissue's ability to handle physical stress
					------------------------------------------
					Presumably related to physical status and the extent of inflammatory activity present
					Typically expressed in terms of:
						How easy is to provoke pain?
						How long does pan last after provoked?
						Γ Severity: the level of pain
					Important for guiding
						How conservative to be during physical exam?
						Clinical decisions regarding treatment frequency, intensity, duration, and type
		Reliability of Goniometric Measurements
			Intra-rater: much variation but 3˚-4˚ for both UE & LE
			Inter-rater: 5˚ for UE, 6˚ for LE
		----------------------------------------------------------
		``Active insufficiency vs. Passive Insufficiency
			Specially for multi-joint muscles
				When assessing ROM and strength in the hand, wrist, and elbow
					-When making a fist, the fingers are flexed which lengthens the extensor tendons across the wrist, MCP, and IP joints, preventing full motion into flexion
					-When assessing strength and range of motion in these areas, it is important to be sure all joints are in a position that limits active or passive insufficiency
					--------------------
					Active insufficiency Γ(mm shortened = restricted force) 
						-Occurs when a multi-joint muscle shortens over BOTH joints simultaneously, and creates so much slack, that muscle tension is almost completely lost
						-This results in the muscle appearing to be weaker than normal
						-This concept relates to assessment of AROM and manual muscle testing
					Passive insufficiency Γ(mm stretched = restricted movement) 
						-Occurs when the multi-joint muscle is lengthened to its fullest extent at both joints, but also preventing the full ROM of each joint it crosses
						-This results in the joint(s) seeming to move less than they actually can
						-Applies to assessment of AROM and PROM
		------------------------------------------------------------------------------
		``(Γ)My Research about sources of ROM Limitation ← (See 'Kin Sim.txt') ccc

	MMT Manual Muscle Testing/Resistive Testing - Muscle Weakness
		General principles
			``Stabilization or Fixation
				-Is required to isolate desired joint movement and/or muscle action.
				-Provided manually by the PT and internally by the patient’s more proximal muscles.
				``-This is one reason we typically test proximal muscles before testing distal ones, to ensure the patient is capable of stabilizing the proximal muscles.
			Substitution occurs when 
				Substitution should always be discouraged!
				A muscle or muscle groups attempt to compensate for a lack of function of a weak or paralyzed muscle.
					I.e.: a patient may shrug his shoulder using upper trapezius to compensate for weak rotator cuff or deltoid musculature
				Cue the patient to hold the position without grabbing on to the table, i.e.
			Resistance or Pressure
				Amount of force applied to determine the “strength” of the muscle being tested.
				Dependent on:
					Size of patient & therapist
					Body part being tested
					Leverage used by therapist
					Age of patient
			Breaking
				Is when the PT’s resistance is able to move the patient’s body segment out of position when the appropriate amount of resistance is applied.
				Breaking should be avoided when there is potential for damage to internal structural components of the muscle.
		-----------------------------------------------
		MMT Good Practices [MSK1_C1_7]
			Patient Positioning
				Place the patient in a position where they can activate a specific muscle or muscle group in relative isolation.
			Stabilization or Fixation
				Apply necessary stabilization or fixation of the body region proximal to the muscle/group being tested.
			Movement Against Gravity
				If you have not yet seen the movement performed against gravity, ask the patient to perform this
				But – you probably SHOULD HAVE already seen this
			Apply Resistance
				Apply resistance at an appropriate place within range
				When possible STOP testing before BREAKING!
			Observation & Recording
				Observe for substitutions and correct accordingly
				IF UNCERTAIN OF THE OUTCOME, perform test on same muscle 1 to 2 additional times as necessary
				Record the result
		-----------------------------------------------
		Order of Muscles Tested PREC_mOrderTest
			To prevent fatigue of proximal muscles, order of the MMT should be from Proximal to Distal.
			MMT progression should be planned to prevent frequent change of patient position (All muscles in prone, etc).
		-----------------------------------------------
		Theorethical Method
			We do the movement first (touch).
			Later, the patient does the movement.
			Then, we stabilize first, and second we apply force against the patient and quantify (1st stabilize, 2nd press).
			Asign grade.
			If patient can not move agains gravity (3/5), we test gravity eliminated position.
			-------------------------------------------
			Place the patient in a position where they activate a specific muscle or muscle group in relative isolation.
			Stabilize the body region proximal to the muscle/muscle group being tested
			Apply manual resistance to the body region distal to the muscle/muscle group being tested.
			Assign a strength grade of 0-5 based on the patient’s ability to maintain their position against the therapist’s resistance.
			Once the patient is position, the goniometer or inclinometer may be aligned to quantify the amount of movement
				Γ Açò seria per a ROM. Ací, estem parlant de MMT
		Realistic Method
			-Standing behind the patient is not the best position for the PT when performing these tests as it does not allow for optimal leverage or to allow the PT to see if the patient is experiencing pain (by the look on the face)
			-------------------------------------------
			1st PROM (is not PROM exactly, it's a help) for Showing or only Showing without PROM, 2nd AROM, 3rd apply pressure MMT.
				-No need to make the patient to go back and do AROM since we helped and we felt the streght (pt. could be in pain also), so we judge if proceed or not with the MMT.
			Basicament estas ensenyat-li al pacient que fer primer
			We do this because something is wrong already (we found out previously on Assesing), or
			We could not find it on Assesing stage.
			If we fail to find out in this step, nothing is wrong.
		---------------------------------------
		ROM|| Numerical Grade Test Performance. (!!E.Grade & Doc).Kin
		ROM||       5/5 Patient completes full ROM against GRAVITY with MAX  Resistance.
		ROM||       4+/5 patient completes full ROM against GRAVITY with nearly MAX Resistance.
		ROM||       4/5 Patient completes full ROM against GRAVITY with MOD Resistance.
		ROM||       4-/5 Patient completes ROM against GRAVITY with nearly MOD resistance.
		ROM||       3+/5 Patient completes ROM against GRAVITY with MIN resistance.
		ROM||       3/5
						Patient completes ROM against GRAVITY with NO resistance.
						Therapist places structure agains gravity (helps patient) and patient is able to hold.
		ROM|| FAIR  3/-5 Therapist completes -2/3 ROM against gravity OR full ROM gravity minimized with MOD resistance.
		ROM|| POOR+ 2+/5 Therapist completes -1/3 ROM against gravity OR full ROM gravity minimized with MIN resistance.
		ROM|| POOR  2/5 Therapist completes full ROM gravity minimized with NO resistance.
						Gravity Minimized Positions
							If muscle cannot perform desired movement against gravity.
								Moving segment may move in a plane or on a surface that runs perpendicular to the force of gravity
		ROM|| IPOOR 2-/5 Therapist unable to complete full ROM gravity minimized.
		ROM|| TRACE 1/5 Muscle contraction can be palpated but NO joint motion.
		ROM|| ZERO  0/5 Therapist exhibits No palpable contaction.
		--------------------------------------
		Numeric Grade
			Normal 5
			Good   4
			Fair   3
			Poor   2
			Trace  1
			Zero   0
			---------------
			Subjective analysis of the patient’s ability to hold position
				5 & 4
			Not subjective
			3, 2, 1, & 0
		---------------------------------------
		Manual Muscle Tests (MMT) vs. Selective Tissue Tension Testing (STT) (AKA Selective Tissue Testing or Resistive Testing)
			These tests are performed in the same manner, but provide slightly different information
			------------------------------------
			Similarities
				To detect abnormalities
				Assessment of
					strength,
					weakness, and
					pain
			------------------------------------ Differences
			MMT (Test Strength)
				More contractile tissue (muscle) oriented + pt. have full ROM (!!E)
				What is the purpose?/Performed for the purpose of
					Quantifying (at least subectively) the strength with which the muscle can contract
					Making sure that the force output it is equal in both sides
					Find out about Contractile vs. Non-Contractile
				What is the outcome of an MMT?
					A grade of 0–5 (6 in total)
						What do we do when we assign a grade?
							Stablish a baseline of strenght
				What other kind of info can you get when a muscle contracts?/What you would feel?
					If it is injured (pain)
						Then, use this info as diagnostic method
			STT [MSK1_C1_7]
				More non-contractile tissue oriented + pt. do not have full ROM (!!E)
					-So, if you evaluate (eval), for example, shoulder abd and it is not painful and full ROM you select MMT. However, later, if you do flexion and it is extremelly painful or they cannot get there on that gravity position, you select STT. You can mix both in the same evaluation.
					-Let's say that, for example, for the shoulder, they can't get there (not full ROM) because of a restriction like capsule or ligament, even though is not painful, then you cannot quantify it as a MMT, but you still want to see if it is painful and what is the strength, then you use STT. This will guide your documentation; how you quentify each of those.
				A variation of manual muscle testing (MMT) that is more appropriate for certain patients.
				It biases more the pain aspect than MMT (not numerical scale)
				Even less objective than MMT, we want this one to guide the evaluation
				How does STT helps us to assess irritability? (!!E)
					How fast they get the pain?
					How easily are the patient's symptoms provoked?
					Once provoked, how long does it take for symptoms to subside?
					---------------------------------
					Irritability Classification: SEVERITY (pain intensity) + IRRITABILITY (resistance intensity) (classificació meua!!)
						More combinations of these between them exist, but in general:
						---------------------------------------------- (Qué significa açò? ¿?)
						High severity     with high irritability
							High pain     with light resistance
						Moderate severity with moderate irritability
							Moderate pain with moderate resistance
						Low severity      with Low irritability
							Low pain      with high resistance
				---------------------------
				What is the purpose?/Performed for two purposes: (!!E)
					To determine (very generally) if
						1.The muscle strong or weak
						2.The contraction of the muscle produces pain
				What are the outcomes?
					Strong and pain-free/painless
					Strong and painful
					Weak and pain-free/painless
					Weak and painful
				STT Procedure [MSK_V01_07]
					1.Perform testing of specific muscles/muscle groups as with a typical MMT EXCEPT:
						Apply resistance very gradually
						If minimally or not painful, slowly increase resistance
						Cease applying resistance before pain is significant
					2.After applying resistance consider:
						Based on amount of resistance, would you classify the muscle as STRONG or WEAK?
						Was contraction of that muscle/movement PAINFUL or PAINLESS?
				Conclusions based on STT Ratings (in relation to a muscle) (!!E)
					Strong and pain-free/painless
						No lesion of contractile tissue
					Strong and painful
						No tears in the muscle
						Minor lesion/injury of contractile tissue
					Weak and pain-free/painless (!!E)
						Weakness due to neurological damage
						Significantly atrophied tissue (only weakness)
						Possible, full rupture of muscle tissue
							Cóm pot un m. que està totalment trencat xl tendó, tindre força? ¿?
					Weak and painful
						Major lesion of contractile tissue
						Atrophy
						Neurological (nerve supply) issue
						Total rupture¿? of the tendon that does not hurt with profound weakness
							More rupture, more painful
							(Γ)Then, total rupture or partially ruptured (a tear) here? ¿? 
					---------------------------------------------------------
					Question 3
						A physical therapist performs a selective tissue tension test on the biceps. Which of the following results would be indicative of an injury to a nerve that innervates the biceps?
							Weak and Painful
							Strong and Painless
							Strong and Painful
							Weak and Painless

							Weak and Painless
				How STT help us to determine an exercise program for the day?
					Assess severity and irritability? Depending on the pressure we apply. How quickly the pain changes. Help guide exercise prescription on day 1?
					---------------------------------------------------------
					I.e.: High severity with high irritability (High pain with light resistance) (!!E)
						Not strengthening exercises
						Not a lot of exercises
						Not AROM
						Yes target other muscle groups
						Yes work on other planes of motion
						Yes gravity minimized positions
						Yes "modalities" like cold/ice, specially in an acute phase
						Yes exercises they can replicate at home that are not irritable
						Yes a lot of pt. education
							How to move and what positions to avoid
							Find out what positions they are confortable with
					I.e.: No irritability or severity
						We can be more aggressive with the treatment (all the Nos previously mentioned)
			-------------------------------------------------------
			MMT or STT Case
				Example 1
					-Patient has anterior knee pain reported at 7/10 "with activity" and the PT is going to apply resistance to the quadriceps. MMT or STT? 
						-STT because of the pain, pt. cannot achieve full ROM; we won't be able to quantify through MMT. How do we know this? make the patient to do AROM against gravity, if not possible then SST directly. Anyway, since it says here "with activity", AROM against gravity may not aggravate them much, so we can go ahead first with this one.
						-Why/Logic behind STT? Go through the full range, if they cannot then SST.
				Example 2
					-Patient has anterior knee pain reported at 7/10 with activity and the PT is going to apply resistance to the hip abductors. MMT or STT? 
						MMT since hip abductors is not specific of the area of impairment.
				Example 3
					-Patient has intermitent shoulder pain (2-5/10) that comes on mostly with overhead activity and the PT is applying resistance to the ERs. MMT or STT?
						Two case scenarios here:
							-Act with caution here: sometimes you have to be flexible with your testing according to the pt. symptoms (changing on the fly)
								make them move into MMT position:
									if it does not hurt, MMT
									if the pain gets irritable quickly, then STT
										is it painful with that postioning? 
										is it strong or weak?
										How quickly they break? 
										How much resistance can I get before causing pain?
							-----------------------------------
							-MMT to evoke the pain and find out if we can test on other positions since they have the pain only overhead (see [MMTs_RC_Pos3]).
							-STT of all the RC muscles to bias the specific muscle with the injury ¿?
			MMT or STT Case
				-The patient was in a car accident two weeks ago after which she had considerable pain "around" the hip and pelvis – most of which has subsided.
				-A physical therapist applies resistance testing to hip flexion. 
					The patient does not have pain during this procedure, but the outcome is determined to be “painless and weak”.
						-What are TWO possible diagnoses involving the ability (voldrà dir inability? ¿?) for the hip flexors to contract that could result in this response?
							Potential neurological involvement (weakness)
								If it would be weak in the hip flexion what nerve root may be invelved?
									Muscles & Nerve Roots (i.e.: hip - anteriorly, not around) (1 or 2-joint mm.?)
										Illiopsoas - Psoas Major (Flexes hip with iliacus) - VPR L1 L2 L3
										Quadriceps Femoris - Rectus Femoris - L2 L3 L4
								How do we confirm that it is a nerve root invlovement?
									Lower Quarter Screen (LQS) (!!E)Repasar Inervacions Anatomia
										Involves testing myotomes & dermatomes, sensation, reflexes (bilaterally)
											``If it would be a lession of L2 L3 L4, would we spect all the myotomes of the LE to be weak?
												✗1.All the movements like Quad Extension, Ankle MMT, etc. will be strong at that moment
												✗✓2.Only dermatomal testing would be diminished at L2 L3 L4
							Complete tendon rupture (painless)
								We would not see the same dermatomal distribution in this case
								Rupture of the "origin" of the Illiopsoas or Rectus Femoris
						-How might you distinguish between these? (also see above)
								Weakness on ROM & MMT
									Could be the Illiopsoas with no Rectus Femoris involvement
								Test the knee to see if the Rectus Femoris is strong
									-A rupture of the insertion of the Rectus Femoris (not painful at knee but still with some flexion due to other mm.) could affect the hip.
		``Case [MSK_V01_07]
			A patient reports during the history that her shoulder pain is 6/10 at rest and 9/10 when using the arm functionally. She Injured her arm during a lacrosse game 3 days ago.
				1.Why might it be difficult to get an accurate MMT grade of shoulder muscles?
					Muscle contraction is inhibited by pain and spasm
						Applying resistance in this case can cause further damage
						Muscle can appear to be weak as a consequence even if is not weak per se
				2.Other than determining the patient's level of strength, what might be the value in eliciting a contraction of specific shoulder muscles?
					Applying light resistance that does not cause tissue damage might aid with diagnosis and guide treatment
						Diagnosis of injured tissues
							E.G.: Contraction of external rotators vs. internal rotators
						Determining irritability and Guiding treatment
		---------------------------------------
		Alternatives to MMT
			Reasons for employing an alternative to MMTs
				MMTs have poor inter-rater reliability
					Scores above 3/5 are assigned fairly subjectively.
					My 4/5 might be your 4-/5 and someone else’s 4+/5.
				MMT grades are based on a 6-point, ordinal scale (0-1-2-3-4-5)
					-Even though the ratings are numerical in nature, they are not based on standard intervals because the difference in quantities between grades is not exact.
					-This makes it difficult to show improvement using MMTs.
					-This is especially true for small gains in strength.
				How realistic is it to assign an accurate 5/5 grade?
					-A smaller, less muscled physical therapist will be inclined to assign 5/5 grades more often while a larger, stronger individual may only rarely assign them.
			--------------------------------------------
			Isometric testing with hand-held dynamometer
				Involves having the patient push into a force-plate held and stabilized by the physical therapist.
				----------------------------------------------
				Isometric Dynamometry vs. Handheld Dynamometry
					``Isometric Dynamometry (Quantitative)
						Involves the mechanical quantification of muscle force.
					Handheld Dynamometry (Quantitative)
						A more convenient method of quantifying muscle output
						Involves isometric loading a force-plate mounted in a small hand-held device
			Isokinetic testing
				Involves using equipment that can quantify the force produced during a muscle contraction
			One-repetition maximum testing (estimated one rep max testing)
				-Involves having the patient perform as many reps as possible of an exercise using a percentage of his/her one estimated maximum.
				-----------------------------------------------------------
				Qualitative Isolation Test vs. Quantitative Functional Tests
					1) the assessor observes a representative movement
					2) Movement is performed in a predetermined fashion (push up) 
					3) criteria for acceptable performance are established.
				-----------------------------------------------------------
				Qualitative Isolation Test
					Involves observing the patient perform a representative movement and assessing the quality of the performance.
					These may be performed in a standardized or non-standardized fashion
						NON-STANDARDIZED TESTING
							-A physical therapist may, in theory, qualitatively assess the performance of any task relevant to the patient’s daily function Assessment 
							-This form is relatively subjective including documentation of any aberrant or inefficient movements the PT observes
								I.e.:
									Poor form
									Taking longer to perform the task
									Effort that is obviously challenging
						STANDARDIZED TESTING
							Involves performance of a task for which normative criteria or values have been established. 
								I.e.:
									Calf/Heel Raises (Double vs. Single Leg)
										Double Leg (10)
											Able to complete symmetrically?
											Provide cue at first loss of height
											Stop test at second loss of height
										Single Leg (10)
											Perform on uninvolved side first
											Cues as above, also consider use of momentum.
											Record number of reps on each side
										Single Leg (AMRAP)
											As many reps as possible (AMRAP) or some relevant number (e.g. 20 or 30)
											Complete as above
									30-second chair rise and 5x sit to stand
										Can also record time to 5 reps
										5x Sit to Stand Test
											See "Chair Stand-Below Average Scores.png"
									Lateral step down
										-Subject stance
										-Contralateral leg positioned over the floor adjacent to the step and was maintained with the knee in extension
										-Flexion of stance knee
										-Repeated for 5 repetitions
										--------------------------
										See "Lateral Step-Down Test Scoring Criteria.png"
											Arm strategy (+1 point)
											Trunk movement (+1 point)
											Pelvis plane (+1 point)
											Knee position (+1/2 points)
											Maintenance of steady unilateral stance

											Scoring:
												0 or 1 points = good quality of movement,
												2 or 3 points = medium quality
												≥4 points = poor quality of movement.
									Single leg squat
					------------------------------
					Purpose
						-To observe the patient perform a representative movement and assess the quality of the performance. Prior to assessment, the criteria needed for acceptable performance should be established.
						-I.e.: Patient performs step-downs repeatedly while the therapist observes technique and form.
						-Visual
							The patient should perform the task in a pre-determined and consistent fashion. Assessment may be “semi-quantitative”.
				Quantitative Functional Tests (QUANTITATIVE ISOLATION)
					1 or 10 Repetition Maximum or Rep. Max. (1RM or 10RM)
						Quantitative measure of muscle strength
						Methods that determine the maximum resistance that may be lifted 1 or 10 times with good form
						They should only be utilized with relatively healthy and/or advanced individuals who are not at risk of injury
					Reps to Failure/Fatigue
						"to fail” VS. "to fatigue"
							-"to fatigue" is probably more appropriate and is demonstrated when the patient loses form or is unable to continue due to fatigue
								-I.e.: One or multiple Rep. Max. testing of the middle trapezius during an exercise designed to target this muscle, will result in gross compensation with the patient using the rhomboid and posterior deltoid without accurate assessment of middle trap function.
						Especially true for muscles designed more for endurance than strength or power
							I.e.:
								-Shoulder stabilizers need to demonstrate endurance for athletic functions such as pitching, throwing, and swimming
								-Endurance is also more important for most postural muscles
					----------------------------
					Purpose
						Isolate the muscle or movement of interest and use reliable and repeatable tests to quantify performance.
					I.e.: The use of isokinetic equipment that measures degree of force output.
					Visual
						-This image shows isokinetic testing of the quadriceps muscle using a machine that shows the force produced on a computer screen.
						-Isolate the muscle or movement of interest and use reliable and repeatable tests to quantify performance.
						-I.e.: The use of isokinetic equipment that measures degree of force output.
						-Visual
						-This image shows isokinetic testing of the quadriceps muscle using a machine that shows the force produced on a computer screen.
			Functional tests (such as hop tests or step-downs or seated shot put)
				During which the PT assesses performance based on some pre-determined criteria
					-Describe what the patient can do functionally (e.g. able to carry 10 lb. bag up flight of stairs or able to lift 50 lb. above shoulder height)
			Simply describing a specific functional task that the patient can/cannot perform
				I.e.: Able to carry 10 lb. bag up flight of stairs.
				I.e.: Able to lift 50 lb. above shoulder height.

	Sources of ROM Limitation [MSK_L01]

	Tissue Flexibility Assessment - General [KIN&MSK1_TissueFlexAssGeneral]
		Flexibility Testing (AKA Muscle Length Testing or Tissue Length Testing) - Lack of elasticity/flexibility in muscles/tendons
			-For normal movement to occur, tissues and structures surrounding synovial joints must be capable of elongating sufficiently
			-Even if basic ROM is not limited, inflexible structures can alter biomechanics during function which may result in tissue damage
			-To assess the ability of structures to move and elongate as needed during normal movement
				Γ From out towars interior
					Skin
					Fascia
						-Fascia is made up of sheets of connective tissue that is found below the skin. These tissues attach, stabilize, impart strength, maintain vessel patency, separate muscles, and enclose different organs.
					Muscle/Tendon
					Joint Capsule/Ligaments
			---------------------------------------
			Assessment of Tissue Flexibility/Muscle Length
				What, when, why, how?
					What are we assessing when assessing tissue flexibility and/or muscle length?
						The ability for muscles/tendons to-lengthen during movement
					When would we assess these tissue flexibility and/or muscle length?
						Whenever movement at the joint being assessed is aberrant, limited, or painful
					Why are we assessing tissue flexibility and/or muscle length?
						Is a possible source of limitation related to mm length?
					How do we assess tissue flexibility and/or muscle length?
						Compare bilaterally
						Limitation on one side, both sides, or neither?
			---------------------------------------
			Elasticity/Flexibility Tests
				See PROC_HipFlex for Hip
			Stretching Techniques [Kin_StretchTechGeneral]
				-Manual Stretching (partner stretch)
					Fundamentals & Procedures:
						Therapist moves the segment being stretched slightly beyond tissue resistance
						Solicit feedback during manual stretching about the intensity of the stretch
						REHAB_Early Indicated in early stages of rehab
							Control stretching intensities to make sure patient does not over or under-apply stretch
						Instantly monitor patient response
					When:
						Patient unable to reach actively since physically limited and needs help
					Pros & Cons
						PROS
							When patient has difficulties to relax
							When there is spasticity of the muscle
						CONS
				-Self-Stretching [KIN_SelfStretch]
					("Self-Stretching Techniques.png")
					Fundamentals & Procedures:
						Performed independently by the patient
						Variations of self-stretching ¿?
						Passive movement of the body segment using the upper extremities
							Fixation of distal attachment & use of body weight for leverage
							Active movement of antagonist to muscle being stretched
							For effective self-stretching, instruction of proper alignment & stabilization is essential
					Pros & Cons
						PROS
							Practicallity: patient can do it at home (not depending o the clinic) and advance rapidly on the therapy
						CONS
							N/A
				-Mechanical Stretching
					Use of equipment to apply stretching force
						Cuff Weight
							Example 1: Use of a cuff weight ↓ to stretch the knee into extension
								-In this approach, the therapist would strap a cuff weight onto the patient’s ankle, place the patient in Prone and hang the knee off of the supportive surface using gravity to pull the knee into extension
						Dynamic splint
							Example 2: Use of a Dynamic splint ↓
								-Dynamic splints allow a joint to be placed in a position of stretch at a very low intensity over a very long duration
								-Therefore associated with longer duration
								-These are an ideal way to induce creep in connective tissues
				-Assisted stretching
				-Neuromuscular Inhibition Stretching techniques (when difficulty getting the patient to relax) (variation of ``Proprioceptive Neuromuscular Facilitation PNF) [Kin_PNF]
					-A common issue when a physical therapist attempts to perform manual stretching or when a patient performs self-stretching is difficulty getting the patient to relax.
					-These techniques, most of which represent some variation of Proprioceptive Neuromuscular Facilitation (PNF), utilize the body’s existing neuromuscular reflex reactions to promote relaxation of muscles. 
					------------------------------------------------------------------------------
					.Hold-Relax / Contract-Relax
						-Γ PT stretches, pt. holds/iso tight/agonist mm then relaxes, then PT stretches again; all same muscle
						-('01 PT2229_M7_HoldRelax-ContractRelax.pdf')
						.................................................................................
						The basis for many manual therapy approaches know as Muscle Energy Techniques
						Possible mechanisms of action:
							-Muscles tend to reflexively relax following a voluntary contraction
							-Conscious awareness of contraction followed by relaxation helps the patient’s muscles become more aware of the presence of muscle guarding.
							-Physiological source: Activation/Deactivation of the Golgi Tendon Organ
					.Reciprocal Inhibition / Agonist Contraction 
						-Γ PT stretches, pt. holds/iso antagonist mm then relaxes, then PT stretch again / could be pt. isometric contraction & PT stretches at the same time¿?; different muscles
						.................................................................................
						Mechanism: 
							Contraction of one muscle group usually causes reflex relaxation of the opposite muscle group
							Occurs to prevent counterproductive muscle contraction during activity
							Common indications: 
								Pain in the muscle being stretched
								Significant muscle guarding
						-('02 PT2229_M7_ReciprocalInhibition.pdf')
					.Hold-Relax with Agonist Contraction
						-Combines the two previously mentioned approaches
						-Γ PT stretches, pt. holds/iso tight/agonist mm, then pt. holds/iso antagonist mm, then relaxes, then PT stretches again / could be pt. isometric contraction & PT stretches at the same time¿?; different muscles
						-('03 PT2229_M7_HoldRelax+AgonistContraction.pdf')
					------------------------------------------------------------------------------

			-------------------------------------------
			Assessing Flexibility of One-Joint vs. Two-Joint Muscles
				Most flexibility testing, assesses the length of muscle/tendon and fascia
				``Functional Excursion of the Muscle
					-For muscles specifically, these tests assess the distance the muscle is capable of shortening after it has been maximally lengthened
					-----------------------------------
					In one-joint muscles
						Determined by movement of the joint it crosses
					In two-joint muscles
						Depends on configuration of both joints the muscle crosses
					-----------------------------------
					I.e.:
						ILIOPSOAS
							One-joint muscle
							Only crosses the hip
							Decreased flexibility would only be shown by restriction of movement at the hip
						HAMSTRINGS
							Two-joint muscle
							Cross the hip and knee
							Decreased flexibility shown by restriction of combinations of movement at the hip and knee
			-------------------------------------------
			Measurement of Muscle Length 
				-Baseline and follow up measures of muscle and other tissue length can be taken by measuring the angle of the limb when the muscle is placed on sufficient stretch
				-Lack of flexibility may occur due to adaptive shortening of soft tissue structures → Severe adaptive shortening may result in a contracture
					Contracture
						Adaptive shortening of muscle or other soft tissues resulting in significantly limited ROM
						It is often difficult to reverse and typically occur after extended immobilization
						Described by identifying the position in which the motion is fixed
							I.e.: An elbow flexion contracture results in limited elbow extension motion
			-------------------------------------------
			Various environmental factors affect tissue flexibility
			Upon hearing any of these in the patient history, the PT should consider performing flexibility testing
				Systemic, joint, neuromuscular or muscular diseases
				Surgical or traumatic events
				Inactivity or immobilization

	Joint Mobility Assessment/testing [MSK_C01_06] [MSK1_L4] [MSK1_JointMobAss]
		-Before doing Joint Mobilization AKA Manipulation "Tretament" (See also SUB_JointMobTech & REF_MaitlandGrades), you do "Assessment" if you suspect this could be a problem
			Make sure you use properly these two terms
		-When a physical therapist uses manual force in an attempt to produce arthrokinematics motions (not through normal extremity motion, for example) such as sliding or gliding between joint surfaces
		---------------------------------------
		Assessment of Joint Mobility [KIN] [MSK1_L01]
			Before doing a joint mobilization assessment¿? treatment, you should test joint mobility
				I.e.: Talus - posterior-anterior force (see video [01:07:35sec])
			-----------------------------------------
			What are we assessing when we perform joint mobility assessment?
				The ability for joint surfaces to move relative to one another
					Glide, slide, roll, etc.
					Basically, assessing if arthrokinematics at the joint are normal
			When would we perform joint mobility assessment?
				Whenever movement at the joint being assessed is nor moving properly, is:/Performed to assess if mobility between joint surfaces is:
					Aberrant
					Limited ROM (hypomobility)
					Exessive ROM (hypermobility)
					Painful
					Normal → No need to assess
			Why are we assessing joint mobility?
				Determine if the source of the limitation is related to arthrokinematics
				Also, to guide treatment
			How do we assess joint mobility?
				Apply manual force to joints in direction that arthrokinematics occurs
					Begin on UN-involved side
					Compare to involved side
			---------------------------------
			Regional Interdependence [MSK1_L01]
				-The concept that seemingly unrelated impairments in a remote anatomical region may contribute to, or be associated with, the patient's primary complaint (weakest link)
				-Treat locally, fix globally
				-----------------------------
				I.e.:
					``What problems might result in the spine if:
						A patient has hypomobility at C5-C6 Γ(al estar més baix, es pot pèrmitir hypomobility)
							What issues might develop at the segments above and below this area?
								Excessive movement to compensate (hypermobility)
						A patient has hypermobility at C4-C5 Γ(està massa alt, no hia prou distancia; no es pot permitir hypermobility)
							What issues might develop at the segments above and below this area?
								Not viceversa but a variety of symptoms
									Muscle spasm
									Muscle guarding
									Pain
									Limitation
									Altered movement patter
									Etc.
				-----------------------------
				Knowledge Test
					.A patient has left hip pain. Decreased mobility in which of these areas could contribute to this?
						Lumbar spine
						Right hip
						Right knee
						Left knee
						All of these

						All of these
					.A patient has hypermobility of the right knee. This places increased demand (& overuse) on muscles in what other areas?
						Hip, quads, hamstrings, ankle, etc.
					.A therapist has been treating a patient for 4 weeks after a shoulder injury. The patient has regained about 80% of the range of motion but is still lacking towards end ranges of flexion and ABDuction, and bilateral rotation.
						Other than the glenohumeral joint, which of the following areas should the therapist assess/and if appropriate treat?
							Upper and mid-cervical spine
							Thoracic spine (general rule: the closer we get to the area the better; around T4)
							Lumbar spine
							Elbow
							All of these

							Thoracic spine (general rule: the closer we get to the area the better; around T4); ΓSTJ

	Case 1 - 61 y.o. male 4 weeks s/p rotator cuff repair
		The patient is presently permitted to perform PROM exercises but must stay below 90° of abduction because the shoulder capsule, which was resected during surgery, is still healing. He has some pain at rest and notable causing muscle guarding during PROM.
		--------------------------------------------
		For this type of exercises find like the RED, ORANGE, and GREEN flags (no related to MSK1_Flags); (Γ)let's called them "poles"
			Red pole → /rp./
			Orange Pole → /op./
			Green Pole → /gp./
			-------------------------------------------(Γ)
			a.61 y.o. /op./
			b.TIMELINE: 4 weeks s/p rotator cuff repair
			c.permitted to perform PROM exercises → /gp./
				c.1.PRECAUTION: but must stay below 90° of abduction /rp./
					because the shoulder capsule still healing
				c.2.muscle guarding during PROM /rp./
			d.pain at rest /rp./
			--------------------------------------------
			What is the rationale of performing mobilization in first place
				a, b →
					Prevent/Reduce atrophy
						Due to his age and more if he was inactive before the surgery
						Helps to reduce atrophy after inmobilization due to surgery
				d →
					Reduce pain and irritability
						Stretch receptors, etc.
				c.2 →
					Reduce muscle guarding
						-After a surgery, muscles tend to tens up and go in spasm, so doing mobs favors relaxation
				Other reasons →
					Grades I and II will help with the healing process due to fluids movement
						More blood irrigation
							More nutrients reach the site
			Which grades(s) of joint mobilization would be appropriate for this patient?
				Grade I, REF_MaitlandGrades
					This is the force we apply
					We cannot go over 90° so:
							Maitland's Grades
								Grade I - Without reaching 90°
								(Γ)Grade II - Would not be adequate already since "within available range" could mean more than 90°
			What would be the rationale for performing this grade of mobilization?
				-``The RC is inside of the joint capsule, so to get there we have to go through the joint capsule. So, the joint capsule has to heal too in addition of the tendon they are repairing
			What special considerations might the PT take during/after performing joint mobilizations?
				During:
					c.1 → PRECAUTION: but must stay below 90° of abduction
					The number of reps: it would be accomplished asking the patient
						irritability - Do 30 to 60 seconds and ask for feedback: -How does it feel?-
				After:
					Ask for
						.Severity
							The next visit of after a while form the therapy
								If the pt. reports soreness for 1 or 2 hours after the therapy
									Follow up questions:
										Quantify the pain (numeric pain scale 1 to 10 NPRS)
											It would be ok → if it was like 3
											It would be NOT ok
												if it would be like 5 and above
												Pain during hours
												Not a clear answer here
												-The point of doing joint mobilization is for pain control. So, if the pain get wors, stop it. The purpose of the joint mob is filling at least nothing but not worse. Do something else for pain control or wait a little bit.
													-However, with frozen shoulder, the joint mobilization could be inconfortable (8/10 is acceptable), and this is ok as long as we do not put the patient in missery for days.
													-Remember the contraindication for joint mobilization - Traction
						.Irritability
							Already seen in "During"
	``Case 2 - 25 y.o. F sprinter who ruptured Achilles Tendon 4 weeks ago
		-Surgery performed to repair ruptured tendon appears to be healing well, but is not yet fully healed. Patient is immobilized in a removable splint in 0° of dorsiflexion. Comes to PT with a script that includes mobilization of the talo-crural joint. The patient is limited to performing only very limited PROM and light isometrics at the ankle.

		a.ruptured Achilles Tendon /rp./
		b.4 weeks ago /op./
			b.1.Surgery performed to repair ruptured tendon appears to be healing well /gp./
			b.2.but is not yet fully healed /rp./

			at the ankle
				e.1.limited to performing only very limited PROM /rp./
				e.2.light isometrics at the ankle /rp./

			1.Which bony segment would be mobilized in this type of intervention?
				The Talus ("Talo-Crural Joint.png")
					-Here is where PL & DF mainly accurs ("Actions Bones Ankle.png") 
			2.On/within which structure would it be mobilized?
				On the Mortis
			3.Based on the concave/convex rule, in which direction would mobilization be most beneficial?
				PL b/c we do not want to put stress on the tendon with DF (DF is also more difficult after an ankle spring)
					So PL:
						(Γ)Open-Packed position: 10° plantar flexion, midway between maximum inversion and eversion
						(Γ)Plantarflexion: 30°-50°
						(Γ)Open Chain: Talus on Tibia + Fibula
						Convex on Concave
							Posterior roll, Anterior glide
								✗posterior roll = anterior to posterior
									(Γ)Means moving the talus from anterior to posterior glide ("treatment plane.png")
			4.Considering that the joint capsule was not injured, what would be the rationale for performing joint mobilizations with this individual?
				To maintain ROM on the sorrounding structures
					-I.e.: if we do not move the joint capsule or stretching is not detected for a period, the long collagen fibers are going to being reabsorbed and substituted by shorter ones (move it or loose it). They also will be stick to each other b/c they are not being moved respect to each other. Make the capsule more pliable in resume 
			``5.What grades would be appropriate for this purpose?
				-Dorsiflexion: 15°-20° ("Kin Normal Ranges.txt")
				-Start with (See Maitland's Grades: 'REF_MaitlandGrades', Γ'Maitland Mobilization Grades.mp4', 'Maitland Mobilization Grading - A Visual Aid.mp4') Grade II Γ(GM.II) to warm up, continue with III Γ(GM.III), end on IV
					-The Achiles tendon is outside the capsule (the capsule is not injured in comparison with the case before) so we do not need to be that conservative; (Γ) on plantarflexion¿?
	``Case 3 - Grade 4 Joint Mobilizations [01:56:00sec "MSK04.mp4"]
		Describe patient cases (for Shoulder, Elbow, and Hip) whom Grade 4 mobilizations would be appropriate. In writing this case, list:
			1.The patient’s age and pathology

		-----------------------------------------
		El suc de l'exercisi es dir:
			In open pack position
				Concave-Convex Rule
				Roll & Glide
				In which position we put the patient for this
				Would you apply Grade 4 on a patient with precauions?
					Compare the precautions with the normal grades (°)
						-I.e.: if the precuation is 90° and the patient has 80°, we would be able to apply Grade 4 until reached the 90°.
					In what direction we would mobilize under this precautions?
						We have to know first about the surgery
							Was it Anterior or Posterior approach?
								I.e.: let's say it is a posterior approach (they went in though the posterior capsule)
									What direction you want to be more careful with?
										Arthrokinematics/Accesory motion: Posterior to Anterior (glide) (No serà al revés? ¿?)
										Osteokinematics/Physiological motion: Flexion
					How would you modify your tratment if they not tolerate a certain Grade (i.e.: Grade 5)?
						Using "Modalities" such as heat or ice and lower the grade back to 4
		The elbow works differently when it comes to jont mobilization

	Special Tests - Initial Considerations
		Do not overlay on them
		They are not the main course but the desert; they should never be the primary focus of your exam
			-After you perform the more general tests (ROM, Stregth, Joint Mobility) w/t a more general value since provide infor about pt. deficits on which to focues the treatment. Then, after that, you can focus on identifying specific conditions or diagnoses
		If the test is a provocative (does the test causes pain or not?) one, do it only unilateraly. Or when somebody presetns with pain in one side only. However, only when is normal strength , ROM, joint mobility, and felxibility you compared side to side. [MSK1_L05]
		Most special tests (Disatvantages):
			-Implicate a specific structure or condition
				We can get "tunnel vision" and get lost wrongly focusing in one specific structure
			-Are provocative in nature
				They provoke the patient's symptoms potentially causing additional pain and inflammation
			-(many) Have questionable validity - Maybe they work maybe they don't
				Some are god, some are bad. Are you going to remember the validity of each test?
			-Some are to rule in other out
			-Sometimes, patient can´t even get in that position
			-``Used more as a diagnostic tool instead of looking for the imparements.
			-When listened the words of the "diagnosis", patient can start to spiral, thinking on the worst. Listening imparements is very different.
				-Having those conversations can affect how the patient responds to the pain, injury, treatment, etc. [MSK02.mp4 00:19:00sec] This way, you can change the locus of control.
					I.e.:
						Diagnostic (patient perception of no locus of control):
							You have a torned MCL → -Am I gonna play again?!-
						Imparement (Patient in control):
							You have a tear on the MCL with Lack of ROM & Quad weakness
			-It does not help to provide treatment; we need to find out the imparements
		------------------------------------------------------
		What information do they provide? [MSK_V01_08]
			-Most special tests provide information about the "specific condition" or "diagnosis" (meaning named pathology or the tissue injury) causing the patient's problem
				I.e.: a rotator cuff tear, a rupture of the ACL, or thoracic outlet syndrome
					-While these are often relevant, the diagnosis alone does not usually provide adequate information to design a treatment plan (no és prou específic per a un PT)
		What do we do with this information?
			Diagnoses provide some clues as to the treatments that are commonly done
			Knowing which treatments are contraindicated or that are likely to make the patients worse
				-I.e.: If you can identify that a patient has a meniscus tear, then this would make a physical therapist more cautious about prescribing activities that involve cutting and twisting
		Do we do them bilaterally? [MSK1_L06]
			Not necessarily, depends on which one and the purpose of it (provoking symptoms?)
				Pain is the outcome of those and that in one reason
					-If a patient comes and tell us that he has pain in one hip, it does not make any sense to test the other side and provoke pain there
				Some tests will provoke pain wheter it is a pathology or not (Scour Test)
					-Imagine with a pt. that does not have a pathology there and we do Scour Test. We will provoke pain and we will confuse the pt. if we ask if that is the pain they are coming for
						-But if we test the other side and then the pain is the same, it does not mean that they have a pathology on both sides
							-Γ Maybe if you reach a point in which you have to use Scour Test and then you provoke the pain but you are not sure if it is because of the agressivity of the test per se or because they have really pain, then go and do in the uninvolved side to compare

	Symptom Modification Procedures Γ(SMP) (an article from 2018) [MSK_V01_08] (See [MSK1_MobilProc]::Hip joint)
		-The application of an outside force which changes a movement pattern or muscle activation pattern in an attempt to reduce the severity of a patient's symptoms with a particular activity
			-Instead of tests that provoke symptoms, PT should focus on using Symptom Modification Procedures
			-The typical procedure is to:
				First of all, identify movements that do provoke symptoms
				Then, introduce some change to that movement
				Finally, find out: does the change actually reduce the patient's symptoms?
				--------------------------------------------------------------------------
				Advantages of this typical procedure
					Improve patient buy-in - It is very empowering at the eyes of the patient if we achieve that
					Simplifies objective exam
					Guides treatment !!
						They can provide direction for treatment
							What to do next
							How do I treat
							What needs to be done
						They are more meaninfull than the Special Tests for the pt. and for the PT since provide a direction
							``We'll know if to go to Screening or Evaluation

				Examples [MSK02.mp4 - 00:22:00sec]
					Manual cervical traction - To see if it decreases the pain
					Scapular assistance test
						I.e:
							It guides the treatment knowing:
								It maybe stregngth
									So you will have to teach them to use the strengh they have
					Eccentric step test [MSK02.mp4 - 00:23:42sec]
						Without cues to contract glut max
						With cues to contract glut max
					Patellar taping
					Mobilization with movement
						-A procedure that we can perform to identify if a certain mobilization increases the patientls movement and so it crosses over from special test into treatment
					``Isometric loading [MSK02.mp4 - 00:40:33]
						Can be done to decrease patient's pain
					Abdominal bracing or neutral spine positioning
						-Putting the pt. in a position or recruiting muscles that help to support the spine so they have less back pain during function.
					Give them an assisted device
						A cane can help to decrease pain
					--------------------------------------
					All followed by movement or activity to determine if symptoms are lessened
		-Provides some perspective about how to best use special tests and which types are of most value
	Question 5
		``Which of the following is an example of a symptom modification procedure?
		    Palpation for pain and tenderness
		    Manual muscle testing to quantify strength
		    Selective tissue tension testing to determine if symptoms are provoked during contraction
		    Mobilization of a joint followed by reassessment of range of motion

		    ✓Mobilization of a joint followed by reassessment of range of motion
	Question 6
		Which of the following is NOT a benefit of performing Symptom Modification Procedures?
		    Increasing accuracy of diagnosis of the injured structure
		    Directing the physical therapist towards potentially beneficial treatments
		    Improving buy-in from the patient
		    Simplification of the objective examination

		    ✓Increasing accuracy of diagnosis of the injured structure

	////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
	Palpation [KIN_PalpGeneral]
		(See [MSK1_PalpHip])
	////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

	Typically one of the final procedures performed during a physical exam
	----------------------------------
	Reasons
		-If palpation is being performed to identify the injured structure(s), it often will provoke the patient’s pain or other symptoms
		-While sometimes necessary, provocation of symptoms should be minimized because:
			-Provoking symptoms too early can make other testing less effective
				If irritable enough, will result in “everything hurting”
			-Causing significant pain early in the exam may decrease rapport and/or faith the patient has in the physical therapist
		-Using palpation to identify the injured structures too early may result in “horse blinders”
			-In other words, focusing on the specific tissue early on may cause the PT to place less focus on tests that implicate other structures.
	Why palpate?
		-As mentioned above, to identify the injured structure. However, there are other equally important reasons to perform palpation.
			REASON 1
				To determine the severity and irritability of involved tissues.
					Are symptoms elicited even by a light palpation?
					Or is significant pressure required? 
			REASON 2
				To determine the presence of impairments.
					Spasm: Additional tension in muscle tissue
					Signs of inflammation
						Swelling
						Warmth
	Best Practices for Palpation
		Use Light Pressure
			Use light pressure, at least initially, then slowly increase pressure
		Don't Let Your Eyes Fool You
			When palpating, focus more on what you feel, not what you see
			Some PTs will close their eyes when palpating
		Use Your Anatomy
			Attempt to "see through the skin" using your fingers
			Know what is too deep to palpate and what is not
		Palpate Directly on Skin
			And most importantly, unless in a sensitive area, palpate directly on skin whenever possible
	Question 4
		Palpation is not performed early in the physical therapy exam because of the potential for it to:
		    Is unrelated to the other components of the physical exam
		    Require frequent repositioning of the patient
		    Bias the physical therapist towards suspecting injury to a specific structure
		    Relieve pain prior to initiating any specific type of treatment

		    ✓Bias the physical therapist towards suspecting injury to a specific structure

	FUNDAMENTAL CONCEPTS FOR DESIGNING AND PRESCRIBING EXERCISE PROGRAM
