	-------------------------------------------------------------------
	Definitions
	Exercise Prescription for ROM (Indications for PROM Exercises)
	Exercise Prescription for Stretching
	For ROM & Stretching

Definitions
	ROM exercises
		Exercises in which a body segment is moved through its full or partial range against no form of resistance other than gravity
		ROM exercises are not equivalents to stretching exercises!
			While patients with ROM deficits may perform either or both, these types of exercise are performed for different purposes
				ROM vs. Stretching Exercises
					ROM
						Used to maintain existing joint and soft tissue mobility
						Often implemented to minimize the development of contractures
						Occurs in the unrestricted portion of joint motion
						Can be passive, active or active-assisted
					Stretching Exercises
						-Therapeutic maneuver designed to lengthen soft tissue structures that have been deemed “short” in order to increase ROM
						-Occur at the end-range of motion
						-Include passive and active stretching
	AROM exercises
		Patient performs the motion using his/her own muscles without any form of assist
	AAROM exercises (Active-Assisted ROM)
		Patient performs active movement that is assisted by an outside force
	PROM Exercises
		-Movement of the patient’s body segment is produced solely by an external force, such as gravity, a machine, another individual, or another part of patient’s body

Exercise Prescription for ROM (Indications for PROM Exercises)
	Prescribe PROM to
		Most common indication
			Maintain joint & soft tissue mobility and prevent the formation of contractures when active movement is not possible.
			Stress tissue conservatively into end ranges when active movement is not tolerated 
			Maintain gains made between bouts of exercise that stretches shortened tissue
		May also be used to
			Assist with the healing process
			Assist with nutrition of articular cartilage 
			Assist in circulation & vascular dynamics
			Minimize stiffness of muscles
			Maintain awareness of motion when it is lacking
			Decrease pain
			Maintain motion when AROM and AAROM are contraindicated
		-----------------------------------------------------------
		Limitations of PROM Exercises
			Passive ROM Exercises will NOT
				Maintain contractility of muscle
				Prevent muscle atrophy
				Increase strength or endurance
				Assist circulation to the extent produced by voluntary muscle contraction
				Reduce significant contractures
			``Should PROM exercises be prescribed as a primary method to increase PROM when PROM is limited?

	Indications for AROM Exercises
		-A patient has the ability to contract muscles actively and move a segment, BUT displays significant weakness of muscles in that segment to the extent that makes even light resistance exercise too difficult.
		-The benefits of AROM are the same as those for PROM exercises with the added benefits that arise from contraction of muscle (these will be discussed in another module)
		--------------------------------------------------------
		When the goal is to:
			Maintain contractility of muscle when it is being minimally used
			Provide sensory feedback to and from muscles being minimally used
			Provide stimulus to maintain bone that is receiving minimal stress
			Develop coordination & motor skills for low level functional activities
			Increase circulation & prevent thrombus formation (blood clots)
			Improve cardiopulmonary function for VERY low level patients
			Maintain mobility above and below an immobilized region
		---------------------------------------------------------
		AROM exercises will NOT:
			-Increase strength in muscles of at least average strength  
			-Develop coordination or skill except in the plane utilized during exercise. For example, after hip surgery, a patient performs hip AROM flex and extension. This will not carry over into additional movement coordination during AROM abduction
			-Be appropriate or safe when active contraction of muscle is contraindicated
		---------------------------------------------------------
		Cases
			-``A patient has been casted at the elbow for eight weeks following a fracture of the right olecranon process. The physical therapist prescribes AROM exercises for the right shoulder and wrist. Why? To preserve mobility

	Indications & Limitations for AAROM Exercises
		A PT should prescribe Active-Assisted ROM exercises when
			-Movement in the unrestricted portion of motion that is produced by a combination of voluntary muscle contraction and externally applied force
			-External force may be applied manually or mechanically
			-Used when prime mover is not capable of completing motion
			-AAROM only if AROM is not through full ROM
			-All indications and limitations described for AROM exercises also apply to AAROM exercises

	General aspects of prescribing ROM exercises
		The implementation of these will vary based on
			the type of exercise being prescribed
				(ací falta lo de resistance i endurance ¿?)
			the target population
				Healthy Population
					less conservative
					-----------------
					Individuals without pathology or significant risk factors
					Young and active individuals who are familiar with exercise
					Athletes
					Some individuals of geriatric age who are generally fit
				Special populations
					Greater care should be taken when first prescribing exercise for this target population
					More conservative
					-----------------
					Highly Inactive
					Obese
					Over 65 y.o and not presently exercising
					Of pediatric ages
					Unacquainted with exercise
					At high risk for developing serious health issues or have existing health conditions
		--------------------------------------------
		``Elements of Resistance Exercise Programs (See "PROC_ResisTrain - Resistance Training - Elements of Resistance Exercise Programs")
			.Intensity
				• How vigorous is the activity being performed?
				• Amount of effort? Amount of load that is moved during the exercise
				-Load placed on tissues?
				-Examples:
				--Lifting of weight
				--Use of red theraband asresistance
				--Perceived difficulty of 7/10
			.Volume
				• What is the total number of exercise tasks performed? Sets of repetitions performed
				• Number of times per week or even day resistance exercise sessions are performed
					¿?No es açò "Frequency"? Sí, deu ser un error!!
				-Examples:
				--Total number of reps and sets and/or different forms of the exercise
				--3 sets of 12 reps performed of
				--4 different strengthening exercises
			.Duration
				• How long is the activity performed?
				-Examples:
				--Holding a plank for 30 seconds
				--Performing single leg stancefor 20 seconds
				--Walking for 30 minutes
			Frequency
				• How many times is the activity performed over a given time period?
				-Examples:
				--Twice per day
				--Three times per week
				--Once an hour
			---------------------------------------------------
			I.e.: What is what?
				One hour (Volume)
				6 Exercises=(Frequency)
				On his 1st set, he does 10 reps of 250
				His next 2 sets of reps include:
					8 reps of 275 and 290 pressed 4 times.
				Altogether he has performed 3 sets of 10, 8, and 4 reps collectively.
					x3set = 10rep (250), 8rep (275), 4rep (290)=(Intensity)
						x3set = 10rep, 8rep, 4rep = (Duration) 
		---------------------------------------------------------------
		Progression/Modification of Exercises (increase/decrease difficulty)
			-It is often necessary to slightly modify prescribed exercises for the purpose of making them less difficult, more practical, or more challenging
			-Swapping one exercise for an entirely different one is typically not as effective as modifying the initial exercise because it is another attempt to guess how the patient will respond
				Slightly modifying the exercise based on a known response provides a more calculated starting point
			-Ideally, an activity should be challenging for the patient but with potential for success
			-------------------------------------
			Modifying Patient Exercise or Activity
				The dificulty of an exercise should be reduced when:
					Most attempts to perform the exercise or movement are unsuccessful or result in low quality movements
					The patient experiences a significant increase in pain or other symptoms
				The difficulty of an exercise should be increased (AKA: progressed) when:
					The activity appears to be easy, effortless and/or minimally challenging
					The patient has experienced a plateau in progress towards goals
			-------------------------------------
			Strategies for Effectively Modifying the Difficulty of Exercise
				1. Alter the range through which the patient moves during the activity
				2. Alter the patient's base of support (BOS) during the exercise
				3. Change the speed at which the patient performs the exercise
				4. Reduce feedback provided during the exercise
				5. Increase or decrease the lever arm applied during the exercise
				6. Alter the complexity of movement patterns
				7. Progress from one form of exercise to a similar but different form
				8. Increase dosage
				-----------------------------------
				Manipulation of Intensity (REPE amb lo de dalt? ¿?)
					Is achieved by increasing the amount of the load 
					E.G.:  Heavier weights, thicker elastic resistance, more manual force used during MRE, etc. 
					More focus would be placed here when training for "strength"
				Manipulation of Frequency
					-Would be training more sessions per week, day or month
					-This could be a focus for all forms of training, but especially those requiring motor learning, such as "balance or coordination"
				Manipulation of Volume
					Is performing more reps or sets during a training session
					More focus would be placed here when training for "endurance"
				Manipulation of Duration
					Is achieved by lengthening the amount of time spent training within each training session 
					Again, probably more of an "endurance" consideration
				--------------------------------------------------------
					-However – and this next point is VERY important, a much more effective and efficient way to progress resistance exercises in a physical therapy setting is to slightly modify the manner in which the patient performs the exercise or activity. 
					-Modification of nearly any resistance exercise can be achieved by modifying one of the following variables: 
						Base of Support
						Length of the lever arm
						Range through which the movement is performed
						Plane of movement
						Speed of movement
	---------------------------------------------------------------------------------
	Precautions & Contraindications for Performing ROM Exercises
		-A precaution is when
			performing a certain intervention may not be productive or safe, depending upon that patient’s specific situation. 
		-A contraindication is when performing a certain intervention is never productive or safe. In this instance, these are combined into one list but often they are separated into two lists
		----------------------------------
		ROM exercises are contraindicated when:
			Motion is disruptive to healing
				-Controlled motion is often beneficial to healing and prevents the adverse effects of immobilization, but excess motion can be counterproductive. The goal is to find the “Goldilocks Zone” providing the amount of motion that is “just right”
			If the patient is experiencing a severe acute phase of inflammation
			If the patient is experiencing severe pain at rest 
			There are signs of Deep Vein Thrombosis (blood clot)
			The patient has an unstable cardiovascular condition
	----------------------------------------------------------------------------------
	Appropriate Parameters for Prescribing Range of Motion Exercises
		PROM
			Frequency of multiple times per day
			Volume that produces minimal fatigue, minimal pain, and avoid signs of inflammation
		AROM (See "Exercise Prescription ROM MMT or Stretching.txt - Indications for the various forms of ROM exercises: Say if PROM, AROM, or AAROM")
			The parameters for AAROM and AROM exercises are based largely on initial conditioning level and desired goals
			Frequency should be once to multiple times per day based on patient tolerance  
			Volume is based on training goals
				-If the patient lacks endurance, prescribe higher number of reps/sets, first performing AROM and utilizing AAROM to complete the volume when fatigue begins to set in. (Com cuan fem peses i algú ens ajuda. Aguant = més series amb el pes normal)
				-If strength is the issue, prescribe lower numbers of reps with more total sets (més series de menos repeticions; molt de pes just per a fer 3 pepeticions¿?). For low level strength, only AROM would be implemented with the patient resting when fatigued before beginning another set

Exercise Prescription for Stretching ¿?Necesite investigar més este tema
	What does the evidence say about Duration and Frequency of stretch?
		Hamstrings                                             30-60 sec daily                         5 days/week
			                                                   2 min hold, 3x/session                  2 sessions/week
			                                                   30-60 sec 1x/day VS 30-60 sec 3x/day
		Calf Stretch (65-89 years old)                         10 rep, 15 sec
		Hamstrings (around 56 years old)	                   60° > than 15° or 30°                   5 times x week
		Use of weight enough to hold position may help in elderly subjects
	---------------------------------------------------------------
	Total End-Range Time (TERT) (No tinc clar estes identacions, pot ser que no existixquen com estan ací ¿?)
		The longer the tissue is at end-range, the better the chance for a permanent change in length
			Allows for plasitc changes and creep in connective tissue
			Driving principle behind: low-load, long duration stretching
			One Source (McClure PW. et al. Phys TIW. 1994):
				For contractures, start at 60 minutes/day
			Defer to patient tolerance and signs of lasting inflammation

		The amount of time at end range is key
			Do NOT need to be all at once
			May break it up into several unas during the day
				I.e.: If you want 60 minutes of TERT in a day you could do the following
					5 stretches held for 12 minutes each
					3 stretches for 20 minutes each
	---------------------------------------------------------------
	What are the reasons for performing stretching?
		Improve or conserve mobility (ROM) after immobilization, for example
	When should stretching be performed by the physical therapist?
		-When the patient is not able to stretch to normal ranges (AROM) due to weakness or when in risk to reinjure himself after surgery. By the patient? When the patient is not able to strech to normal ranges (AROM) but the strenght is normal
	What is an effective stretch duration?
		From 30 to 60 seconds
	What measures can be taken to help a patient relax during stretching?
		All the manuevers in this chapter like making the patient contract, then relax, then PT stretches the muscle

	How do we define stretching?
		-Any therapeutic maneuver designed to increase mobility of soft tissues and subsequently improve ROM by lengthening structures that have become adaptively shortened
		-Therapeutic stretching may also be used to prevent such occurrences

	When we apply stretch, what is it that we are stretching?
		Muscle stretching
			When stretching shortened muscles, the stretch is applied opposite to the functions of a specific muscle to stretch that muscle
				-e.g. Stretching of calf muscles: The plantar flexors (gastroc and soleus) perform plantar flexion. To stretch these, the ankle would be placed in sustained dorsiflexion
		Joint stretching
			-Stretching that targets a non-muscular structure (e.g. ligament or joint capsule) or stretching the joint in the same direction of movement that is restricted.
	--------------------------------------------------------
	Indications for Stretching
		Limited ROM secondary to loss of extensibility in tissues (contractures, adhesions and/or scar tissue formation)
		To prevent loss of extensibility when potential for shortening is high
		``Opposite significantly weak muscles
		Part of total fitness program to prevent injury and maximize performance
		After vigorous exercise
		Before vigorous exercise?
	Contraindications for Stretching
		ROM limited by bony block
		Stretching beyond ranges considered "within normal limits" or when there is existing hypermobility
		Recent Fracture
		Acute inflammation, infection, or Hematoma
		Sharp pain with resistance
		Contractures that provide stability
		Vigorous stretching after extensive immobilization
		Vigorous stretching of weak muscles
	See "R Elbow Fx 55F.txt - 55 y.o. female who sustained R elbow fx"
	-------------------------------------------------------------------
	PROC_Stretch
		Achieving an effective stretch involves placing the patient in the best position to elongate the target tissues
			Achieving full stretch involves position all the involved segments (2-Joint-Muscles)
				Rectus femoris:
					Knee in flexion
					Hip in extension
					Pelvis towards posterior tilt
				Hamstrings:
					Knee in extension
					Hip in flexion
					Pelvis towards anterior tilt
		An effective method of determining what this position is for a given muscle would be to consider the three As of stretching
			Anatomy
			Attachments
			Actions
		----------------------------------------- [MSK1_StretchEx]
		Methods of Stretching - Stretching exercises, performed by
			Manual stretching (Stretching Interventions)
				performed by the physical therapist as the patient relaxed the body segment to be stretched
				With manual stretch, the therapist moves the segment being stretched slightly beyond tissue resistance
				Solicit feedback during manual stretching about the intensity of the stretch
				This type of stretch often indicated in early stages of rehab so that the PT can: 
					Control stretching intensities to make sure patient does not over or under-apply stretch
					Instantly monitor patient response
				---------------------------------------- 
				(See [Kin_StretchTechGeneral])
	-------------------------------------------------------------------
	Parameters Associated with Stretching Exercises/Interventions
		Appropriate stabilization
			Affective stretch involves fixation of either the proximal or distal attachment of the muscle
				When the stretch is performed manually by the PT, it is most commonly to stabilize the proximal segment and move distal segment 
					I.e.: manual stretch of the rectus femoris involves stabilization of the pelvis and spine, and movement of the femur 
				When the stretch is performed by the patient, he/she can externally stabilize the appropriate segment(s) 
					I.e.: for the hamstrings, the distal segment is fixated on a supporting surface
		Intensity of stretch
			-The amount of force applied when stretching tissues. This is likely dependent on the status of the patient’s tissues and his/her tolerance to stretch
				-Stretching of newly healed tissues or tissues that have been immobilized for some time should be applied more conservatively to avoid injury and inflammation 
				-Typically a “Low” intensity is best 
				-Force applied reaches the point of tissue resistance 
				-Feels comfortable to slightly uncomfortable, but does not cause sharp pain or reproduction of the patient’s symptoms
				-Over the course of the duration, if tolerated, additional stretch may applied as muscle guarding diminishes and tissue accommodates
		Duration of stretch
			Research and opinions vary greatly on this and it is likely dependent on the patient and the reason for performing stretch
				Regardless, it is essential to hold the stretch for greater than the 10-15 second holds
			For basic flexibility: 
				30-60 second duration is probably optimal 
				Combinations of frequency and total duration may influence this 
				If more bouts of stretching are performed, the duration of each stretch applied may not need to be as long 
					i.e.;  2 bouts of 60 seconds of stretch may be as beneficial as 4 bouts of 30 seconds of stretch 
			For significantly shortened tissues: 
				Duration of stretch should be minutes to many minutes as tolerated 
			------------------------------------------------------------
			How rapidly tissues are elongated depends up on the purpose for performing stretching
				-For basic flexibility and stretching of shortened tissues, stretch should be applied slowly
				-Elongated tissues more rapidly and for shorter durations is known as "ballistic stretching". The purpose of ballistic stretching is to decrease sensitivity to quick stretch of tissues during functional activity 
					I.e.: 
						-Dancers who must perform high kicks overhead 
						-Gymnasts landing in a split position 
								I.e.: A football lineman who will have his arm tissues stretched as he attempts to block someone attempting to run past him 
						-The mechanism of ballistic stretch is to decrease sensitivity of golgi tendon organs and muscles spindles to prevent muscle guarding 
						-Ballistic stretching is NOT appropriate or effective for lengthening shortened tissue
						-Frequency of stretch
		Frequency of stretch
			If the patient has no Pathology, daily 
			With tissue irritability? 
				2-5 sessions/week 
				Examine for signs of inflammation
		---------------------------------------------
		How often should one stretch?
			If the patient has no pathology, daily 
		With tissue irritability? 
			2-5 sessions/week 
		Examine for signs of inflammation (See "Exercise Prescription for Stretching")
	-------------------------------------------------------------------
	Cases
		Questions 1
			Patient 1 is unable to achieve full or active or passive knee extension
				Neither ROM nor stretching exercises are most needed
				ROM exercises are most needed
				Stretching exercises are most needed

				Stretching exercises are most needed
					-Because passive range is limited, this shows that soft tissues have adaptively shortened. Stretching will be necessary to apply enough elongation stress to cause them to return to normal length. ROM exercises will not provide sufficient stretch because they are not sustained Γ(per això ROM no es igual a estirament, perque ROM no es moviment suspés al final del rango!)
		Questions 2
			Patient 2 is unable to achieve full active knee extension but can achieve full passive knee extension
				Neither ROM nor stretching exercises are most needed
				ROM exercises are most needed
				Stretching exercises are most needed

			ROM exercises are most needed
				-ROM exercises here are beneficial to maintain the passive range that the individual has. Without them, adaptive tissue shortening may occur. Stretching is not necessary because the patient has full passive range
		Questions 2
			Patient 3 is able to achieve both full active and passive knee extension but unable to extend his knee with a weight on his ankle
				Neither ROM nor stretching exercises are most needed
				ROM exercises are most needed
				Stretching exercises are most needed

				Neither ROM nor stretching exercises are most needed
					-Neither are indicated because the patient can both actively and passively move through full range. The problem is muscle weakness and neither ROM, nor stretching will achieve this. - only resistance training will.

		ROM Exercises Performed in Gravity Minimized Positions
			If a patient has at least 2/5 strength (is able to move full range in a gravity minimized position) but has <3/5 strength (does not have full movement against gravity), AROM exercises are likely most beneficial performed in a gravity minimized position
				Examples may include:
					Hip abduction performed in supine
					Hip and shoulder flexion performed in side-lying

			.Consider the following example:
				It would be most appropriate to prescribe AAROM exercises in a gravity minimized position for a patient who has ___ .
					3-/5 strength
					2+/5 strength
					2-/5 strength
					1/5 strength

					2-/5 strength
						-If the patient has 2-/5 strength, they can almost perform full AROM movement in a gravity minimized position. Therefore, if they get some assistance, they can likely perform this motion, but be challenged enough for it to be of benefit.

For ROM, MMT & Stretching in general
	-As a PT, it is important to know when to modify exercise based on difficulty and/or progression, as well as strategies for modifying the difficulty of exercises
	--------------------------------------------------------------
	Parameters Associated with Exercise Prescription
		-If too little is prescribed, it will not produce the desired changes
		-If too much is prescribed, it may be harmful
		-Dosage of exercise needs to be provided at a level that meets the threshold to produce bodily changes (principles from the Physical Stress Theory)
			The goal is to prescribe exercise at a level that meets threshold without exceeding it enough to cause injury
				low                 (below the Threshold) (minimal to no changes)
				moderate            (in the Threshold) (positive changes)
				intense             (above the Threshold)
				excessively intense (above the Threshold) (injury)
	---------------------------------------------------------------
	How do you determine initial parameters with a patient?
		1st.Refer to the tests and measures obtained during the physical exam
		2nd.
			1. As a General rule, if the patient has a significant lack of AROM
				-resistance training is not yet appropriate. In this instance,
				-AROM and AAROM are likely appropriately used to gain range and overload muscles since basic movements such as these likely create stress above threshold levels
				-Isometric exercises may also be appropriate in this specific example
			2. MMT and other forms of strength testing will provide general guidelines on how much resistance they may tolerate
			3. Modify the initial parameters based on the severity and irritability (see SINSS) of the patient’s symptoms
				Be initially conservative with patients who have higher levels of severity and irritability
					SEVERITY
						How intense is the patient's pain when present?
					IRRITABILITY
						How easily are the patient's symptoms provoked?
						Once provoked, how long does it take for symptoms to subside?
	---------------------------------------------------------------
	Cases
		Case 1:
		After completing the initial evaluation, you prescribe bridging exercises for a patient with weak gluteal muscles. The patient easily completes 10 repetitions and reports that the exercise was “minimally challenging” (2/10) difficulty.

		What are some ways you could increase the challenge of the bridging exercise to help this patient achieve the appropriate level of tissue overload?

		Case 2:
		Last visit, you prescribed squatting exercises for a patient to help decrease lower extremity strength. Squatting was performed only against the resistance of body weight for two sets of ten repetitions. During each squat the patient was instructed to lower his body so that his hips and knees were each flexed to a 90-degree angle. On this visit, the patient reports feeling 6/10 pain in his hips and knees for about an hour after performing the exercise and significantly lower extremity fatigue for the rest of the day.

		What are some ways you could modify the way the patient performs this exercise to reduce the level of difficulty and minimize post-exercise pain and fatigue?

Principles in Exercise Instruction - Strategies for Effective Instruction
	Teaching exercise and educating a patient are two different things
	-------------------------------------------------------------------
	1. Non-distracting environment
	2. Demonstrate proper performance
	3. Have patient model performance
	4. When necessary, manually guide patient through movement
	7. When providing feedback, explain why something is incorrect
	8. Teach exercises in small increments

	5. Clear and consise verbal & written instructions
	6. Compliment written exercises with pictures

	--------------------------------------------------------------- (d'on t'has tret açò ¿?) ☠
	AROM vs AAROM vs PROM vs Stretching Exercises
		AROM When:
			Existing AROM maintenance when another structure immobilized
		AAROM When:
			Same as AROM
			Movement very limited (help is needed)
		PROM When:
			PREC_NoContr
			Existing contraction maintenance
			Stiffness/Contracture avoidance
				Not a subtitute of Stretching
		Stretching Exercises When:
			To regain full ROM before other treatments

			Mechanical vs. Non-Mechanical Pain
				R1.8.Mechanical Pain
					-Patient will report an incident, traumatic event, or an event that precipitates the onset of symptoms
					-Symptoms can be aggravated or relieved with changes in body or limb position or as a response to specific movements
					-Symptoms can be reproduced or provoked with:
						Palpation
						Active or passive movement
						Resistive Tests
						Special Tests
				R1.9.Non-Mechanical Pain
					Difficult to connect the onset of pain with a specific incident or event
					Visceral pain is characterized as:
						Dull, diffuse, poorly localized
						May rhythmically build and recede
						May be described as "constant"
						Does not change with changes in posture or position
					Symptoms can be reproduced or provoked with:
						Chemical or mechanical stimulus
						Typically, within the organ's own environment
						Follow a predictable referral pattern
			General Signs and Symptoms - 5 D's, And 3 N's
				5 D's
					Diplopia
					Dysarthria
					Dizziness
					Dysphagia
					Drop Attacks
				And (A)
					Ataxia
				3 N's
					Nausea
					Nystagmus
					Numbness
		Yellow Flags
			There are two ways in which this term is used in rehabilitation settings
				Y1.A finding that is atypical for the patient's presumed, current condition, but not something that warrants immediate investigation or action
					-A finding that "could be indicative of a red flag condition" but given the patient's individual situation, more likely is not
					-A reason for "ongoing monitoring" of the situation
						I.e.: 30-year-old female with knee pain around the patella
							Reports recently losing 10 pounds and occasional lightheadedness, but is otherwise healthy
								-Weight loss and light-headedness could be associated with cancer and/or neurological issues but because the patient is otherwise healthy, these could be normal findings
				Y2.Cognitive or psychosocial risk factors associated with chronic pain or disability"
					I.e.:
						Depression
						Catastrophizing
						Y6.Fear-avoidance beliefs
			Categories of Yellow Flag psychosocial risk factors
				Y3.Depression
				Y4.Anger
				Y5.Trait anxiety (emotional stability)
				Y6.Fear-avoidance beliefs for physical activities
					Fear-Avoidance Beliefs Questionnaire.png
				Y7.Pain catastrophizing
				Y8.Rehabilitation self-efficacy
					Beliefs that rehabilitation will/will not help?
			Validated Screening Tools for Yellow Flag Conditions
				Anxiety and Depression
					Patient Health Questionnaire - 9 item screen (PHQ-9)
						Identifies presence and severity of these mental health issues
				Y6.Fear of Movement
					Fear-Avoidance Beliefs Questionnaire (FABQ)
						16 item screen with 2 subsections
					Tampa Scale of Kinesiophobia
						17 item screen which measures fear of low back pain (LBP)
				Pain Catastrophizing
					Pain Catastrophizing Scale (PCS)
						Assesses extent of catastrophizing
		Orange Flags
			The presence of serious psychological or mental health issues that are likely to affect the outcome of rehabilitation
				I.e.:
					O1.Severe mental illness
					O2.Personality Disorders
					03.Illicit Drug Use
					------------------------------------
					PHQ Patient Health Questionnaire.png

Lower Quarter Screening (LQS) (AKA Neurological Screen) [MSK1_LQS]
	Exam and Treatment of the Lower Extremity (generality) [MSK1_L01] [MSK1_L02]
		For each region:
		In general, how do we evaluate?
		Common conditions
			For each condition:
				What do patients with this tell you?
				What do you often find on common?
				How do we USUALLY treat it?
	------------------------------------------------------
	What is a “screening procedure”? [MSK1_V01_11]
		Used to determine if the patient needs to be examined in more detailed fashion for a certain problem or not
	What is a Lower Quarter Screen (LQS) procedure”? Why do we use it?
		To identify Lumbosacaral Nerve Roots (radiculopathy) that can be damaged
			-Can be:
				compression
				inflamation
				overstretch injury
			-This way, the area of the skin that correspond to them, can be affected as well (that is why we perform Dermatome Testing)
		To identify if there is a neurolocal issue (Γ and differenciate between UMN or LMN)
	When should a physical therapist perform one? 
		Purpose of a basic Lower Quarter Screen
			-Performed when neurological involvement is SUSPECTED or POSSIBLE but not certain/This is the way in which we can screen for potentially neurologic issues
				If neurological involvement is CERTAIN then
					screening is omitted and detailed neuromuscular exam is performed
			Not usually performed if neurological involvement is not suspected or highly unlikely
				But - always err on the side of caution
	Standard Components of the Lower Quarter Screen (LQS)
		Lower Motor Neuron Function
			LQS Component 1: Dermatome Testing (AKA Sensation Testing)
				Derma = Dermis = Sensation
				We start 1st with this one
				You should know the deramtomes
				We compare side to side to see if it feels similar
				We screen the areas most likely to be affected
				---------------------------------------------
				Sensory innervation of spinal nerve roots
					Best Practices
						Light touch should be applied using an object that creates a neutral stimulus
							This means the stimulus is not hot, cold, or sharp
							Good examples include use of a cotton swab or very small brush
							Bad examples include
								use of metal (may feel cold)
								your finger (may feel warm or cold)
								the sharp end of a paper clip
						Procesure
							Apply light touch within the dermatome being tested on one side of the body
								Re-apply light touch in the same spot on the opposite side of the body
									Determine if the patient responds differently or the same to the stimulus one each side of the body
										Repeat for each remaining dermatome
				--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
				-Dermatome Regions - The individual dermatome regions within which Sensation Testing should be performed are listed below. [MSK1_DermatomeTest] (!!E)
					-(See "dermatomes MSK1.pdf", "Dermatome Testing MSK1.png" & "01_12 LowerQuarterScreen_03_Dermatomes 06.01.2024_14.42.53_REC.mp4")
					-Dermatome Testing is only the intial screen (it must be fast), if you find something and not sure and you need in more deep, you will learn more testings techniques in Neurology. Also, you do not have to visualize perfectly the myotomes (did you mean dermatomes? ¿?) in the first checking, and if you find something you can go back to make sure you differenciate which one is for sure
					----------------------------------------------------------------------------
					(Γ)Llegenda
						C = Cervical
						T = Torso
						L = Leg
						S = Sacrum/Side
					(Γ)¦
						C5 = gordo amb biceps (gros però muculós)
						C6 = síbol del OK
						C7 = soldat = Pistola
						C8 = el que queda
					L1 – Across inguinal region & across inguinal ligament
					L2 – Mid & anterior thigh, 2 inches (5 cm) below the ASIS
					L3 – Distal anterior mid thigh (middle 3rd) to the medial epicondyle
					L4 – Medial portion of the thigh, then acros patella to the medial malleolus
						¦4 = acció de sentarse amb el genoll
							T4 també es important per que cau en els mugrons
					L5 – Fibular head to great toe across the dorsum of the foot
						¦Dorsum i planta del peu
					S1 – From the lateral side of the leg, across the lateral calf, to the lateral border of foot (plantar surface of the foot) ¦Side = "S1"
					S2 – Along the medial aspect of the posterior thigh. And to the medial calf and medial plantar surface of the foot
					-----------------------------------------------------------------------------
					S3 – Isquial tuberosity. Perineal region – not typically tested during routine screening
					S4/S5 – Perineal region - not typically tested during routine screening ¦ 5 por el culo te la inco
						--------------------------------------------------
						No often tested neither worth it (perineal region)
					(See slight differences from this version on 'CNVS-Immersion 3A - 2024 - Student.pptx::slide 3')
				----------------------------------------------------------
				ASIA Key Root Level Points vs. Dermatome Testing [MSK1_ASIA]
					How do we perform sensation testing?
						As is the case many times, depends upon your purpose
					The American Spinal Injury Association (ASIA) Key Points
						-One spot within each dermatome thought to be MOST consistently innervated by nerve fibers from that root level
						-In testing Key Points, PT applies light touch only over that spot
						-In My Opinion (IMO): More effectively/appropriately used to assess sensation in patients with severe or definitive neurological impairment (especially of CNS)
							I.e.: If somebody has spinal cord injury (it is a major deficit)
								Pre-series side to side and at different levels, but in MSK we do it in a diferent way (see next):
									Broader Dermatome Testing
										Involves using longer “strokes” to assess light touch
										Each stroke covers a large portion of the dermatome
										Unilateral vs. Bi-Lateral Testing
											MY PREFERENCE IS UNILATERAL
												-PT performs a stroke on one side of the body and then repeats in same area on opposite side
												-If the body has a slight difference side to side, the brain will have a hard moment differenciating
											Some PTs will perform strokes on both sides of the body simultaneously
												What patients might this work for?
												Which patients would have a harder time discerning side to side differences?
					ASIA KEY Points vs. strokes ("CNVS-IMMERSION 2A - SPRING 2024.pptx")
						Key Point Examples
							Patient w/spinal cord injury
								Trying to establish which levels are/"are not" affected
							Patient with CVA or TBI
								Determining drastic R vs. L differences
							Patient with SEVERE radiculopathy (Nerve Root) or peripheral neuropathy
								Which side(s)/Levels severely involved?
								If usure of the case, this is the category in which most surely fall into!
						Broader Stroke Examples
							SCREEN for neural involvement of unknown nature or severity (i.e.; LQS)
							``Nerve Root or Peripheral Nerve pathology of Mild to Moderate Severity
								-Nerve Root involvement may occur in differing pattern than keypoints
									Neural Anatomical Anomalies
								-Peripheral may show deficits in only PART of dermatome and may be different pattern than Key Points
						------------------------------------------
						FOR MSK PRACTICAL EXAM Imm2A:
							Best bet is going to be to perform sensation screening:
								Using multiple longer strokes within each dermatome
								Testing one side at a time
						-------------------------------------------
						(See differences from this version on 'CNVS-Immersion 3A - 2024 - Student.pptx::slide 3')
			LQS Component 2: Myotome Testing [MSK1_MyotTest]
				A gross strenght test to evaluate the integrity of the nerve roots
				Motor innervation of spinal nerve roots
				Myo = Muscle = Motor
					Myotome Testing Procedure 
						-The majority of the steps for performing myotome testing are typically the same as those for performing a manual muscle test MMT. However, remember that this is not MMT and you do not assign a grade, only see if it strong or weak!
						---------------------------------------------------------------------
						This should be quick to perform too
						1. Place the patient in a position where they activate a specific muscle or muscle group in relative isolation
						2. "Stabilize" the body region proximal to the muscle/muscle group being tested
						3. Apply manual resistance to the body region distal to the muscle/muscle group being tested
						Note: A zero to five grade is NOT assigned during myotome testing. Instead, the physical therapist looks for relative strength or weakness in comparison to the opposite side.
						----------------------------------------------------------------------
						-Myotome testing involves
							-testing of muscles that are most characteristically innervated by peripheral nerves that originate from the nerve root(s) being tested.
						-The muscle/muscle groups that you will be expected to test for each myotome are: [MSK1_MyotTest] (!!E)
							(See "01_13 LowerQuarterScreen_01_Myotomes 06.01.2024_14.55.35_REC.mp4")
							(See 'OneNote
								::Innerv Kin Com Proj::Top of the document
								::Actions
								::Down on the document::Fang')
							L2 L3 – Hip Flexors
							L3 L4 – Knee Extensors (Can be tested functionally with a step up)
							L4 (L5) – Ankle Dorsiflexion
								Can be tested functionally with Active Heel Walking
							L5 – Great Toe Extension
								¦Fang = Fam = Panxa = Panxa de Superman i Superwoman
							S1 – Ankle Plantarflexion
								Can be tested functionally with heel rises or Active Toe Walking 
								¦Superman Hook Kick ¦L5 i S1 són contraris (Ext, Flex)
							L5 S1 – Knee Flexion (Hip Extension can also be tested) ← From the video; not mandatory
								¦Cuan s'ajunten L5 S1, continua amb la filosofia "Superman Hook Kick", però no de contacte taló sino de planta del peu
							S1 S2 – Ankle Eversion (& Ankle Plantarflexion)
								¦Aversió (=eversió) a les lesvianes Superherois
							S2 S3 S4 – They don't have muscular representation, but are usually indicated with a historical finding of "bowel and bladder problems" ← From the video; not mandatory

							-Note: Make them do the functional test if there is one better, because it puts more muscle demand. However, if you see obvious deficits with the initial test, do not make them do the functional test

						-(See "myotome testing MSK1.pdf") (Lx)=¿? (!!E)Revisitar 'OneNote::Anat' primer
							PROXIMAL NERVES
								ILIACUS                               L2 L3
								ADDUCTOR LONGUS (OBTURATOR)           L2 L3 L4

								RECTUS FEMORIS (FEMORAL)              L2 L3 L4
								TENSOR FASCIA LATA (GLUTEAL)          	       L5 (S1)

								GLUTEUS MAXIMUS (INFERIOR GLUTEAL)             (L5) S1
							SCIATIC NERVES
								SEMITENDINOSUS/MEMBRANOSUS (TIBIAL)            L5
								BICEPS FEMORIS (SHT HD) (PERONEAL)                S1 (S2)
								BICEPS FEMORIS (LNG HD) (TIBIAL)                  S1 (S2)
							PERONEAL NERVES

							TIBIAL NERVES

						I.e.:
							If the patient has a problem on L2
								pt. may feel numbness in this area
									1st.→ L2 – Mid Thigh, anterior ← Dermatome Regions ← LQS Component 1: Dermatome Testing
								pt. may experience weakness here
									2nd.→ L2 L3 – Hip Flexors ← Myotome, The muscle/muscle groups ← LQS Component 2: Myotome Testing

							Early experiments on monkeys; not allowed today on humans ← Reason for variations on human bodies

			LQS Component 3: Deep Tendon Reflexes (DTRs) (AKA: Lower Motor Neuron Reflexes)
				("01_14 LowerQuarterScreen_04_Deep Tendon Reflex Testing 06.01.2024_15.32.31_REC.mp4")
				Assessment of the involuntary reflex loop between musculotendinous structures and the spinal cord
				Used to assess integrity of the neural connections between peripheral nerve structures and the spinal cord
					-About using DTRs to test for nerve roots vs. peripheral nerves. DTRs are used to identify Nerve Root pathology and not peripheral nerve lesions. However, because there is some phrasing in the asynchronous lesson that might make this confusing, I am giving credit for both answers. Specifically, it is stated that "deep tendon reflexes are used to assess integrity of the neural connections between peripheral nerve structures and the spinal cord". It is true that the DTRs assess the integrity of "connections" between the SC and peripheral nerves, but the connection between these is a nerve root.
					-So to be clear, while a peripheral nerve lesion with involvement of the nerve root can produce an abnormal DTR response, a DTR test will not efficiently help you to identify if a peripheral nerve is the source of the deficit. Below is a more detailed explanation of why this is the case.
					-While a major lesion to a very specific peripheral nerve might affect a reflex response, DTRs are not helpful or not used to identify peripheral nerve lesions. If there is pathology of a nerve root the DTR may be used, along with information from dermatome and myotome testing to determine if this is the case, and if so, which one(s) based on the pattern you see with these tests (i.e., Quadriceps = L3, L4; Achilles = S1, S2; Posterior Tibilialis L4, L5 - not shown in videos but we will do at immersion).
					-A peripheral nerve deficit will present in different patterns than nerve root pathologies. Certain sensory nerves (known as cutaneous nerves) provide innervation to patches of skin and there is some overlap with dermatomes but not totally the same - usually a much smaller area. And same is true with motor nerves - there will be some overlap with the muscles activated during myotome testing but not exactly.
					-In a nutshell, a lesion affecting most peripheral nerves will not affect a DTR and even if there is a lesion to nerves to the quads, gastrocs, or tib posterior (the muscles associated with DTRs in LE) and it would have to be a very serious lesion - and most peripheral nerve injuries are of lesser involvement.
					------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
					This involves activity of the monosynaptic reflex arc: ("Testing Reflexes MSK1.png")
						1 A reflex hammer strikes a tendon activating mechanoreceptors
						2 Mechanoreceptors stimulate afferent neural signals that travel via a peripheral nerve to the spinal cord (shown in blue)
						3 Within the spinal cord, efferent (motor) neural activity travels back down to the muscle being tested (shown in red)
						4 This results in an involuntary contraction of the muscle
				Grading of Deep Tendon Reflexes (DTRs) (!!E.Grade & Doc).Kin
					Based on the intensity of the involuntary contraction elicited, a reflex grade of 0-4 is assigned
						Response is absent
							Grade of 0 Γ(Gr.0)
						Response is present but diminished
							Grade of 1+ Γ(Gr.1+)
								-Note that the term “diminished” is in comparison to the opposite side and/or what would be considered a normal response for most individuals.
						Response is normal
							Grade of 2+ Γ(Gr.2+)
								Again, “normal” is in reference to a typical response for this test.
						Response is hyperactive
							Grade of 3+ Γ(Gr.3+)
								A hyperactive response is a more pronounced muscle contraction than would be typical
						Response is hypertonic/clonus
							Grade of 4+ Γ(Gr.3+)
								A hypertonic or clonus response is one that is
									significantly more pronounced in comparison to a typical response and
									may indicate upper motor neuron (CNS) pathology.
				Question 9
					Deep Tendon Reflex Testing is performed to determine if which of the following types of anatomical structures is causing the patient's deficits?
					    Tendons
					    Muscles
					    Nerve Roots
					    Peripheral Nerves

					    ✓Nerve Roots
			----------------------------------------------
			Testing of Deep Tendon Reflexes AKA Lower Motor Neuron Reflexes
				("01_14 LowerQuarterScreen_04_Deep Tendon Reflex Testing 06.01.2024_15.32.31_REC.mp4") [MSK1_ReflexTest]
				Deep tendon & pathologic reflex testing
					DTR Good Practices
						Palpate first if necessary
						Hold on loosely (but don’t let go…)
						Be a drummer
						ALMOST ALWAYS use flat side of the hammer
							The pointy side is for biceps tendon where we hit our fingernail
						Make sure you get multiple responses for each one, do not do it only one time
				----------------------------------------------------------------------------------
				Patellar Tendon Reflex
					Tests the integrity of the L2 L3 L4 nerve roots
					You can use the Jendrassik's Maneuver Γup to 10 seconds, to favor the response
						("Jendrassik's Maneuver.jpg" & "Jendrassik's Maneuver 02.jpg")
							Reinforcement technique
								Upper extremities
									clench teeth
									squeeze thigh
								Lower extremities
									lock fingers and pull one against the other
								FYI:
									-Voluntary UMN innervation of the arm "overflows" to increase the excitability of the LMN pool ofthe lower extremities (makes easier to elicitthe reflex)
				Medial Hamstring Tendon Reflex
					Γ Medial; at popliteal space
					Tests the integrity of the L5 S1 nerve roots
				Achilles Tendon Reflex
					Tests the integrity of the S1 S2 nerve roots
					Get the tendon in slightly stretched position
						Put your fingers underneath the foot and raise the foot up; make sure they are relaxed
				Posterior Tibialis Tendon Reflex
					Tests the integrity of the L5 S1 nerve roots
						Fingers just under the 4th and 5th metacarpal heads (met heads)
							Pull into dorsifelxion and eversion
								Strike in the arch of the foot
			----------------------------------------------
			From [CNVS-Immersion 3A - 2024 - Student.ppt::slide 5']
				Pathological Reflexes (Upper motor neuron)
					Babinski
					Hoffmans
					Ankle Clonus 
			----------------------------------------------
			Interpretation of DTRs may be summarized as follows (Interpreting Lower Quarter Screen LQS Findings)
				[MSK1_InterretDTR01]
				1.Response is the same on both sides
					No evidence of a lesion affecting a peripheral nerve or nerve root
					NOTE: This is true if both sides are equally absent, diminished, or hyperactive, but NOT if both sides are hypertonic
				2.The response on the involved side is absent or diminished in comparison to the uninvolved side
					Possible lesion affecting a peripheral nerve or nerve root
				3.The response on the involved side or both sides is hypertonic
					-Possible lesion affecting an upper motor neuron (CNS structure) PT should consider an immediate follow up with a neurologist or other specialist
					.................................................................................
					(Γ)Em preocupe cuan
						2.Involved side absent or diminished (ff from involved) (peripheral nerve or nerve root)
						3.Hypertonic en un costat o els dos = Hypoclonus
				--------------------------------- [☠.1]
				Red Flag Differential Diagnosis ("Red Flag Differential Diagnosis.png")
					-(See [MSK2_MyelopathyTable]::09 MSQ Back.txt, It is the same)
					-Radiculopathy = Imparement of a nerve root in one side of the body
						It is not common or even coincidential to have it on both sides at the same time
					-Myelopathy = Imparement or compression of spinal cord or other central nervous system structure (an upper motor neuron lession)
					------------------------------------
					Sign/Symptom
						Myotomal Weakness
							Radiculopathy
								+ = We will see weakness on one side of the body
							Myelopathy
								+ = We will see weakness on one side of the body but differently
						Dermatomal Change
							Radiculopathy
								+ = Loss of sensation
							Myelopathy
								+ = Loss of sensation
						Deep Tendon Reflex
							Radiculopathy
								Diminished
							Myelopathy
								Hyper-reflexia/Hyperreflexia (UMN) = A clonic response, not suddle and exagerated
						Upper Motor Neuron Signs (ankle clonus, positive Babinski sign, and Hoffman’s sign, DTR’s hyperflexic)
							Radiculopathy
								N/A 
							Myelopathy
								+
						Ataxia (incoordinated movement)
							Radiculopathy
								N/A 
							Myelopathy
								+
						Unilateral or Bilateral Neuro Signs (very imprtant)
							Radiculopathy
								Unilateral
							Myelopathy
								Bilateral weakness and numbness (concerning!!)
			---------------------------------------------
			These forms of testing mainly focus on determining if there is impairment of a lower motor neuron structure
			Another way of saying this would be identifying a lesion that affects peripheral nervous system structure
		Pathologic Reflexes (Testing of Pathological Reflexes) [MSK1_PatholReflex]
			AKA: Pathology affecting an "Upper Motor Neuron"
			AKA: A lesion affecting the central nervous system
			--------------------------------------------------
			For some patients, it is important to perform another aspect of neurologic screening like this one.
			What are pathological reflexes?
				Used to assess for the possible presence of a lesion affecting an Upper Motor Neuron (Central Nervous System).
			When should they be performed?
				When, as a result a patient history or other physical findings, leads the physical therapist to suspect that the patient MIGHT have an upper motor neuron lesion.
				--------------------------------------------------
				Pathologic Reflex Test 1: Assessing for Ankle Clonus Reflex
					("01_14 LowerQuarterScreen_04_Deep Tendon Reflex Testing 06.01.2024_15.32.31_REC.mp4")
					1. The patient typically lies in a relaxed supine position.
					2. The physical therapist slightly flexes the leg to be examined at the knee and with the other hand gently moving the ankle a couple of times in dorsiflexion and plantarflexion of the ankle, before rapidly dorsiflexing the ankle.
					3. The physical therapist holds the ankle in dorsiflexion. The initial brisk dorsiflexion and sustained pressure can be done with slight eversion of the foot.
					4. A positive Clonus sign is recorded when the examiner feels and sees the oscillations against this pressure.
				Pathologic Reflex Test 2: Assessing for a Babinski Sign
					("01_16 LowerQuarterScreen_04_Babinski Sign 06.01.2024_17.20.04_REC.mp4")
					1. The patient is typically supine.
					2. The plantar surface of the patient’s foot is stroked with the examiners nail or other semi-sharp instrument (metal end of reflex hammer), moving from the posterolateral aspect of the foot, along the lateral border and across the metatarsals toward the ball of the foot.
					3. A positive finding is great toe extension and fanning of the smaller digits
				Pathologic Reflex Test 3: Assessing for Hoffman’s Sign
					The patient is typically seated and relaxed with the middle finger slightly extended.
						1. The PT applies a “noxious stimulus”, usually a flick using his/her own finger, to the nail bed or distal phalanx
						2. A positive finding (opens in a new tab) is flexion of the IP joint of the thumb. This is sometimes accompanied by flexion of the index finger as well.

		UMN vs. LMN
			UMN
				Myelopathy
					(Γ)An injury to the spinal cord due to severe compression that may result from trauma, congenital stenosis, degenerative disease or disc herniation.
				It is very general in comparison to LMN
				You will see
					Hyperreflexia
					Clonus
					Babinski
					Hoffman
					Things more bilaterally
					Potentially several levels
			LMN
				N/A

			What does your LQS suggest? ← This is what we try to get from LQS
				.Is this case appropiate for a PT? (PENDENT x completar ¿?)
					✓.Yes
						.It is neurological (neurological can be mechanical too) (is it nero-based pain or not? ¿?)
							.Can we treat?
								✓.Yes
									.What nerves are implicated?
										.Dermatomes (numbness & tingling)
											Mainly L5 S1
										.Myotomes (weakness & strength)
											Mainly L5 S1 S2
											.Reflexes
										.Others ¿?
								.No
									.What do we do next?
						.Is it not neurological (mechanical)
					.No
						.Referral
				.Conclusion
					Since he is Hyporeflexive and nothing else is bilateral, this is a LMN issue.

						-The fat that is "a.", it points to diabetes
						-He has also Hemosiderin Staining: it occurs when the smallest blood vessels, known as capillaries, begin to leak. This can be due to a wound, a broken bone, a surgical incision or other types of trauma. It is also associated with certain illnesses that affect circulation. The most common of these is chronic venous insufficiency.

PREC_NoChangePos/Eficiency:
	With the patient seated, the Therapist should test as many arm muscles as possible without changing position.
	Maximices Eficiency for the Therapist.

PREC_Bilat: Always test bilaterally to compare and have a reference.

PREC_ProxDist: Test always from proximal to distal.
	On the contraty, if something is wrong proximal, we'll test proximal double time.
	Testing proximal, we'll give is an idea of how things are distally.

PREC_NoMMT: We do not want to apply Resistance when:
	Due to age.
	Due to recent injury.
	Due to surgery.
	Due to prescription.
	Due to excessive pain in situ.
	Due to pain on rest.

PREC_NoContr
	Contraction of muscle is not recommended or permitted

PREC_Stretch
	Stretching without considering individual impairments
		Existing hypermobility
		Neglect of (-)hypomobile areas
	Ballistic stretching is not adequate to stretch shortened tissue.
	"No pain, No gain": avoid mostly. However, there is situations in which we want to use it.
	Consider age - Tissue becomes fragile with age.
	Consider pathology - Some patologies weaken tissue consistency.

HEAL_SoftTissueHealing
	Stages of Tissue Healing
		Scar Tissue Formation After injury
		1st Acute Inflamation Stage
			-Goal
				Prepare for growth of new tissue
			-The Acute Inflammation Stage occurs one to 4 days after injury.
			-Note that the time periods associated with the stages of healing are highly variable. Under less than ideal circumstances acute inflammation can last much longer.
			-The acute inflammation stage consists of 3 substages that prepare tissue for healing and re-growth.
				Vascular Response
					Vasoconstriction of blood vessels in the area
					followed by vasodilation of vessels flowing into the area
				Hemostatic Response
					Formation of a clot to minimize blood loss
				Cellular Response (phagocytosis)
					Recruitment of white blood cells to the area to remove debris AKA phagocytosis
		2nd.Subacute/Repair Stage
			-Goal
				Initial formation of new tissue
			-Occurs three to 20 days after an injury.
			-Fibroblasts begin to synthesize and deposit new connective tissue (collagen fibers).
			-Initial random deposition of weak scar tissue
			-The deposition of collagen fibers is initially random.
			-The first fibers deposited are very weak, then stronger fibers are deposited. More structured deposition of fibers occurs as stage progresses.
			-(Type III) Collagen
			-Acts as glue to hold wound together
		3rd.Maturation and Remodeling Stage
			Goal
				Healing tissue increases in strength
				Later re-designed to adapt to necessary stresses and to the strength
			Acute & Remodeling phases
			Stronger (Type I) fibers
			Deposited in more organized fashion
			The Maturation and Remodeling Stage begins around 21 days after an injury.
			After 3 weeks, deposition of collagen fibers occurs in a much more organized fashion.
			This process is guided by tensile fibers to which the healing tissue is subjected.
			Fibers now become aligned in positions of maximum efficiency, parallel to lines of tension applied on tissue.
			Remember, tissues experience constant turnover.
			Remodeling occurs due to collagen turnover.
			Collagen is taken up and laid down again in a more organized and efficient fashion.

PREC_BoneTissue
	-Bone tissue that is still maturing will have different properties as compared to mature bony tissue. Therefore, physical therapy management that affects bone tissue in adolescents may need to differ from that of adults.
		Long bone growth occurs in epiphyseal plates
		Area in mid-epiphysis where cartilage layer persists until maturity
		Cartilage gradually replaced with bone through calcification
			Continues through ages 18-21 until full ossification/mature adult bone
				This is the reason that ultrasound treatments over areas containing epiphyseal plates are contraindicated in adolescents. Heating this tissue may hinder growth and cause tissue damage.

PREC_DenseTissueHealingStage (bone healing) - Stages of Tissue Healing
	[MSK1_C2.3 "PT 2231_Mod 2_Principles of Fracture Repair.pptx"] 
	See also [PrinFractRep_]
	---------------------------------------
	1st.Acute Inflamation/Inflamatory Stage
		Prepare for growth of new tissue
		Dilation of blood vessels
		Prevention of infection
		Hemorrhage and cell death
		Inflammation and clot formation
		Invasion of blood vessels and macrophages into clot
		Formation of granulation tissue
		1-4,5 days after injury (highly variable)
			Under less than ideal circumstances acute inflammation can last much longer
		3 substages that prepare tissue for healing and re-growth - HEAL_Dense_AcuteInflamation_SubStages
			Vascular Response
				Vasoconstriction followed by vasodilation into the area
			Hemostatic Response
				Formation of a clot to minimize blood loss
			Cellular Response (phagocytosis)
				A primary purpose is to prevent infection
				Recruitment of white blood cells to the area to remove debris (AKA phagocytosis)
		-------------------------------------------------
		Case
			-Suzie broke her collarbone while skiing and is now recovering. Which of the following will happen FIRST as her bone begins to repair itself?
				✗Fibroblasts connect the broken ends of the bone
				✗Compact bone is formed at the site of the fracture
				✓The blood vessels in her bone that began to hemorrhage at the time of the fracture will begin to clot
	2nd.Subacute/Repair/Reparative Stage - HEAL_Dense_Subacute
		3,4-20 days (total: 1-6 weeks) after an injury
		Fibroblasts begin to synthesize and deposit new connective tissue (collagen fibers)
		Fibroblasts cells are MOST active during this stage
			-------------------------------------------------
			Deposition of collagen fibers is initially random
			Early period
				Fibroblasts begin to lay down tissue
				Fibroblasts are a quick fix
				---------------------------
					The first fibers deposited are very weak, then stronger fibers are deposited
			More structured deposition of fibers occurs as stage progresses
		Initial formation of new tissue
		Callous replaces granulation tissue
		Invasion of blood vessels begins maturation of callous
		Calcified cartilage forms
		CC progresses to trabecular bone (matured callous)
	3rd.Maturation and Remodeling Stage - HEAL_Dense_Remodeling
		See Quantity of collagen and breaking strength of connective tissue as the healing process progresses Chart
				"Quantity of collagen and breaking strength Chart.png"
			After the scar tissue is complete, the collagen fibers keep improving in strenght
			Implications for rehabilitation
				Specific Adaption to Imposed Demands (SAID) Principle ¿?
				Physical Stress Theory:
					Help the colagen turnover on redirecting the fibers in the most optimal position for strenght
					-----------------------------
					-tissues adapt when they experience a lack of stress, and when tissues are not elongated on a regular basis, the body will lay down shorter tissues that eventually will restrict movement
					-----------------------------
					-Tissues accommodate to stress by altering structure and composition to meet mechanical demands of routine loading
						Tissue exposed to higher-than-normal levels of stress will get stronger
						Tissue exposed to lower levels of stress will deteriorate
					-Maintenance Range
						The baseline level of stress to which tissues are regularly exposed over a certain period
						Tissue changes in quality when stress levels deviate from maintenance ranges
					------------------------------
					-Physical Stress Level (Chart, modified/improved) - Tissue Response to Deviation from Maintenance Ranges - PREC_Hypertrophy_Atrophy - Hypertrophy, Atrophy, & Injury
						Muscle
							Hypertrophy
								Training at a level that taxes his body slightly more than his daily functional activity
							Atrophy
								Training stops during a time (how long ¿?)
							Injury
								Training at a level that taxes his body significantly more than his daily functional activity
						Tissues Other than Muscle (Hypertrophy & Atrophy)
							Dense connective tissue in:
								Ligaments
									Hypertrophy
										Increasing of reducing exercise variables. ¿?
										More dense ¿?
									Atrophy
										N/A
								Nerve tissue
									Hypertrophy
										Increasing of reducing exercise variables. And maybe more coordination demands. ¿?
										Optimal modification and increased neurnal connections. ¿?
									Atrophy
										N/A
								Respiratory tissue in lungs
									Hypertrophy
										Increasing of reducing exercise variables. Maybe, more or less aerobic demands. ¿?
										Only more dense. ¿?
									Atrophy
										N/A
						-------------------------------------------
						Stress Levels >> Maintenance Ranges
							Overload = Injury
						Stress Levels > Maintenance Ranges
							Increased tissue tolerance to subsequent stresses
							Increased Tolerance = Adaptation/Hypertrophy (See Ch 11 i integrar ací ¿?)
								Tissue production > Tissue degeneration
								Increases in cross-section, density, volume
						Stress Levels = Maintenance Ranges
							Maintenance
								No overa increase in tissue quality
								Tissue degeneration = Tissue production
						Stress Levels < Maintenance Ranges
							Decreased tissue tolerance to subsequent stresses
							Decreased Tolerance = Atrophy
								Tissue degeneration > Tissue production
								Decrease in cross-section, density, volume
		----------------------------------------------
		Healing tissue increases in strength
		----------------------------------------------
		Months to years REPE
		Around 21 days after an injury
			0-21 days (Pendent Chart en Captures ¿?)
				Scar collagen accumulation increases & stabilizes at the end
			0-21 days(3 weeks)-42 days (6 weeks=a month and 1/2)
				Breaking Strength increases & stabilizes at the end
		----------------------------------------------[MSK1_C2.3 "PT 2231_Mod 2_Principles of Fracture Repair.pptx"]
		Months to years REPE
		Combo of bone resorption and deposition
		Restoration of normal architecture (osteons, marrow cavity)
		Remodeling rate influenced by weight bearing
		---------------------------------------------- [MSK1_C2.3 "PT 2231_Mod 2_Principles of Fracture Repair.pptx"]
		Factors Affecting Healing Time
			Age of the Patient
				Younger Patients Heal Faster
			Site and Configuration of the Fracture
				-Bones surrounded by muscle heal faster than bones that lie subcutaneously or within joints
				-Cancellous bone heals faster than compact bone
				-Epiphyseal separations heal faster than epiphyseal fractures
				-Long oblique or spiral fractures having large surface areas heal faster than transverse fractures having smaller surface areas
			Initial Displacement of the Fracture
				-Non-displaced fractures with intact periosteal sleeve (manga¿?) heal twice as fast as displaced fractures
				-The greater the displacement, the longer the healing time
			Blood Supply to Fracture Fragments
				Reduced vascularization increases risk of
					non-union
					avascular necrosis
		----------------------------------------------[MSK1_C2.3 "PT 2231_Mod 2_Principles of Fracture Repair.pptx"]
		General Treatment of Fractures
			Protection Alone
				Sling (cabestrillo)
				Indications:
					Undisplaced stable fractures of ribs, phalanges, metacarpals, clavicle (in children)
					Mild compression fractures of spine
					Impaction fracture of proximal humerus
			Immobilization by External Splinting without Reduction
				(Γ)What does reduction mean in fracture?
					-The doctor will push or pull the ends of the fractured bone until they line up. This part of the procedure is called reduction
				Relative immobilization: can still move other aspects of the extremity
				Casts (molde), splints (entablillar, entablar)
				Indications:
					Undisplaced but unstable fracture
			Closed Reduction with Immobilization ("Closed Reduction followed by immobilization.png")
				-Displaced fracture where surgeon predicts reduction can be done accurately and maintained without need for surgery
			Closed Reduction with External Fixation ("")
				Severely comminuted unstable fractures
				Open fractures with extensive soft tissue damage including arteries and nerves
				Severely comminuted unstable fractures ("")
			Closed Reduction with Internal Fixation ("")
				Can reduce fracture without surgery but can't maintain it with immobilization
				Unstable fracture of femoral neck
				Place pins and screws after reduction is achieved
			Open Reduction with Internal Fixation (ORIF) ("Open Reduction Internal Fixation (ORIF).png")
				Used when closed reduction is impossible
				Displace avulsion fractures, intra-articular fractures
				Soft tissue entrapment in fracture
				Displaced fracture crossing epiphyseal plate in children
		----------------------------------------------[MSK1_C2.3 "PT 2231_Mod 2_Principles of Fracture Repair.pptx"]

				Numbness of the extremity or
				persistent weakness of extremity muscles

				Fever may be a sign of it
				Septic Joint or Osteomyelitis
					Persistent signs of inflammation around joint or fracture site
						redness
						swelling
						pain
						increased skin temperature
		Deposition of collagen fibers occurs in a much more organized fashion
		Fibers now become aligned in positions of maximum efficiency, parallel to lines of tension applied on tissue
			-This process is guided by tensile fibers to which the healing tissue is subjected
			-Remember, tissues experience constant turnover. Remodeling occurs due to collagen turnover. Collagen is taken up and laid down again in a more organized and efficient fashion.
		Total quantity of connective tissue remains the same throughout stage
