	-----------------------------------------------
	Creep
		Gradual lengthening of tissue due to a constant load
			Another way to make lasting changes in tissue length without necessarily inducing plastic changes or tissue damage.
			Occurs because when we apply a sustained force Occurs molecular bonds w/in tissues shift and reform at the longer length.

PREC_BoneHealingStage - Repair/Healing of Bone Tissue in 4 stages
	1st.Hematoma formation
		This involves hemorrhage of damaged vessels & formation of clot
		THIS BEGINS IMMEDIATELY; ONLY A BASIS FOR FURTHER DEVELOPMENT OF TISSUE.
		-------------------------------------------------------------------------
		A Clot forms at ends of fracture fragments and around entire site
		Other events associated with inflammatory response also occur
			Macrophages clean up the area
		Injured tissue has no stability during this stage
	2nd.Fibrocartilaginous procallus formation
		Fibroblasts and osteoblasts form new connective tissue
		TYPICALLY 2-3 DAYS TO 2 WEEKS AFTER FRACTURE (fx)
		-------------------------------------------------------------------------
		Capillaries grow into the area
		The new, temporary tissue is known as a procallus
		Types I, II, and III collagen laid down as fibrocartilage
		Area begins to stabilize (but movement of fractur fragments still very detrimental)
	3rd.Callus formation
		Deposited along with collagen
		TYPICALLY BETWEEN 2-6 WEEKS
		-------------------------------------------------------------------------
		Calcium & phosphates are deposited in bulk
		This marks the beginning of change from cartilage to bone
		New tissue begins to mature
		Beginning of bony union & stability
		Disruption still possible but less likely
	4rt.Ossification and remodeling
		More consistent ossification of cartilage tissue occurs
		Extra callus tissue reabsorbed and replaced by mature bone
		Remodeling is accomplished through osteoclast & osteoblast activity
		Further change based on stresses
	--------------------------- [MSK1_C2.3 "PT 2231_Mod 2_Bone Fracture Classification.pptx" & "PT 2231_Mod 2_Bone Review.pptx"]
	Bony fractures
		A disruption in the continuity of the bone
		No need to study theses docs for MSK1
			Different Types of Fractures _ Bone and Spine.pdf
			Eponymous Fractures
		-----------------------
		Mechanisms and "Types" of Fractures:
			Cortical Bone ("Common Fracture Types.png" & "Folder: "Types of Fractures MSK1")
				Bending Mechanisms: Tension Failure
					A.Transverse Fractures
						A fracture in which the break is across the bone, at a right angle to the long axis of the bone
					B.Greenstick Fractures
						-A greenstick fracture occurs when a bone bends and cracks, instead of breaking completely into separate pieces
					C.Relationship of Fracture Fragments to Each Other
						C.1.Oblique Fractures
							-In this fracture, instead of the break being at the right angle, it goes in an oblique direction to the long axis of the bone. The fracture is confined to one plane. In other words, the bone has broken at an angle
								C.1.1.Oblique non-displaced/Un-displaced fracture
								C.1.2.Oblique displaced fracture
						C.2.Angulated fracture
						C.3.Translated fracture
						C.4.Rotated fracture
						C.5.Distracted fracture
						C.6.Overriding fracture
						C.7.Impacted fracture (Γ Exception: Cortical Bone + Cancellous)
							-This is a fracture in which the ends are driven into each other. Cancellous bone is typically involved, and union often occurs rapidly.
				Twisting Failure: Torsion
					D.Spiral Fracture
						-This fracture is easily confused with the oblique fracture. Instead of a straight break as in oblique fracture that is only in one plane, the break, in this case, traverses both the planes. To understand this you need to imagine a three-dimensional view of the bone.
				Traction Failure
					E.Avulsion fracture 
						("02_02 Supination ADduction (SA) (Inversion) Ankle Fracture.mp4") (PENDENT: x transcriure l'audio ¿?)
							Hi ha que vore el video per entendre tot açò!!
							Stage 1: 3 Potential Outcomes
								Radigographic Hallmark: Fracture of the Fibula Below Syndesmosis
								Note: No Syndesmotic Disruption in SA
							Stage 2: Oblique/Vertical Fracture of Medial Malleolus
								A) The position of the foot
								B) Force at the time of injury
								C) Strength of ligamentous & osseous structures
								Radigographic Hallmark: Oblique/Vertical Facture of Medial Malleolus
								Note: Multifactorial Injury
						------------------------------------------
						-Common at sites of tissue insertion (Lauge-Hansen classification)
							-(Γ)The Lauge-Hansen classification system was developed based on a cadaveric study which included imprecise replication of the true pathobiomechanics experienced during ankle fracture and incorporated deduced concepts like foot position at the time of injury and direction of the deforming force.
						-Can be either ligamentous or tendinous
						-Pulls a piece of the bone away
						-On the radiograph, avulsion fractures will be radiolucent (mes o menos brillo? ¿?) ("radiolucent Tyoes MKS1 No Official.png") as a result of hemorrhage and actual space between the fragment and the main body of bone.
			Cancellous Bone
				Compression Failure
					F.Compression fractures
						(Quina es la ff entre Compression i Impacted? ¿?)
					G.Buckle or Torus fractures
						-A torus fracture or buckle fracture is a pediatric impaction fracture in which the cortex of long bone buckles, with no loss of cortices
							-Torus is derived from Latin (tori) meaning a swelling or protuberance;
							-Failure of cortex on compression side, 2-3 cm proximal to physis;
							-Torus (buckle) fractures of distal metaphysis of radius & ulna is most common fracture in lower forearm in young children;
							-Impact of indirect violence of fall on outstretched hand crumples dorsal cortex, but the volar cortex remains intact;
								Distal fragment is angulated dorsally;
							-Deformity should not occur in torous fracture because the periosteum and cortex are intact on the side of the bone opposite to fracture. ¿?
		H.Linear fractures
		I.Comminuted fractures
			-If the injury results in multiple breaks in the bone, they are visible as different fragments. These kinds of fractures are called comminuted fractures.
		J.Segmental fracture
			-Is a fracture composed of at least two fracture lines that together isolate a segment of bone, usually a portion of the diaphysis of a long bone. The bone is fractured at two distinct levels. One fragment retains the complete cortex of the bone.
			-The reduction of this fracture is difficult and nonunion is common.
		-----------------------
		Traumatic fracture
			A traumatic fracture is when you have some type of injury or accident that disrupts the continuity of the bone.  
			Examples: being in a car crash, falling 
		Pathological fracture
			A pathologic fracture is caused by some other pathology.
			Example: a tumor in a bone will eventually lead that bone to fracture.  
		Periprosthetic fracture
			A periprosthetic fracture is a fracture that occurs in the area around where a surgery occurred.
			Example: Periprosthetic fractures in the proximal femur near where a hip replacement was performed used to be quite common.  
		Stress fracture
			A stress fracture is a tiny crack in a bone that is often caused by repetitive overuse. Most common in weight-bearing bones of the lower leg and foot
			Example: endurance sports like long-distance running
	"Classification" of bony fractures
		Relationship of Fracture Fragments to External Environment
			Open fracture
				An open fracture, also known as a compound fracture, breaks through the skin
			closed fracture
				A closed fracture, also known as a simple fracture, does not break through the skin

		Displaced
			Are MUCH more likely to require surgery, because the fracture cannot always be reduced due to misalignment of bones 
		Non-displaced
			A non-displaced fracture is preferable, because that means the ends of the bones are still aligned correctly with each other.

		Incomplete
			In an incomplete fracture, the fracture line doesn’t go all the way through the full surface of the bone
		Complete
			In a complete fracture, the fracture line goes all the way through the bone
		Comminuted
			In a comminuted fracture, small fragments or pieces of bone break off
	Another classification from "Different Types of Fractures _ Bone and Spine.pdf" (No need to study this one!!)

	Fractures are identified by the following (Description of Fractures): 
		Site ("Anatomy of a Long Bone Features Landmarks.jpg")
			Epiphysis (Γ proximal & distal)
			Epiphyseal line
				-------------------------
				Salter-Harris Classification of Epiphyseal Fractures
					("Salter Classification of Epiphyseal Fractures.png")
					("PT 2231_Mod 2_Bone Fracture Classification.pptx")
					-----------------------------------------------------
					-The Salter-Harris classification system is specific to children and is used to assess fractures that occur at growth plates. (!!E.Grade & Doc).Kin
						Type I
							Plane of separation along the epiphyseal plate through the zone of cartilage transformation
							Periosteal attachments will sometimes remain intact
							Usually produced by a shearing type force
							Blood supply intact and future growth is normal
						Type II
							Most common (75%)
							Fracture extends across the corner of metaphysis
							Periosteal hinge remains intact
							Future growth is considered normal because blood supply remains intact
						Type III
							Rare
							Usually occurs at distal tibia
							Future growth usually normal but displaced fractures produce an irregular articular surface
							Implications for rehabilitation or future issues?
						Type IV
							Intra-articular fracture that extends throught the epiphyseal plate and into the metaphysis
							ORIF with “perfect” fixation needed to have any chance at union
							May lead to nonunion/malunion with progressive disturbance of growth
						Type V
							-Crushing injuries
							-Diagnosis is difficult secondary to lack of displacement
							-May lead to permanent damage with a complete or partial cessation of growth leading to limb length discrepancy and deformity
				Winquist and Hansen classification system
					Another grading system that can be used for femoral shaft fractures.
						Follow the reading order in the image ("Winquist and Hansen classification system.png")
							"https://www.orthobullets.com/trauma/1040/femoral-shaft-fractures" (Pendent the pasar a un .doc¿?)
						---------------------------------------------------------------- (!!E.Grade & Doc).Kin
						1-A simple transverse AO Type 32-A3.2 fracture with minimal comminution (Winquist-Hansen Grade I).
						2-A simple oblique-transverse fracture without comminution (Winquist-Hansen Grade 0) of a femur that had a distal femoral fracture treated with a plate 16 years earlier.
						3-A simple oblique AO Type 32-A2.2 fracture (Winquist-Hansen Grade 0).
						4-A slightly comminuted (Winquist-Hansen Grade I) spiral AO Type 32-A1.2 fracture.
						5-A spiral AO Type 32-B1.2 fracture with a bending wedge (butterfly fragment) (WinquistHansen Grade III).
						6-A comminuted irregular complex AO Type 32-C3.1 fracture of Winquist-Hansen Grade IV.
						7-A segmental, AO Type 32-C2.1 fracture.
						8-An oblique fracture of the middle third of the femoral diaphysis with minimal comminution (Winquist-Hansen Grade I) and a dislocation of the knee.
					----------------------------------------------------------
					("Intra-articular Fracture & Fracture-dislocation.png")

	---------------------------
	Law of Physical Stress Theory for Bone Tissue = Wolff’s Law [Wolff_]
		Bone tissue will adapt to the physical demands placed upon it
		Bone responds favorably to compression loads. Will grow stronger and in direction of compression loads
		Application of weight bearing in remodeling stage of fracture healing is beneficial
			It considers increases AND decreases in baseline stresses
				When bone tissue is loaded moderately, tissue quality increases.
					-In response to compressive loads, bone tissue experiences increased deposition of mineral salts and production of collagen fibers.
					-This is done along the direction of the compression forces in a manner that remodels the bone to best withstand similar stresses.
				When bone tissue experiences decreased loading:
					-Less deposition of bone tissue occurs, with same resorption rate
					-Results in reduced bone mass and bone will become mechanically weaker
		Effects of Aging on Bone Tissue
			Bone tissue resorption becomes > deposition
				This results in:
					Reduced Bone Mass
					Bone becomes stiffer (brittle) but mechanically weaker
				Changes with age may be delayed with activities that encourage muscle force and weight bearing loads
		----------------------------------------------- [MSK1_]
		Bone is deposited in sites subjected to increased stress
		Bone is resorbed from sites of decreased stress
		Alignment of trabecular systems is along the lines of stress
		Marked cortical thickening is observed on the concave (compressive) side of a curved bone
			Electrical properties of bone (-) on the concave side (deposition) (+) on the convex side (resorption) ¿?

		Fractured Bone Has Lost
			Continuity of bone
			Continuity of the periosteum
			Continuity of the vessels
			Vitality of the bone ends
			Position and alignment
			Supporting and resisting functions
		Fractured Bone Introduces
			Inflammatory reaction
			Hemorrhage and clotting
			Pain and shock
			Cellular stimulation (bone marrow activation)
			Bone fragments
			Infection
			Interposed soft tissue
			Emboli
		Requirements for the Formation of Callus
			Source of cells
				Periosteum, endosteum, marrow
			Cellular blastema
				Stimulation to proliferate/differentiate
			Matrix synthesis
			Fusion of “old and new”
			Sufficient volume of tissue for strength
			Reworkable initial tissues
			Narrow enough for the gap to be bridged
			Freedom from gross movements, loading, infection and obstruction
			Time
		Callus Formation
			Osteoblasts form a trabecular kind of bone that is vascularized
			Bone forms subperiosteally
			Trabeculae develop in the marrow cavity
			Close to the fracture site, hyaline cartilage forms externally
			Cartilage mineralizes and is replaced endochondrally
			Bone is remodeled to become denser and more lamellar (woven)
				Formation of Haversian canals begins
			Callus volume reduces as density increases
			Shaft bone makes increased amounts of external callus unless rigidly immobilized
			Broken cancellous bone uses internal callus for rapid healing
			Marrow cavity will eventually be restored
	----------------------------
	3 major types of cartilage
		Elastic Cartilage
			Also known as yellow cartilage.
			Found in flexible structures like the outer ear and nose.
			Mostly elastin and type II cartilage fibers.
		Fibrocartilage
			Also known as white cartilage.
			More fibrous in structure like dense connective tissue found in ligaments and tendons.
			Prevalent in intervertebral disks, pubic symphysis, at tendonous attachments, and in menisci.
			Made up of type I collagen fibers.
		Articular Cartilage
			Also known as white¿? cartilage or hyaline cartilage.
			Found on the ends of long bones.
			Made up of type II collagen fibers.
			Because articular cartilage is an important component of synovial joints
			Articular cartilage exists as a thin layer on the surfaces of long bones where they meet in synovial joints.
			-----------------------------------------------------
			Composition
				Water
					60-85%
				Solid Matter
					Cells known as chondrocytes (10% of tissue)
					Type II collagen fibers (10-30% of tissue)
					Proteoglycans (3-10% of tissue)
			----------------------------------------------------
			Chondrocytes are mature cartilage cells that produce → Cartilage Fibers:
				-Cartilaginous matrix (this consists of proteoglycans and hyaluronic acid, a gel polysaccharide that helps matrix substances bind)
				-The proteoglycan aggregate becomes entrapped within a collagen network, and together they form the supportive structural matrix of articular cartilage.

PROC_CapsPatt
	Described in terms of which movements are most limited to those that are least limited
	Determine that a patient’s structure is limited in a capsular pattern. Diagnose if the integrity of the joint capsule is a source of limitation
		It provides direction on how to restore normal motion
		When the articular joint capsule of a joint becomes ... movement at that joint will become limited in a predictable pattern
			...injured or 
			adaptively shortened, ...
		--------------------------------------------------------------
		Perform
			joint mobilization assessment that stretches the shoulder capsule and
			provide the patient with exercises that increase capsule flexibility

PROC_ResisTrain - Resistance Training
	Goals - are to increase
		Strength
			 The ability to produce tension and force during a single, maximum effort.
			 This occurs in response to instantaneous demands to overcome resistance.
			 Increases in strength occur due to neural adaptation or muscle fiber hypertrophy.
			 ----------------------------
			 Neural Adaptations VS Hypertrophy Timeline (See "Neural Adaptations VS Hypertrophy Timeline.png")
				 In general:
					 2-3 wks for significant neural adaptations
					 6-12 weeks for hypertrophy and tissue changes
					 Strenght comes much later
		Endurance
		Power
		---------------------------
		I.e.: Strength or Endurance? 
			Endurance
				Shoulder pain with overhead reaching due to rotator cuff impairment (IR and ER)
				Low back pain due to impairment of lumbar paraspinals
			Strength
				Difficulty with sit to stand due to quadriceps impairment
	--------------------------------------
	Resistance Exercise Programs (See "Exercise Prescription for ROM (Indications for PROM Exercises) - Elements of Resistance Exercise Programs")
	--------------------------------------
	Determining Intensity
		High VS Low Representations
			May be determined using a perceived scale of exertion that asks how difficult the effort was
				Rate of Perceived Exertion Scale
					1 (nothing at all)
					2 (weak)
					3 (moderate)
					4 (somewhat stron )
					5 (strong)
					6
					7 (very strong)
					8
					9
					10 (very, very stron)
	--------------------------------------
	Using variations of One-Rep Max test
		See SUB_5RM - Five-Rep Max, SUB_10RM - Ten-Rep Max, & SUB_RepsToFat - Reps to Fatigue
			Case Study
				Marilyn is beginning a training program.  Her trainer asks how much weight she has safely used in the past when performing biceps curls.  Based on this, the trainer asks Marilyn to perform biceps curls until she is “significantly fatigued” with 20.  Marilyn stops after performing 12 repetitions.
					1st.Which percentage of her one rep max is 20closest to?
						65% (buscar en el tauler segons 12 rep.)
						12 reps equates to 67% of her one rep max (closest to 65%)
					2nd.Based on this value (20, 12rep → 65%), what is her estimated one rep max for biceps curls?
						30(ara es cuan gastem el pes)
						To find the estimated one rep max 65% of her one rep max = 200.65 x 1RM = 20 1RM = 20/ 0.65 = ~30
					3rd.If Marilyn’s goals for training are “muscle tone and endurance” what resistance intensity should the trainer have her start with?
						15(el més prop en el tauler per a 65% ¿?)
						For muscle endurance, initial intensity should be 50% of 1 RM = 15
	---------------------------------------
	Specific Approaches
		Manual Resistance
			Any form of active exercise in which a dynamic or static muscle contraction is resisted by an outside force
			An activity may be considered resistance exercise any time the muscles in the moving segment are challenged to a greater degree than usual.
			Resistance exercise may be
				Heavy or light weights 
				Elastic resistance 
				Movement against or even with gravity 
				Provided by the PT’s manual contacts 
				In many other forms
			-----------------------------------------
			Benefits
				Enhanced quality of life
				 
				Appropriately prescribed, may provide mechanical stresses that enhance healing
				Positive changes in body composition
				A decreased risk of injury
				An increase the strength of connective tissues
				 
				An increase in how muscles perform
				An increase in function
				Improved balance
		---------------------------------------
		Manual Resistance Excises (MREs)
			The resistance to the patient is provided by the PT.
			PT. opposes the patient’s muscle contraction using his or her upper extremities and/or body weight
			The manner in which these are used can vary greatly including:
				Isometrically, as is often done during a manual muscle test (MMT)
				Concentrically, where the PT permits the patient to overcome the manual resistance
				Eccentrically, where the PT uses enough force to overcome that provided by the patient 
				In straight or diagonal planes
				Many PNF (proprioceptive neuromuscular facilitation) approaches are a variation of manual resistance in a 
					diagonal plane
					In one or opposite directions
						i.e., resistance to the agonist muscle in one direction followed by resistance to the antagonist muscle in the other direction.
			------------------------------------------
			ADVANTAGES
				The ability for the PT to spontaneously vary the resistance which may be beneficial to: 
					Apply less when the patient becomes fatigued and allows performance of more reps
					Change resistance to address weak & strong points in the range
					Immediately reduce resistance in response to pain or discomfort
					Fine control over joint instability for patients with unstable joints 
				MRE’s are especially beneficial in the early stages of rehab for these reasons

			DISADVANTAGES
				It is very difficult to quantify the resistance provided
					Typically, resistance will be generally documented as minimal, moderate, heavy, or maximal 
				Maximum resistance is obviously limited by the PT’s strength and body type
					But – giving yourself the best leverage can considerably increase this. 
					Note the following example where a smaller and lighter individual uses leverage to provide effective resistance over a larger patient.
			------------------------------------------
			Muscle Endurance VS Muscle Power
				Muscle Endurance
					Performance of low intensity activity over prolonged time.
					Muscle endurance may involve
						Repeated contraction against a load
						Sustained muscle tension over time
						Decreased muscle fatigue during activity
						----------------------------------------
						-Increased muscle strength does not ensure muscle endurance
						-Endurance is typically associated with aerobic power and better delivery and use of oxygen and there is no guarantee that carryover to endurance will occur. 
						-A worker who has muscle strength but lacks muscle endurance can lift heavy loads but is more likely to injure him/herself as fatigue sets in.
				Muscle Power
					Rate at which muscles perform work → (Force x Distance)/time
					Considers both the ability to move a load (strength) and the speed of the movement while performing an activity
					This may be expressed in single or repeated bursts of movement i.e: 
						Power would allow an individual to propel him/herself up one stair more quickly (singly burst) 
						Or climb a set of stairs more quickly (repeated bursts)
					As humans age, they tend to lose power at a faster rate than they lose strength.
						I.e.: as your patient loses power, she may still be able to stand from a chair but it would take her longer to do it.
			---------------------------------------------------
			Basic Principles of Resistance Training
				Overload
					• Overload occurs when a Muscle is challenged to perform at "higher than usual" level
					• Involves use of more resources
					• Achieved via manipulation of training parameters
					- Intensity
					- Volume
					- Duration of contraction
					• Overload is relative to one's situation
				Specificity
					• Changes that result from training are highly specific to the training methods used
					• Achieving specificity involves consideration of:
					- Mode of exercise
					- Velocity
					- Position
					- Movement pattern
				Carryover/transfer function
					• Considers how much the exercise will translate into improved functional performance
					• Carryover sometimes possible even when specificity is limited
					- Trained limb → Non-trained limb
					- Strength training → Endurance gains
					- Carryover within velocities
					NOT ALL IS LOST IF NOT PERFECTLY SPECIFIC
				Limiting Reversibility/Detraining
					• Reversal of physiological adaptations to exercise
					• 1-2 weeks after cessation
					• Prevention of detraining
					STRATEGY 1
					- Incorporate exercise into daily activity. E.g.: sit to stand during function (repeated, extra concentration on form)
					• Maintenance Program
					. 2x/week?
			---------------------------------------------------
			Training Parameters
				See "Elements of Resistance Exercise Programs"
			---------------------------------------------------
			Training Goals
				For strength and hypertrophy:
					Higher intensities (weight) promote greater tissue changes
					Frequency should be less ⇊ often to allow recovery between sessions
					Durations should be shorter ⇊ because they are more intense
					Lower combinations of sets and reps (low volume) are more appropriate at higher intensities
				For muscle endurance:
					Low to moderate Intensity
					Frequency and Duration can be higher/longer because muscle groups will be less fatigued 
					Higher volumes will allow for performance of more reps during training sessions
				-----------------------------
				Overview of when to use High VS Low Intensities in Resistance Training in physical therapy practice
					Moderate to Low Intensities
						Involve Sub-maximal loading of muscles
						When?
							1. Towards the beginning of training program
							2. In early stages of tissue healing
							3. After immobilization
							4. Children and older adults
							5. The goal is muscular endurance
							6. For a warm-up or cool down
					High intensities
						Involve maximal or Near-Max Loading of muscles
						When?
							1. With (for who?) healthy adults in final stages of rehab
							2. Individuals with Ø known pathology
							3. For muscle strength and hypertrophy
							4. With competitive athletes
			--------------------------------------------------------------------------
			Appropriate Use of Types of Contractions in Training
				-The use of the different types of muscle contractions elicited during various forms of resistance exercise can be useful for achieving different training goals under different circumstances
				-This is especially true when designing rehabilitation programs for patients receiving physical therapy. 
				--------------------------------
				ISOMETRIC
					There are various forms of isometric exercises that may be implemented in different situations to address various goals.
						Muscle Setting
							-Involves simply tightening muscle against no formal resistance. Setting exercises are considered low intensity exercises for the following purposes.
								Promote relaxation and circulation
								Decrease pain and spasm
								Acute stage fluid mobilization
								Maintenance of fiber mobility during healing
								Prevent or limit atrophy
								Strengthening very weak muscles
						Stabilization Exercises
							Sub-maximal and sustain sustained co-contraction of muscles surrounding a joint or body region.
							Commonly performed with the patient holding a body segment in the middle of their available range against outside resistance
							Mostly commonly this occurs in weight bearing postures
							Purposes for performing stabilization exercises include
								Reduce instability in joints where it is present 
								Enhance postural stability in positions where this is challenged
							Many postural and core stability exercises are a form of stabilization
						Multiple Angle Isometrics
							Occurs when resistance is applied isometrically at varying joint angles within a patient’s available ROM
							These may be performed using manual or mechanical isometric resistance.
							I.e.:
								In the diagram below, the patient would push into the wall with his shoulder in abduction angles of 180° (A), 120° (B), and 90°(C)
							-----------------------------------------
							Multiple angle isometrics should be considered when the patient has significant ROM, but dynamic resistance is contraindicated and/or painful. Remember, isometric training only builds strength within about 10 degrees of the angle at which they are performed. For this reason, whenever possible, training at four to six different angles in the range is recommended. Even while doing this, carry over into dynamic strength is limited.
						---------------------------------------
						Isometric Resistance Exercises
							Involve activities that involve no noticeable change in muscle length and no visible joint motion
								1. Strengthening in initial rehab stages to:
									- Address weakness in individuals lacking significant AROM
									- Prevent or minimize atrophy
									- Elicit muscle contraction w/o joint movement
								2. Static strength and endurance for function (point of view of functional activities ¿?)
									- Posture
									- Low level agonist & antagonist co-contraction
									-- Joint stability
									- Develop strength at specific joint angles
							--------------------------
							Examples of how to perform isometric exercises include:
								1. Hold against manual resistance
								2. Hold load in anti-gravity position
								3. Maintain position against body weight
								4. Push/pull immovable object
							-----------------------------------------
							Considerations (precautions) For Safe Performance
								The patient should apply & release resistance gradually
									This will prevent generating too much tension too quickly in healing tissues and minimizes uncontrolled joint sheering forces.
								Avoid holding of breath and inducing a "Valsalva maneuver"
									These build up pressure on cardiovascular structures within the thoracic cavity which can be dangerous for many patients.
									Patients should be instructed to "breathe out lightly during each repetition" AKA: "Blow out the birthday candles".
								Closely monitor patients with cardiac issues during isometrics
									Intrathoracic pressure is especially dangerous for these individuals
							-----------------------------------------
							Case
								Consider the following patient and determine if he could benefit from multiple angle isometrics:
									A 44-year-old patient had rotator cuff surgery three weeks ago. The prescription for PT from the surgeon says, “ROM exercises as tolerated and isometric strengthening, but no concentric exercises”.
									Would this approach work for him?
										Yes, because AROM is painful and likely not tolerated yet. PROM will not increase muscle activity. Concentric exercises are contraindicated at this point as, in the surgeon’s opinion, they will place too much stress on healing muscle tissue. While not as effective as concentric strengthening, isometrics at multiple angles will produce more functional carry over than at limited angles.
				CONCENTRIC
					Concentric contractions occur when the muscle shortens and produces enough force to overcome the load against it.
				ECCENTRIC
					Eccentric contractions are produced when the muscle attempts to shorten, but is lengthened because the load overcomes the amount of force generated by the muscle.
				-------------------------------------------------------------------
				Dynamic Resistance Training
					Involves strengthening exercises that involve joint movement
					These exercises may elicit concentric or eccentric contractions.
					---------------------------------
					Characteristics Of Concentric and Eccentric Activities
						Concentric Activities
							Produce contraction and shortening ofthe target muscle
							Create movement of body segments in the direction of the primary action ofthe muscle being activated
							The extemal resistance opposing the muscle action is overcome
						Eccentric Activities
							The muscle lengthens under tension
							Create deceleration of body segments moving in the opposite direction of the action ofthe muscle being activated
							The load overcomes the force produced by the muscle which is often activating in an attempt to control the load
						Considerations for Safe Performance of Isometric Exercises
							The patient should apply & release resistance gradually
								This will prevent generating too much tension too quickly in healing tissues and minimizes uncontrolled jointsheering forces
							Avoid holding of breath and inducing a "Valsalva maneuver"
								These build up pressure on cardiovascular structures within the thoracic cavity which can be dangerous for many patients.
								Patients should be instructed to "breathe out lightly during each repetition" AKA: "Blow out the birthday candles"
							Closely monitor patients with cardiac issues during isometrics
								Intrathoracic pressure is especially dangerous for these individuals
						----------------------------------------------------
						If the patient is permitted to perform both, what would be the Rationale for selecting Concentric or Eccentric Exercises?
							Most commonly guided by muscle capacity & functional needs of patient
							Common considerations:
								Do the activities the patient needs to perform involve concentric, eccentric muscle activity or both?
								Is soft tissue in an early state of healing?
								Is the goal prevention of injury or re-injury
								Stage of rehab
			-------------------------------------------------------------------
			Constant VS Variable Resistance
				Constant Resistance
					-When the amount of resistance provided against the contracting muscle stays the same throughout the range of the movement.
					-With this type of resistance, challenge to the contracting muscle changes at various parts of the range, being easier at some portions of the range and more difficult at others.
					-----------------------------------
					I.e.:
						-When performing a biceps curl with heavy resistance the muscle is typically challenged to a greater degree during the early and mid-parts of the curl when the movement is against gravity to a greater degree, but less towards the end of the range.
						-Free weights and pulley systems are examples of constant resistance.
				Variable Resistance
					When the amount of resistance provided against the contracting muscle changes throughout the range of the movement.
					This is designed to keep the challenge to the contracting muscle similar during the entire movement range.
					-----------------------------------
					I.e.:
						-Weight machines with irregularly shaped cam or pulley systems that require greater force at certain angles.
						-Manual resistance is one example.
						-Some forms of elastic resistance that increase as the device achieves greater elongation.
		Mechanical Resistance Exercises
			Involves the use of specific equipment or an apparatus to oppose muscle contraction and may be imposed in a constant or variable load.
			It is easy to accurately quantify the resistance provided and very high levels of  maximum resistance are possible.
			However, for some forms of mechanical resistance there may be limited carryover into function due to lack of specificity
			access to equipment is not always convenient.
			Common forms of Mechanical Resistance we will examine include:
				Free weights and pulley systems
					Provide a constant load.
					As with all forms of constant resistance, the maximum muscle tension is only produced at certain points in range. But this may sometimes be varied by changing body position.
					--------------------------------------------------
					Advantages
						Versatility - many different types of exercises may be performed with one piece of equipment
						Exercises using the equipment may be performed in multiple positions
						Dynamic or isometric and sometimes eccentric contractions are possible
						They allow for single or multi-planar movements
						Resistance may be increased in small increments 
					Disadvantages
						Most exercises must be performed slowly 
						A spotter is needed for high loads 
						Carryover into function may be limited due to joint angle considerations
				Plate-Loaded Resistance Machines
					Depending upon the specific machine they may provide constant or variable resistance due to the presence of an elliptical-shaped cam or pulley.
					---------------------------------------------------
					Advantages
						If capable of variable resistance, the user can achieve maximum muscle tension through the entire range 
						Very efficient isolation of single muscle groups 
						Provide substantial external stabilization – which is often beneficial for patients with back, neck, or shoulder pathology 
						Easy to learn and use 
					Disadvantages
						Most machines only allow for single plane movement 
						Must be performed at a slow speed 
						Expensive 
						Often limited functional carryover
				Elastic resistance
					Very commonly used form of resistance in rehabilitation settings due to its convenience and low cost. 
					Examples include elastic bands (TherabandTM), sports cords, surgical tubing, etc.
					Elastic resistance is typically variable with resistance increasing with elongation.
					It provides the maximum amount of resisted force when angled 90 degrees to moving bone at the beginning of the motion.
					Differing levels of resistance are possible through varied elastic thicknesses that are coded by color.
					---------------------------------------------------
					Advantages
						Versatile, cheap, portable, and practical 
						Safe at high speeds 
					Disadvantages
						Can’t quantify resistance exactly 
						Wear & tear is very common
				Isokinetic equipment
					-Type of resistance training aspects that are very different than the other forms of mechanical resistance. This is because it is a form of resistance in which velocity at which the muscle shortens is held constant. 
					-When it first came out, Isokinetic resistance exercise was considered a superior method of training. Subsequent research has shown it is not necessarily better, just different. 
					-“Iso-kinetic” loosely translates into “same speed of movement” and is quantified in degrees/second.
					-A maximum effort results in a maximum force output that is consistent thru ROM rather than at certain angles as with other forms of mechanical resistance. Commercial product names include:  Biodex, Cybex, Kincom.
					--------------------------------------------------
					Advantages
						The ability to train at speeds specific to desired function 
							If an athlete or patient performs a certain task at a certain speed, training the muscles at that speed may aid with functional carryover 
						Reciprocal training in two forms is possible 
							Agonist/antagonist involves providing resistance to one muscle group in one direction of the range and the opposite group in the other direction 
							I.e./E.G.; Knee extension followed by knee flexion 
						Concentric/eccentric training provides resistance that may be overcome using a concentric contraction followed by resistance that may not be overcome that moves the body segment back to its original position while the muscle contracts ¿?
						Fewer compressive joint forces than with slow velocity training ¿?
						The ability to perform additional reps after fatigue 
						The opportunity to avoid painful points in the arc in motion by limiting the range 
						Quantifiable printouts of force output
					Disadvantages
						Access to equipment is difficult because it is both expensive and uncommonly owned 
						The patient tends to need much assist for set up and use 
						Some limits in functional carryover due to: 
							Lack of ability to reach very high speeds 
							Uni-planar movement 
							Open chain motion 
							The fact that functional movements tend to occur at multiple and varying not constant velocities
		Body Weight
			-Many types of closed-chain exercises utilize this approach where the individual must move or hold his/her body or a portion of it against the force of gravity. 
			-Classic examples include isometric holds or concentric movements in and out of the position such as: 
				The squat
				Plank position
				Push ups
				Bridging
		Plyometrics
			Involve producing a high velocity eccentric contraction that is immediately followed by a concentric loading of the same muscles or muscle groups. I.e.: jumping
			----------------------------------------------
			Plyometrics utilize neuromuscular reflex responses to maximize the training response.
			The tension produced during stretching that occurs during the eccentric contraction prepares the muscle for more efficient concentric contraction. This is based on activation of the monosynaptic stretch reflex.
			When muscle tissue is placed on quick stretch, sensory organs within, known as the golgi tendon organ and muscle spindle provide immediate feedback to the spinal cord.
				This provokes the activation of motor neurons to cause a contraction of the muscle to prevent excess stretch that could cause damage. Plyometrics use this response to get a more efficient concentric response after eccentric loading.
			----------------------------------------------
			Plyometric activities may use various sources to load tissues in this manner i.e.: 
				Body weight loading
				Manual resistance
				Loading via external weight
			----------------------------------------------
			When to implement plyometric training?
				Due to stress on tissues and the need for some existing level of range of motion and strength, plyometrics are typically introduced in the later stages of rehab programs
				They are most appropriate for individuals that need to perform high-demand activities that are plyometric in nature
					Most sports involve these types of activities
						I.e.: running, jumping, blocking, cutting, etc.
					In general, the recommended prerequisite ability before implementing plyometrics after injury is when the patient has regained:
						80-85% of normal strength
						90-95% of normal ROM

SUB_HotCold - Preparing Structures & Tissues for Stretch
	Heat
		Superficial
			Hot pack (HP), parafin
		Deep
			Ultrasound
		Connective Tissue (CT) pliability
		Relaxation
		Pain and muscle guarding
	Cold/lce
		Muscle spasm
		Pain
		Spasticity/Rigidity
		To minimize potential inflammation after stretch

SUB_JointMobTech - "Joint Mobilization Assessment/Testing" & "Joint Mobilization Treatments/Techniques" [MSK1_V09] [MSK1_C2.6]
	-To assess how well a joint is moving
	-To determine the direction of the mobilizing force when joint mobilization assessment techniques are used to increase joint motion that is lacking
	-----------------------------------------
	When assessing joint’s mobility or trying to improve restricted joint motion, PTs must:
		-Should be done in an open-packed position to allow for the maximum ability for the joint to move. Otherwise, you may get an inaccurate assessment of joint’s potential for movement
		-the physical therapist introduces passive gliding movements between articular surfaces in the same manner they occur during normal, physiologic movement
			I.e.:
				-If the patient has limited shoulder abduction, the therapist should mobilize the humeral head inferiorly because that is how it moves during abduction – opposite to the distal humerus (superior)
					To mimic the joint mechanics that occur during this motion, a physical therapist should apply an external force to the humerus that causes it to glide in a downward direction
				-If the patient has limited shoulder flexion, he/she would mobilize the humeral head posteriorly because during flexion the distal humerus moves from the anatomic position into an anterior direction
		-------------------------------------
		Why do we pefform joint mobilization assessment and what are the benefits? 
			("02_09 Mod 1 Joint Mobilization 15.01.2024_12.36.46_REC.mp4")
			--------------------------------------------------------------
			SUB_ComprDistract - To see if it induces the patient’s familiar symptoms
				-Joint Compression (açò és una tècnica per se. Mirar si fem una nova secció per a ella ¿?)
					PT pulls joint surfaces close one another (it is the approximation of joint surfaces)
					May occur as a result of:
						Weight bearing
						Muscle contraction
						Rolling of joint surface during normal movement
				-Joint Distraction/Traction
					PT pulls joint surfaces away from one another/involves applying force that separates joint surfaces
						-The pull is perpendicular to the treatment plane in a way that creates more space between articulating surfaces
					Indications
						1.SUB_ComprDistract_PainReductTech - It may be performed as a Pain Reduction Technique (pain relief)
							because it stimulates mechanoreceptors in the joint capsule and activates neural pathways known as a "gate control response" that temporarily reduce pain.
						As a means to warm up the tissue prior to joint mobilization assessment
					Contraindication ("02_12 Contraindications to Joint Mobilization assessment Traction 15.01.2024_14.09.22_REC.mp4")
						NOT to reduce joint restrictions, as evidence does not support that tissue stretch through traction will accomplish this
						----------------------------------- PREC_JointMob
						Inflammatory arthritis
							We're not talking about osteoarthritis, but variations called rheumatoid arthritis. This is because mobilization will typically increase severe inflammation
						Malignancy
							Gray area: but really anything that increases blood flow to an area is a reason to consider staying away from that type of treatment
						Bone Disease
							Specifically bone disease that decreases the strength of bones like osteoporosis
						Neurological Involvement
							We are not talking about any neurological condition but 
								When spasticity around the joint that prevents muscle relaxation and therefore moving the joint could damage tissue
						Fracture
							When fragments are not healed or are unstable
						Congenital Bone Deformities that prevent normal joint mechanics
						Vertebral Artery Compromise - cervical spine
							If we have blockage of the vertebral artery
								-We're only talking about for mobilization of the cervical spine because this can further impede blood flow to the brain
						Hypermobility
							-Is contraindicated because this would mean the patient already has joints that move too much, therefore creating more movement would possibly do no good and some harm
						Connective Tissue Disorders
							If they have more fragile connective tissue that is more easily damaged
					---------------------------------------
					Kaltenborn’s Traction Grades ("Kaltenborn's Traction Grades.png") (!!E.Grade & Doc).Kin
						Describe the system for rating force applied during this intervention
							Grade I Traction
								(Γ slack)
								-Distraction of joint surfaces at the beginning of range with a significant amount of slack remaining
							Grade II Traction
								(Γ no slack)
								Takes up all slack to the limits of joint play and distraction motion
								AKA to the “point of limitation”
							Grade III Traction 
								(Γ beyond slack)
								Stretching of soft tissue starting from the point of limitation and moving BEYOND
								AKA to the “anatomic limit”
			2.Reduce muscle guarding
				because it can also stimulate the Golgi tendon organ in a way that promotes muscle relaxation and helps to interrupt the pain spasm, pain cycle.
			3.Facilitate Healing
				on a subtle level, can facilitate healing, and it can by
					-increasing circulation
					-normalizing joint nutrition by causing an exchange of fluids between cartilage and the synovial fluid,
					increasing joint water content,
					-by creating therapeutic stresses on connective tissue to help lay down collagen in a productive fashion
			4.To PREVENT joint restrictions from occurring
				I.e.: when joint tissue is immobilized or injured, it gets weaker and experiences negative changes; mobilizations will strengthen it. Often, this will occur after surgery and doing the joint mobilization assessment, will maintain distance, lubrication, and mobility between a collagen fibers and provide low levels of stress that reduces the formation of cross-links and attachments between fibers. It can also prevent atrophy of the collagen tissue and provide delivery of nutrients to overall maintain the health of the joint
			5.When joint mobility assessment determines a restriction is present
				Use to reduce restriction preventing normal movement
		Development of Joint Restrictions
			Occur due to lack of stress & movement
				this is in line with the concept of "physical stress theory"
			Possible Sources:
				Immobilization
				Trauma
				Weakness
				Postural considerations
				Chronic Inflammation
			Lack of stress = "adaptive shortening" of surrounding tissues. What tissues?
				Capsule
				Ligaments
				Fascia
	-----------------------------------------
	What Happens at the Joint when we Perform Joint Mobilization assessment?
		The goal is to enhance the ability for accessory motion to occur during normal, physiologic motion.
		-------------------------------------------------------
		Types of Joint Motion (Physiologic vs. Accessory Motion)
			Physiologic/Physiological motion (AKA Osteokinematics)
				Movement of the limbs or trunk in one of three cardinal planes, each occurring around an axis of rotation
				Results from muscle contraction that move a bone or joint
				"Visible" motion
				ROM observed on exam
				Examples include: Flexion, extension, abduction, etc.
				----------------------------------------------------
					I.e.:
						Descriptors i.e.: internal rotation or extension
						Describes the rotational movement of one bone on another
						Results from muscle contractions that move a bone or joint
						"Visible" motion
						Performed voluntarily by patient; ROM observed on exam
			Accessory motion (AKA arthrokinematics)
				Movements that occur between the articular surfaces of joints
				Results from muscle contraction that move a bone or joint
				"Visible" motion
				ROM observed on exam
				Examples include: Flexion, extension, abduction, etc.
				-------------------------------------------------
					I.e.:
						Descriptors i.e. roll, slide, spin
						Describes the gliding motions of the joint surfaces that may accompany the joint rotations
						Movement of joint surfaces relative to one another. Occurs simultaneously with physiologic movement
						Not visible/occurs inside joint
						Cannot be performed independently by patient; must be assessed by PT (i.e. Joint mobility assessment)
		-----------------------------------
		Concave/Convex Rule See "Kin Normal Ranges.txt - Arthrokinematics - Roll & Glide (Biomechanics)"
			-The accessory motion created during joint mobilization assessment is produced in a manner consistent with the concave-convex rule.
			-Used to determine the direction in which joint surfaces move on one another (accessory motion)
			-The direction is described in reference to the anatomical position.
				-When a convex surface moves on a concave surface, the glide of the convex surface is in the OPPOSITE direction of the movement of the bony lever (during physiologic movement).
				-When a concave surface moves on a convex surface, the glide of the concave (Γcon=concurrent) surface is in the SAME direction as the movement of the bony lever (during physiologic movement).
				---------------------------------------------------------
				("02_10 The Convex Concave Rule in under 2 min.mp4" & "RollGlide.png")
					Convex surface
						When the convex surface is moving:
							The joint surface glides in the direction opposite the swing of bone
					Roll:
						Typically occurs in the same direction as the swing of the bone (angular displacement)
					Concave surface
						When the concave surface is moving:
							The joint surface glides in the same direction as the swing of bone
		-----------------------------------
		Closed-Packed Position vs. Open-Packed Position [O/C-PP LQ::Hip (Revisar tot açò::Kin Normal Ranges.txt]
			Closed-Packed Position
				Point in ROM where surfaces are maximally congruent
				Capsule and ligaments are maximally elongated (under tension)
				Surfaces maximally compressed
			Open-Packed Position
				Point in ROM where the articular surfaces minimally congruent
				Capsule and ligaments are slack
				Joint may be distracted
	Performing Joint Mobilization Assessment
		Effective performance of a joint mobilization assessment requires consideration of:
			The treatment plane ("treatment plane.png")
				It is a line perpendicular from the axis of rotation to the opposite articulating surface
				The direction of joint mobilization assessmentusually occurs parallel to the treatment plane
			Direction of Joint Mobilization assessment
				The specific direction that manual force is applied along the treatment plane is dependent upon:
					-The biomechanical relationships of the joint surfaces
					-The concave/convex rule
					-This direction is typically in an anterior, posterior, superior, inferior or sometimes transverse direction
			Start position of the joint
				-Effective joint mobilization assessment requires placement of the joint in an optimal position at the start of the movement
					-Initial joint mobilization assessment is typically performed in loose-packed AKA open-packed positions
					-Remember, the loose packed position is the position in which surrounding soft-tissues are on slack and joint surfaces are free to move
						This allows one to maximize joint play
							-As the patient gains range due to lessened restrictions, the PT may, and often should, progress to less mobile positions
							-Knowledge of the closed and loose-packed positions of various joints is therefore very helpful.
							-For example, the following are loose packed positions for the:
								-Shoulder: ~30 degrees of ABD and horizontal adduction
								-Ankle: Neutral between zero degrees of plantar and dorsiflexion
			Intensity and other treatment parameters
				("02_11 Parameters and Intensity of Joint Mobilizations 15.01.2024_13.26.34_REC.mp4")
			------------------------------------------------------------------------------- [MSK1_Imm2A_JointMobTreat]
			In performing each procedure, you should first determine the 
			    -available arthrokinematic range of the periarticular inert tissues to (See [MSK1_Imm2A_JointMobAss] Es açò a lo que esreferix ¿?)
			        classify whether the joint is
			            normal
			            hypomobile
			            hypermobile.
			    Next, you should perform the distraction and glide procedures listed keeping in mind both:
				    The movement of the bony surfaces associated with the mobilization procedure
				        Distraction/Separation
			                Separation of two joint surfaces about their longitudinal axis
			            Glide
			                -While one bony surface is stabilized, the other surface is moved parallel to the stabilized bones articular surface until inert tissue slack is taken up and stretched in either a continuous manner or through the use of oscillations
				--------------------------- [MSK1_Imm2A_JointMobTreat] [MSK1_ExamImm2A]
				Intensity (these are not for Assessment but for Treatment !!) (Ésta secció deu estar mal ací ¿?)
					.How much Force should be used? (!!E.Grade & Doc).Kin
						.Intensity and force applied are classified by Maitland's Grades of Joint Mobilization assessment
							(!!E.Grade & Doc).Kin
							REF_MaitlandGrades
							("Grades of Joint Mobilization.png")
							For pain modulation
								Grade I (Γ)(GM.I)
									Small amplitude motion "at beginning" of available range
								Grade II (Γ)(GM.II)
									Large amplitude motion "within available" range but not reaching limit of the range
							For increasing inert tissue mobility through stretching at the end of available range
								Grade III (Γ)(GM.III)
									Large amplitude motion performed "up to" the limit of range
								Grade IV (Γ)(GM.IV)
									Small amplitude movement performed "at limit" of range
									Amall amplitude movement up to and slightly beyond range to stretch tissues
							Grade V (Γ)(GM.V)
								Manipulation (this is something different from the others mentioned here)
								Small amplitude, high velocity thrust performed "at limit" of range
									We are not necessarily pushing hard but quicker at the end range
							-----------------------
							Grade 1 – Small oscillation at beginning of available range
							Grade 2 – Large oscillation within available range (without reaching the end)
							Grade 3 – Large oscillation up to end of available range
							Grade 4 – Small oscillation performed AT end of available range and slightly past
							-----------------------
							Mnemonic
								Small, Large, Large, Small
					.When to use more/less forces? (In general) (!!E.Grade & Doc).Kin
						Grade I & II
							We are not doing any sort of elongation of the joint capsule
							-These are used to stimulate a pain control response or to place a very low level of stress on the tissue for the purpose of stimulating healing through increases in blood flow, movement of fluid, and activation of healing cells
							------------------------------------------
							Rx of pain & spasm (mechanoreceptors, gate)
							Facilitate Healing?
						Grade III & IV
							-Performed when we do want some stretch on the joint capsule as is needed when there is suspected adaptive shortening of the tissue
								-Depending how much stretch you want and how much stretch the patient tolerates, you would choose either a Grade III or a Grade IV; to be more aggressive 
							------------------------------------------
							Limitation of motion, stiffness
							Facilitate Healing
				Parameters (i.e: How many bouts of joint mobs should be performed?)
					Minimal guidelines in literature about
						how long perform joint mobs or
						how many times you should do these within a treatment session
					General recommendations:
						3-6 sets of oscillations
						Each set lasting 20-60 seconds
						------------------------------
						Perform a joint mobilization assessment for a short period of time,
							take a break, and then,
								see how the patientls feeling before starting again.
									-And then, you would reassess how the patient felt after each treatment session before you begin a joint mobilization assessment treatment
					Based on patient tolerance
						The real-world answer is it depends entirely on the individual patient and how they tolerate the procedure.
	Joint Mobilization Technique Practice  & ("SS4 -CNVS Jt Mobs 1.18.24.pptx") & [MSK04.mp4]
		Practice Technique 1 – PROM of MCP joint of 2nd digit [01:29:00sec]
			Use opposite hand to passively extend the MCP joint
				WITHOUT hitting tissue resistance
				To the point where you very first feel resistance of the tissue (R1)
				To limit of tissue resistance (R2)
					Oscillate back and forth to this point
					Like grade 3
				Hold at limit of tissue resistance and oscillate slightly PAST this point
					Each time return only to (R2)
		Practice Technique 2 – Using a Tennis Ball [01:32:00sec]
			Apply pressure until you cannot compress the tennis ball any further
				First using the Base of Palm
				Then with Thumbs
				Then just above the Pisiform (like PA glide)
				Use this as frame of reference for all mobs

	Mobilization Procedures [MSK1_MobilProc] (See Folder: "Mobilization Procedures" & [MSK_V01_08]) [MSK1_ExamImm2A]
		Glenohumeral Joint
		    Loose pack position
		        50° abducted,
		        30° horizontally adducted, and
		        slightly externally rotated)
		    ---------------------------------------
		    1.Distraction ("GlenoHumeral Distraction.png")
		        a.Position of patient
		            Supine with arm in loose pack position, support the forearm between your trunk and elbow.
		        b.Hand Placement
		            -Use hand nearest the part being treated and place it in the patient's axilla with your thumb just distal to the joint margin anteriorly and fingers posteriorly. Your other hand supports the humerus from the lateral surface.
		        c.Mobilizing Force
		            Using the hand in the axilla, move the humerus laterally.
		    2.Caudal Glide ("GlenoHumeral Caudal Glide.png")
		        a.Position of patient
		            Same as for distraction
		        b.Hand Placement
		            -Place one hand to support the distal end of the humerus: the web space of your other hand is placed just distal to the acromium process.
		        c.Mobilizing Force
		            With the superiorly placed hand, glide the humerus in an inferior direction
		    3.Posterior Glide (down when pt. in supine) ("GlenoHumeral Posterior Glide.png")
		    	Convex (humeral head) moving on concave (glenoid fossa); opposate movement
		    		Posterior Glide, Anterior Roll = arm Flexion
		    		Anteromedial glide, Posterolateral roll = arm Horizontal Adduction
		    	Open-Packed Position
		    		55° abduction, 30° horizontal adduction, slightly externally rotated
		    	---------------------
		        a.Position of patient
		            Same as for distraction
		        b.Therapist and Hand Placement
		        	-Scapula off of the table a little, and we want to stabilize it 
		            -Stand with your back to the patient, between the patient's trunk and arm. Support the arm against your trunk, grasping the distal humerus with your lateral hand. Place the lateral inner border (Γ tipo tall carate) of your top hand (one closest to the patient) just distal to the anterior margin of the joint (over the head of the humerus; locate acromion), with your fingers pointing superiorly. This hand provides the mobilization force.
		        c.Mobilizing Force
		            Glide the humeral head in a posterior direction by moving the entire arm as you bend your knees
		            	-Because of the plane of the scapula (not perpendicular to the floor when pt. in supine but at 30°), if you go posterior you will bump onto the back of the glenoid surface. So, go posterior but a little bit lateral (so, do we go traction to go lateral? ¿?). For treatment, we can perform traction, but for assessment Dr. Lebec says no since this create different forces on the capsule.
		            If the joint move too much, ask for feedback from the pt.

		Radiocarpal Joint of the Wrist
		    Loose pack position
		        slight extension & ulnar deviation
		    -------------------------------------- 
			1.Distraction ("Radiocarpal Distraction.png")
			    a.Position of Patient
			        Sitting with the forearm supported on the treatment table and the wrist over the edge of the table
			    b.Hand Placement
			        -With the hand closest to the patient, grasp around the styloid processes and fixate the radius and ulna against the table. The other hand grasps around the distal row of carpals.
			    c.Mobilizing Force
			        Pull in a distal direction with respect to the forearm.
			2.Volar Glide ("Radiocarpal Volar Glide.png")
			    a.Position of Patient
			        Sitting with the forearm supported on the treatment table and the wrist over the edge of the table
			    b.Hand Placement
			        -With the hand closest to the patient, grasp around the styloid processes and fixate the radius and ulna against the table. The other hand grasps around the distal row of carpals.
			    c.Mobilizing Force
			        The wrist and hand is glided in a volar direction (towards the floor)
			3.Dorsal Glide ("Radiocarpal Dorsal Glide.png")
			    a.Position of Patient
			        Sitting with the forearm supported on the treatment table and the wrist over the edge of the table
			    b.Hand Placement
			        -With the hand closest to the patient, grasp around the styloid processes and fixate the radius and ulna against the table. The other hand grasps around the distal row of carpals.
			    c.Mobilizing Force
			        The wrist and hand is glided in a dorsal direction (towards the ceiling)

		Talocrural Joint
		    Loose pack position
		        plantarflexed 10 degs
		    -------------------------
		    1.Distraction ("Talocrural Distraction.png")
		        a.Position of patient
		            Supine with the lower extremity extended and the ankle in loose pack position
		        b.Position of Therapist and Patient
		            -Stand at the end of the table and wrap the fingers of both hands over the dorsum of the foot just distal to the mortise.  Place your thumbs on the plantar surface of the foot to maintain the loose pack position
		        c.Mobilization Force
		            Pull the foot away from the long axis of the leg in a distal direction by leaning backward.
		    2.Dorsal (posterior) Glide ("Talocrural Dorsal posterior Glide.png")
		    	Convex (Talus) moving on concave (Tibia and Fibula); opposate movement
		    		Plantarflexion - posterior roll, anterior glide
					Dorsiflexion - anterior roll, posterior glide
		    	Open-Packed Position
					Neutral between 0° of
						plantar & 
						dorsiflexion
					From MSK1: 15°-20° of plantarflexion
		    	---------------------
		        a.Position of patient
		            Supine with the lower extremity extended and the ankle in loose pack position; out of the table
		        b.Position of Therapist and Patient
		            -Stand to the side of the patient and stabilize the leg with your superiorly positioned hand or use a belt to secure the leg to the table. Place the palmer aspect of the web space of your other hand over the talus just distal to the mortise. Wrap your fingers and thumb around the foot to maintain the ankle in resting position.
		            -From Dr. Lebec: Use your thigh to stabilize the foot at the plant. Γ Grab the talus distal to the joint; stabilize above. Arm straight; do not use triceps force to move the joint. Whatch out for attack position of the legs
		            -About positionin the table in the correct high: "If you find yourselfe on the toes, down, down, the table goes"
		        c.Mobilization Force
		            Glide the talus posteriorly (toward the floor) with respect to the tibia by pushing against the talus.
		    3.Ventral (anterior) Glide ("Talocrural Ventral anterior Glide.png")
		        a.Position of Patient
		            Prone with the foot over the edge of the table
		        b.Position of the Therapist and Patient
		            -Working from the end of the table, place your lateral hand across the dorsum of the foot.  Place the web space of the other hand just distal to the mortise on the posterior aspect of the talus and calcaneus.
		        c.Mobilizing Force
		            -Push against the calcaneus in an anterior direction with respect to the tibia (toward the floor) to glide the talus anteriorly.

SUB_KineticChain
	PT use kinetic chain exercises to help with injury prevention and recovery
	Types
		Open Kinetic Chain Exercises, AKA Opened-Kinetic Chain Exercises
			One end of the linkage system is fixed or stabilized, and the other end is free to move independently
				Segment furthest away from the body is free and not fixed to an object but loaded
			Joints can move in isolation and typically move independently of one another
			Typically non-weight Bearing Exercises
			Movement occurs at single joint
			Best way to isolate a single muscle or muscle group
			Greater movement control
		Close Kinetic Chain Exercises, AKA Closed-Kinetic Chain Exercises
			Both ends of the linkage system are fixed (usually the body and ground)
				Segment furthest away from the body (distal segment) is fixed, or stationary
			The body moving on “fixed” or stabilized distal segment
			Resistance that is applied to multiple segments simultaneously
			Movement of the levers are dependent on the movement of all other levers in the system
			Typically weight-bearing exercises
			Movement occurs at multiple joints
			Joints move interdependently
			Loading may be proximal or distal
			To facilitate co-contraction of muscles around a joint
				Is the simultaneous contraction of agonist and antagonist muscles crossing a joint
					I.e.: contraction of both quadriceps and hamstring muscles during functional activity
				Beneficial for a patient experiencing instability
			Integration of neuromuscular control mechanisms
				Causes compression of joint surfaces at multiple joints
					Activation of more sensory receptors
					Proprioceptors
					Mechanoreceptors
					Proprioception & balance benefits
			Approximation of joint surfaces
			It is bringing joint surfaces together using compressive forces
				Results in increased joint congruency between joint surfaces and therefore:
					Greater stability
					Lower sheer forces
				Implications for neuromuscular approximation via manual contacts rehab ¿?
				Approximation that activates joint receptors also results in an anti-gravity response in surrounding muscles
				Watch the following video for an example of this

SUB_1RM - One-Rep Max
	Choose an exercise to test (squat, bench press, etc.) and
	warm up with light cardio activity and dynamic stretching for at least 15 to 30 minutes. 
	Then follow the steps below.
	----------------------
	Steps for 1RM Testing
	1. Performance of 10 reps using a weight that's about half of an estimation of the max resistance.
	2. Rest for at least one to 2 min.
	3. Increase the weight up to 70% of an estimation of the max resistance.
	4. Do 3 reps, then rest for at least 1 min.
	5. Add weight in approximately 10% increments and attempt a single rep each time, resting for at least 1 to 2 minutes in between each attempt.
	6. The maximum weight the individual can successfully lift, with good form and technique, is the 1RM.

SUB_10RM - Ten-Rep Max
	10RM Test
		Choose exercise to test (squat, bench press, etc.) and warm up with light cardio activity and dynamic stretching for at least 15 to 30 minutes. Then follow the steps below.
		------------------------------------
		1. Start at or below perceived 10RM
		2. Instruct the participant to do 11 reps
		3. If 11 reps are performed, then a 2 min. rest is taken, and a conservative increase in weight is made before making another attempt.
			- 5-15 lb. (2.3-6.8 Kg) increase for upper body
			- 15-25 lb. (6.8-11.4 Kg) increase for lower body
		4. If less than 10 reps are performed, then a 2 min. rest is taken, and a conservative decrease in weight is made before making another attempt
			- 5-15 lb. decrease for upper body
			- 15-25 lb. decrease for lower body
		5. The final 10RM value is the last successful lift and should be found within 3-5 maximal efforts

SUB_5RM Five-Rep Max
	N/A

SUB_RepsToFat - Reps to Fatigue
	-It bases the initial intensity on a percentage of the maximum an individual can lift one time
	-Safest way to determine this is using an estimated one-rep max test – not actually having the patient attempt one repetition at a possible maximum resistance
	-This involves having the individual perform as many total repetitions as possible at a known and safe intensity and estimating the 1 rep max from this number
	-See "One-Rep Max estimation chart.png" chart
	-Once the percentage of One-Rep Max is known, the initial training intensity may be determined based on the training goals
		In general, individuals training for strength and hypertrophy should begin with 60-80% of the One-Rep Max.
		50% of the One-Rep Max should be used when training for endurance.
