////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Types of Tissues & Stretching of Ligaments and Tendons
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Structure of Connective Tissue
	In general, connective tissue consists of:
		Cells (specifically fibroblasts)
		Ground Substance
			A mixture of water and various organic molecules
			Presence of ground substance makes connective tissues stronger & more mobile
		Fibers
			Loose Connective Tissue: Elastin & reticulum
			Dense Connective Tissue: Collagen (makes up tendons, ligaments, & joint capsules)

Dense Connective Tissue
	Dense connective tissue is also referred to as dense fibrous tissue due to presence of abundant collagen fibers.
	There are five classifiable types of collagen:
		Type I, II & III most relevant to physical therapy practice ~
			Found in ligaments, tendons, capsules, & cartilage
		Types III–V
			Found in smooth muscle, internal organs, epithelial tissue, placental tissue
	---------------------------------------------
	Collagen Fibers in Tendons
		Are arranged in parallel
		Makes for good structural design for resisting tension
		Example: Transfer of tensile loads from muscle to bone
	Collagen Fibers in Ligaments
		Arranged in parallel
		Contain less densely packed fibers than tendon (structured to resist heavy tension)
		Connect bone to bone and therefore resists/restricts excessive joint motion

Tissue Changes
	.Elasticity Changes
		The ability for soft tissue to return to resting length after passive stretch
		Tissues experience temporary changes in length
		Tissue stretched to this extent will return to its pre-stretch length after a short-duration stretch
			Stretch→ Elongation→ Return to Previous Length
		Why do elastic changes happen?
			Straightening of crimped (rizado) tissue fibers
			Disruption of weak bonds between tissue fibers
			Elastic changes are reversible and temporary
			---------------------------------------------
			I. e.: A basic example of this is when you perform hamstring stretching exercises and are more flexible afterward, but the next day your hamstrings go back to their original level of flexibility
		Consistent stresses in elastic ranges may over time cause permanent (plastic) changes in length through collagen remodeling
	.Plasticity Changes
		The tendency for soft tissue to assume and retain greater length after stress is removed
		Stretch (strain)→ Elongation (strain)→ Retention of Greater Length
		Why do plastic changes occur?
			Collagen Remodeling
			Disruption of the tissue infrastructure
			Cause tissue to assume a permanent, new length immediately after being stretched
				May be micro-levels of damage to tissue fibers without major trauma
				Or larger levels of damage with some tissue trauma
			--------------------------------------------------
			-Remember that inducing plastic changes in tissues must be done with caution because if the trauma is significant enough, this may also produce pain and inflammation which may actually prevent healing over time
			-I. e.: A hysical therapist performs manual stretching into knee extension on a patient who lacks full knee extension after surgery. The physical therapist uses a force significant enough to cause microtrauma to the connective tissues of the posterior knee capsule. This patient experiences a lasting change in tissue length and maintains the gains in extension over a couple of days.
	``.Creep & Viscoelasticity
		-Basis for treatment techniques that involve sustained stretch application
			I.e.: splinting and Total End Range Time (TERT)
		-Another way to make lasting changes in tissue length without necessarily inducing plastic changes or tissue damage
		-Occurs when tissue placed on stretch at a level that usually produces elastic changes but becomes permanently longer because the stretch is sustained over a period of time
		-Gradual lengthening or tissue due to a constant load
			Tissue will lengthen over time even though no additional stress placed on it
			At correct load, it is an effective way to stretch tissue for therapeutic purposes
			Philosophy for splinting, traction, & other forms of sustained stretch in physical therapy
		-Viscoelasticity - Creep: Under a constant load, deformation increases over time

Physiologic changes in muscle tissue
	Sustained stretch of muscle tissue results in an increase in muscle length due to an increase in the number of sarcomeres in series
		-A sarcomere is the basic contractile unit of a muscle fiber, and regularly stretching will increase the number of these lined up horizontally within elongated fibers
		----------------------------------
		Muscle Tissue Function
			Mobility
				Muscle contractions apply loads to bony levers that result in joint motion
					(e.g., performing a biceps curl).
			Stability
				-Some forces from muscle contractions on bony levers are produced not for movement, but to maintain joint position or resist unwanted motions at a joint
					(e.g., the rotator cuff contracts during overhead activity to stabilize the humerus on the glenoid surface).
			Protection
				-Some muscle contractions protect bone and other connective tissue from tensile failure
					(e.g., hip muscles provide proximal stability that counteracts ground reaction forces on foot and ankle bones).
		--------------------------------- (No tenia clar si ficar en esta secció. ¿?)
		Muscle contraction generates forces
			within and 
			external to the muscle
		During muscle contraction:
			Tensile forces are created within the muscle
			Torque, compression, and shear forces can be generated at a joint secondary to muscle tension.
			Types of loads generated at the joint from muscle tension depend on:
			Where muscle attaches to bone
			Direction of pull from the muscle
			Orientation of the line of action from muscle to joint axis.
		Factors Influencing Muscle Tension Production
			1.Muscle Architecture
				Pennation
				Physiologic Cross-Sectional Area
			2. Fiber Type and Motor Recruitment
			3. Length-Tension Relationship
			4. Force-Velocity Relationship
		Muscle Pennation
			Muscle pennation is the Angle of orientation between muscle fibers and tendon
			Line of pull of muscle fibers influences the amount of force produced
				I.e.:: Straight angle of pull = greater force
			More angle is > 0 degrees, less force transmitted to the tendon
			However, muscles with pennation are wider
				Allows fit of more fibers pulling within muscle
				More fibers pulling = greater overall force
		---------------------------------
		TYPE I (a Snorkel=I nada un bon rato.)
			Are known as “Slow-twitch fibers"
			Have low capacity for anaerobic activity but high capacity for extended aerobic function
			Therefore, they are highly resistant to fatigue but not capable of very forceful contractions
			Function during endurance activities, low-intensity exercise
		TYPE IIA (Una mescla de les 2.)
			Classified as “Fast-twitch fibers”
			Are a blend of I and IIb
			Most effective during activities requiring both aerobic and anaerobic systems
		TYPE IIB (la B es una lletra musculà.)
			Also are “Fast-twitch fibers”
			Can function well for short bursts under anaerobic conditions
			Advantageous during activities requiring strength and power
		---------------------------------
		Muscle Fiber/Cell
			Basic unit of skeletal muscle that produces voluntary contractions.
			Consist of bundles of long thin strands known as myofibrils.
				Myofibrils subunits that are better yet known as protein filaments
				Make up 90% of cell volume.
					The two types of protein filaments are:
						Actin
						Myosin
						-----------------------------------
						Actin and myosin protein filaments are arranged in units known as a sarcomere.
							-During muscle contraction, the actin and myosin filaments within each sarcomere slide across one another causing the sarcomere, and ultimately the muscle, to contract.
			Skeletal muscle is organized, you can see that muscle fibers and groups of muscle fibers are encased in four layers of an extensive connective tissue (CT) network.
				Endomysium
					Encases individual muscle fibers
				Perimysium
					CT layer surrounding each fascicle
				Epimysium
					CT layer surrounding entire muscle belly
				Fascicles
					Surround groups of muscle fibers
			-----------------------------------------------
			Why do you think a muscle with a larger cross-section (thicker) will contract with greater force?
				Because it has thicker muscle fibers.
					A muscle with a larger cross-section (thicker) contracts with greater force. There is a larger amount of active proteins within the muscle to produce force. Muscles designed to produce greater force generally have a larger cross-sectional area.
			A muscle gains mass as a result of training that causes an increase in the number of protein filaments (actin and myosin). Muscle mass and cross-sectional area may increase as a result of training.
			-----------------------------------------------
			Length-Tension Relationship of Muscle
				Muscles can produce optimal tension when they are placed at their optimal length prior to contraction.
				This is because the length of the muscle affects the ability for actin and myosin to bind.
				Stretching a whole muscle by extending a joint
					elongates both the parallel and series elastic components, generates spring-like resistance, or stiffness within the muscle that adds to the tension generated during contraction.
				However, there is a limit to how much stretch adds to the muscle’s ability to generate tension
				The optimal length is when the muscle is placed on slight to moderate stretch (20% longer than at rest). When lengthened more, tension goes down
					In general:
						Optimally lengthened muscles generate greater tension
						Shortened muscles generate less tension
						Maximally lengthened muscle will cause a drop in tension
			----------------------------------------------
			Force-Velocity Relationship of Muscle
				Tension production in a muscle is also related to the speed at which the contraction is generated
					In a concentric action
						as the speed of contraction increases, the tension production decreases
					In an eccentric action, 
						tension development appears to be independent of the speed of contraction
					In general, isometric contractions in which there is no change in muscle length
						generate more tension than concentric contractions 
						and less tension than eccentric contractions.
				-------------------------------------------
				Questions (Pendent ¿?)
					-Why do you think there are differences in the way velocity affects generation of muscle tension between concentric, eccentric, and isometric contractions?
					-Can you think of how these concepts are relevant in these clinical examples?
					-Patient A is performing sit to stand, but does not seem to be developing sufficient contraction of the quadriceps to be successful.  What cues influencing contraction velocity might be helpful?
					-Patient B is performing eccentric strengthening of the calf muscles, but reports that after last exercise session he experienced 7/10 pain due to delayed onset muscle soreness for the next two days.  What corrections might lessen the post-exercise discomfort?
			--------------------------------------------------
			Muscle Response to Elongation
				As in other tissues, muscle tissue experiences stress-strain response.
				However, because different muscles are configured differently, this response varies by individual muscle
				All muscle tissue will experience plastic changes and tissue failure if stretched too far
					Muscle Adaptation to Prolonged or Regular Elongation:
						When muscle tissue is placed on prolonged stretch or exposed to stretch on a consistent basis, it experiences:
						1. An increase in number of number of sarcomeres in series
						2. Connective Tissue Changes
			--------------------------------------------------
			Muscle Injury and Healing
				Sprain VS Strain
					A sprain is rupture of a ligament
					A strain is generally considered to be an injury to muscle tissue
				Healing in Injured Muscle
					Occurs via activity of satellite cells
						Reserve cells found alongside muscle fibers
						Activated to form new muscle fibers after injury
						Replace injured fibers
				Connective tissue injury in muscle

Neurophysiologic changes
	Regular stretching of muscles and tendons may desensitize sensory organs located within muscle and tendon allowing greater relaxation during stretching.
		These include the golgi tendon organ and muscle spindle

Stretching of Ligaments and Tendons (No seria millor colocar esta en una secció d'elasticitat? ¿?)
	It is important to understand how they respond to various levels of tension
		-Consider all of the factors that you think will decrease the ability for connective tissue to stretch without sustaining damage.
			-Certain physical, environmental, and physiological conditions may affect the stress and strain response in dense connective tissue structures.
				-Changes in tissue temperature
				-Stage of healing in injured tissues - How does the stage of healing affect stress and strain on tendons and ligaments?
					Stages of Tissue Healing HEAL_SoftTissueHealing
					Effects of Mechanical Stress During Healing
						During initial healing, weak collagen fibers are easily ruptured
							Initial treatments should induce minimal stress
						During subsequent phases - especially remodeling - moderate stresses are productive
							Induces organization of collagen in more parallel arrangement in direction of applied tension forces
						As tissue matures, higher levels of stresses should be applied
				-Response to Prolonged Immobilization
					These changes occur even in uninjured structures
					SAID principle
						"Specific Adaption to Imposed Demands"
					Immobilized tissue will experience:
						Reduced tensile strength
						Adaptive shortening of structures
				-Effects of Aging
					As a result of aging, collagen tissue
						Decreases in overall content and quality
						Loses tensile strength
					Results in reduction in ability of structures to resist tensile loads and deform
						Easier to injure

Cases

.A basketball player fractures his ankle and is immobilized in a cast for 6 weeks.  After having the cast removed, he does not return to basketball for another 4 weeks.  Fearful of preventing the fracture from fully healing, he only engages in daily activities such as walking and household duties.  Once he is cleared to return to basketball by his physician, he begins to play again.

Based on the physical stress theory, which statement is true of the connective tissues in his foot and ankle?

They are neither more, nor less likely to be injured.
They are less likely to be injured.
They more likely to be injured.
They are initially less likely to be injured but after playing for a few weeks more likely to be injured.

They more likely to be injured.

.A patient tells her physical therapist that when stretching her hamstring muscles, she holds the stretch for 10 seconds. The physical therapist recommends that she hold the stretch for 30-60 seconds.

Initial Evaluation - Patient History (Hx) & General Examination (Physical Exam) [MSK_L01] [MSK1_C1.2,3]
	"CNVS-SS1 - MSK Xm 1.9.24.pptx"

	What is the primary purpose of Hx & Physical Exam?
		When appropriate, establish Patho-Anatomical Diagnosis (AKA Physiological Diagnosis or Diagnosis)
			Diagnosis
				the process of determining the nature of a disease or disorder and distinguishing it from other possible conditions
			I.e.:
				What is the anatomical structure that is torn?
				What is the disease condition?
				Etc.
		Identify (most important)
			Impaired body structures
				Impairments vs. Patho-Anatomical Diagnosis (AKA Physiological Diagnosis) [MSK_L02]
					Impairments (impaired body structures)
						Diagnosis can be valuable but "Impairment" is typically more important
						----------------------------------
						Why is identifying patient impairments often more important than identifying diagnosis?
							impairments is what they have right now; it is more current
							diagnosis is what they had 3 weeks ago, for examle
								--------------------------------------------------------
								I.e.: A patient come to us witout having a diagnosis + they do not know his impairment
									What is more important to figure out what is happening and why?
										-B/c we want to know (linking these two things together so we know how to create a plan of care)
											where the limitations are and 
											what is causing pain
								I.e.: If a patient come to us and tell us that they have a torn MCL
										Do we know what intervention to do?
											No. Only knowing what are the impairments is is going to help us
							---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
							I.e.: Two different patients both with the same diagnosys: tore their left MCL 3 weeks ago
								Patient 1 (pt.1)
									a.Still has lots of pain
									b.Has kept leg in brace 90% of time
									c.Hobbling around without assistive device
								Patient 2 (pt.2)
									a.Reports minimal pain
									b.Has been doing ROM exercises and using neighbor’s Continuous Passive Motion CPM everyday
									c.Also using neighbor’s crutches with minimal Wbing (Wbing= putting weight)
								---------------------------------------
								How their imparements differ (what we can asume looking at the cases)?
								Is one pt. stronger than the other? Not necesarily
									--------------------------------------------------------------------
									The pain
									pt.1 has lack of ROM
									pt.1 is weaker than pt.2
										pt.2 has begin to stress the tissues already by partially/minimally weightbearing
										Adjunct version: pt.1 because of the bracing he is not activating the mm. he needs
											-Instructor version: he could be stronger because we do not know how well the bracing was put or used
										pt.1 is also weightbearing but compensating with other muscles
					Patho-Anatomical Diagnosis
						N/A ¿?
			Activity limitations
			Participation restrictions
				----------------------------------------------
				Why identify impaired body structures, activity limitations, and participation restrictions?
					To develop appropriate goals
					To generate a treatment plan to reduce or eliminate these ones
			----------------------------------------------
			Case Example:
				A woman has low back pain one month after giving birth. This low back pain interferes with her ability to perform a number of specific tasks including the ability to lift her child and stand for more than a few minutes at a time. These problems, in turn, limit her ability to care for her newborn child. The physical therapist determines that some of her issues are related to the fact that her abdominal muscles have become weak due to body changes associated with pregnancy.
					-Using this information, match the aspects of the ICF model with the appropriate example described in the case information.
						Impaired body structures and functions
				              Pain and abdominal muscle weakness             
						Activities limitations
				              Decreased ability to stand and lift her child             
						Participation restrictions/Limitations in participation
				              Difficulty performing parental duties
		Why?
			To develop appropriate goals
			To generate a treatment plan to reduce or eliminate these
	------------------------------------------

					(Γ)--------------------------------------------------- Extended Version from My esearch
					(See 'Case:MSK1_47FootGolf' for a real example)
					Research how all of this questions are useful for us as a PT (!!)
					SIN1.Severity
						SIN1.a.Intensity of symptoms when present
							SIN1.a1.Numerical Quantification
								SIN1.a1.A. Numeric Pain Rating Scale/Pain Scale (NPRS) → 0-10
									-Can you rate your pain on a scale from 1 to 10?
								SIN1.a1.B. % limitation of function
									-On a scale from 0% to 100%, where 0% means no limitation and 100% means complete inability to walk, how would you rate the % limitation of function
								SIN1.a1.C. Minimal-Moderate-Severe
									-From minimal to moderate to severe, how would you describe the level of limitation in your daily activities?
								SIN1.a1.D. How do the symptoms fluctuate from the lowest level to the greatest level?
								Γ SIN1.a1.E. Do you take any medication for the pain? (este va ací? ¿?)
							SIN1.a2.Other than numbers
								Through functional behaviour
									-Could you describe how your heel pain affects your ability to perform specific activities, such as walking, standing for long periods, or engaging in recreational activities?
										-This question encourages the patient to provide specific examples of how their heel pain impacts their functional abilities, allowing the therapist to assess the practical implications of the pain on the patient's daily life.
								Are they able to work?
								How are they sleeping? (pain at rest)
								Do they need to take medications to releaf the pain?
					SIN2.Irritability
						-Asking questions about irritability helps to assess the patient's perception of how easily their symptoms worsen or become aggravated in response to various activities or stimuli
							-Understanding Sensitivity: By asking about irritability, the therapist gains insight into how sensitive the patient's symptoms are to aggravating factors. This helps in determining the degree of sensitivity of the condition.
								-On a scale from 1 to 10, with 1 being not sensitive at all and 10 being extremely sensitive, how would you rate the sensitivity of your symptoms to different activities or movements?

							-Identifying Aggravating Factors: Patients may identify specific activities, movements, or environmental factors that exacerbate their symptoms. Understanding these triggers allows the therapist to tailor treatment interventions and activity modifications accordingly.
								-Can you describe any specific activities or movements that tend to worsen your symptoms or make them more pronounced?

							-Assessing Progress: Monitoring changes in irritability over time can indicate the effectiveness of treatment interventions. A decrease in irritability may signify improvement in symptom management and functional outcomes.
								-Have you noticed any changes in how easily your symptoms worsen since starting physical therapy? If so, can you describe any improvements or differences?

							-Guiding Treatment Planning: Knowledge of irritability informs the development of a targeted treatment plan aimed at minimizing exacerbations and promoting symptom relief. Therapists can focus on addressing specific aggravating factors to optimize patient outcomes.
								-N/A

							-Educating the Patient: Discussing irritability with the patient helps them understand the importance of activity modification and adherence to treatment recommendations. It empowers them to make informed decisions to manage their symptoms effectively.
								-N/A
						------------------------------------------------------------
						SIN2.a.How easily are symptoms (sx's) provoked?
						SIN2.b.How long does it take them to subside after being provoked? / How long does it last after the onset?
						SIN2.c.More questions specific of the particular pt. case
							-I.e.: Do the symptoms comeback again or it happens only in the morning? How long are you free of the symptoms after they disapear? When they come back?  
					SIN3.Nature of the symptoms (what is going on?/Why is the patient coming to see you?)
						SIN3.a.Classification of problem
							categories related to the underlying source or cause of the patient's symptoms
							Nerve
							Muscle/tendon
							Joint/ligament
							Etc.
							---------------------------------- A more complete classification
							ask targeted questions designed to explore the specific aspects of the patient's symptoms and medical history
								-Musculoskeletal: This category encompasses conditions affecting the muscles, bones, joints, and associated tissues. Examples include muscle strains, ligament sprains, tendonitis, and osteoarthritis.
									Have you experienced any recent injuries or trauma to your muscles, bones, or joints?
									Can you describe the location and nature of your pain or discomfort?
									Do you notice any specific movements or activities that aggravate your symptoms?
								-Neurological: Conditions related to the nervous system, including nerves, spinal cord, and brain. Examples include nerve compression (such as sciatica), neuropathy, radiculopathy, and central nervous system disorders.
									Have you noticed any changes in sensation or movement in your arms, legs, or other body parts?
									Do you experience any tingling, numbness, or weakness in your extremities?
									Have you ever been diagnosed with a neurological condition such as neuropathy or radiculopathy?
								-Inflammatory: Conditions characterized by inflammation of tissues or joints. Examples include rheumatoid arthritis, bursitis, tendinitis, and inflammatory bowel disease-related arthropathy.
									Do you have a history of inflammatory conditions such as arthritis or tendonitis?
									Have you noticed any swelling, redness, or warmth in the affected area?
									Do your symptoms worsen during periods of increased inflammation or flare-ups?
								-Vascular: Conditions involving blood vessels and circulation. Examples include peripheral artery disease, deep vein thrombosis, and vasculitis.
									-Have you been diagnosed with any vascular conditions such as peripheral artery disease or deep vein thrombosis?
									-Do you experience any symptoms related to poor circulation, such as cramping or numbness in your limbs?
									-Have you noticed any changes in the color or temperature of your skin in the affected area?
								-Metabolic: Conditions related to metabolic imbalances or disorders. Examples include gout, diabetes-related neuropathy, and metabolic bone diseases like osteoporosis.
									-Do you have any underlying medical conditions such as diabetes or gout?
									-Have you noticed any changes in your symptoms related to fluctuations in your blood sugar levels or metabolic status?
									-Do you have a family history of metabolic disorders or related conditions?
								-Psychological/Psychosocial: Conditions influenced by psychological or psychosocial factors. Examples include depression, anxiety, somatization disorders, and pain disorders.
									-Have you experienced any significant stressors or life events that may be contributing to your symptoms?
									-Do you have a history of mental health conditions such as depression or anxiety?
									-Have you noticed any changes in your symptoms related to changes in your mood or emotional state?
								-Autoimmune: Conditions characterized by the immune system attacking the body's own tissues. Examples include lupus, multiple sclerosis, and inflammatory myopathies.
									-Have you been diagnosed with any autoimmune conditions such as lupus or rheumatoid arthritis?
									-Do you experience symptoms such as fatigue, joint pain, or skin rashes that may be indicative of an autoimmune disorder?
									-Have you undergone any testing or evaluation for autoimmune conditions in the past?
								-Traumatic/Orthopedic: Conditions resulting from physical trauma or orthopedic injuries. Examples include fractures, dislocations, and soft tissue injuries.
									-Have you experienced any recent falls, accidents, or injuries that may have caused your symptoms?
									-Do you have a history of orthopedic surgeries or procedures related to the affected area?
									-Have you noticed any changes in your symptoms since the onset of your injury or trauma?
								-Degenerative: Conditions related to age-related degeneration of tissues or structures. Examples include degenerative disc disease, osteoarthritis, and age-related muscle atrophy.
									-Are you experiencing symptoms related to age-related degeneration of tissues or structures?
									-Do you have a family history of conditions such as osteoarthritis or degenerative disc disease?
									-Have you noticed any changes in your symptoms over time that may be indicative of degenerative changes?
								-Systemic/Medical: Conditions with systemic manifestations or underlying medical disorders. Examples include fibromyalgia, chronic fatigue syndrome, and malignancies with musculoskeletal involvement.
									-Do you have any underlying medical conditions or systemic disorders that may be contributing to your symptoms?
									-Have you experienced any recent changes in your overall health or medical status?
									-Are you currently taking any medications or undergoing treatments for other medical conditions that may impact your symptoms?
						SIN3.b.Patient considerations (overall patient presentation/from patient's words)
							Psychosocial
								-How are you feeling emotionally lately? Have you experienced any stress, anxiety, or changes in mood?
								-Do you have any significant life events or stressors that may be influencing your symptoms?
								-How do your symptoms affect your daily activities, work, relationships, and overall quality of life?
								-Do you have a support system in place to help you cope with your symptoms?
							Pain
								About pt. with chronic pain
									-Mesure their progress based on how well functionally (or stregth) they are progressing even if the pain is the same
										-You may have to explain to them this, to push them to progress (a psichosocial conversation): not focussing on pain but on functionality
							Tolerance
								-How well do you tolerate physical activity or movement? Do certain movements or positions worsen your symptoms?
								-Have you noticed any changes in your pain tolerance over time or in response to treatment?
								-Are there any strategies or techniques you use to cope with or manage your pain?
							Character
								-Can you describe the characteristics of your symptoms in more detail? For example, do you experience stiffness, numbness, tingling, or weakness in addition to pain?
								-How would you describe the onset and progression of your symptoms? Have they developed gradually or suddenly?
								-Are there any specific triggers or patterns associated with your symptoms (e.g., time of day, certain activities)?
							Etc.
								Impact on Daily Functioning:
									-How do your symptoms affect your ability to perform daily tasks, such as self-care, work, household chores, and recreational activities?
									-Have you had to modify your daily routine or activities due to your symptoms?

								Sleep Disturbances:
									-Do your symptoms affect your sleep quality or quantity? Do you experience difficulty falling asleep, staying asleep, or waking up feeling refreshed?
									-Have you noticed any changes in your sleep patterns since the onset of your symptoms?

								Emotional Well-being:
									-How do your symptoms impact your emotional well-being and overall mood? Do you experience feelings of frustration, sadness, or irritability related to your condition?
									-Are there any specific concerns or worries you have regarding your symptoms or their potential impact on your life?

								Coping Mechanisms:
									-What strategies do you use to cope with your symptoms and manage any associated stress or discomfort?
									-Are there any activities or practices that help you feel more comfortable or alleviate your symptoms temporarily?
					SIN4.Stage & Stability (S&S)
						SIN4.a.Stage
							Acute
							Subacute
							Chronic
							--------------------------------------------------------------------------
							-In the context of the SINSS framework, determining the stage of the patient's condition involves assessing the severity and duration of their symptoms. While there may not be specific guidelines outlined within the SINSS framework for assessing stage, clinicians typically consider several factors to determine the stage of the condition. These factors may include:
								Duration of Symptoms:
									-How long have you been experiencing these symptoms?
									-Have your symptoms been consistent, improving, or worsening over time?
									-Do you notice any patterns or fluctuations in the severity of your symptoms?
								Severity of Symptoms:
									-On a scale of 0 to 10, with 0 being no symptoms and 10 being the most severe symptoms imaginable, how would you rate the severity of your symptoms at their worst?
									-How do your symptoms impact your daily activities, function, and overall quality of life?
									-Have you noticed any changes in the severity of your symptoms since they first began?
								Functional Limitations:
									-How do your symptoms affect your ability to perform daily tasks, work, and participate in recreational activities?
									-Are there any specific movements or activities that exacerbate your symptoms or limit your function?
								Response to Previous Interventions:
									-Have you tried any treatments, interventions, or self-management strategies for your symptoms? If so, how did you respond to them?
									-Have you noticed any changes in your symptoms or function following treatment or interventions?
						SIN4.b.Stability
							Gotten worse, etc.
							------------------------------------------------------------------------------
							-In the context of the SINSS framework, assessing the stability of the patient's condition involves evaluating whether their symptoms are stable, improving, or worsening over time. While there may not be specific guidelines outlined within SINSS for assessing stability, clinicians typically consider several factors to determine the stability of the condition:
								Symptom Progression:
									-Have you noticed any changes in the frequency, intensity, or duration of your symptoms over time?
									-Do your symptoms remain consistent, or do they fluctuate or vary in severity?
								Functional Status:
									-How do your symptoms impact your ability to perform daily activities and function?
									-Have you experienced any improvements or worsening in your ability to perform tasks or participate in activities?
								Treatment Response:
									-Have you undergone any treatments or interventions for your symptoms? If so, how have you responded to them?
									-Have you noticed any changes in your symptoms or function following treatment or interventions?
								Psychological Factors:
									-How do your symptoms affect your mood, stress levels, and overall well-being?
									-Have you experienced any changes in your emotional or psychological state in relation to your symptoms?
								External Influences:
									-Are there any external factors or triggers that may affect the stability of your symptoms, such as changes in activity level, stress, or environmental factors?
				---------------------------------------

//////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Maitland Approach to Clinical Decision Making in Physical Therapy [MSK_L02]
//////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

It is a cycle rather than linear:
	(Examination → Assessment → Treatment) → (Examination ...)
	-------------------------------
	Examination
		Obtained through
			History (hx) and
			Physical Exam
	Assessment
		The most impostant part of the PR evaluation
		It is putting the pieces (the findings) together and interpreting what it means.
		What causes the patient problems?
		What can do about it to aliviete the patient problem?
		Where is this goint to go and our ability to be succesful with that?
	Treatment
		Based on the Assessment we form our treatment
		And we see what kind of differences we achieve
		--------------------------- Examination
		Then we go back to do Examination again (to see if what we are doing is working) and start once more the cycle
			Then we progress or abandon what we are doing, or try a different approach

	Not as nearly as helpful as we could think
		Almost everybody has some kind of abnormalities associated with
			Lumbar disks
			Facet Joints
			Etc. around the lumbar spine
		There is a huge amount of false positives MRIs

Knowledge Check
	A Journal has recently published a high-quality article describing performance of a screening procedure for a life-threatening condition known as “Condition Q”. [Q_WhenScreening]
		Screening for condition Q should be performed in which of the following situations?
			It is certain that the patient has Condition Q
				No need to screen for it. However, you will still screen for a more advanced category of this cndition if needed
			✓It is suspected but not certain that Condition Q could be present
				You have a reason to spend time in your evaluation and to confirm this
				To rule in/out condition Q
				Confirmation
			It is certain that the patient does not have Condition Q
				No need to screen then for this specific condition
			In both situations A and B
				No
			All patients should be screened for Condition Q since it is life-threatening, and screening does no harm
				No sense
			---------------------------
			The think is that there so many screening procedures that you can do that we have to do the ones we really need
			It also depends on the complexity of the condition, you want to start with basic screening to stablish a baseline
			For vital signs, we always screen however.

		Lower Quarter Diagnostic Images
	////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

	X-rays
		Overview of X-rays
			-A form of invisible electromagnetic radiation, similar to visible light
			-However unlike visible light, they have higher energy and can pass through most objects, including the human body
			-Medical x-rays are used to generate images of structures and tissues (like bone) inside the body

			Types of X-rays
				Film
					Cassette with intensifying crystals and multiple screens
					Final picture representative of radiodensities of structures imaged
				Fluoroscopy​
					-It is a medical procedure that makes a real-time video of the movements inside a part of the body by passing x-rays through the body over a period of time.
				Computed Tomography (CT)/Digital​
					-A CT scan is a diagnostic imaging procedure that uses a combination of X-rays and computer technology to produce images of the inside of the body. It shows detailed images of any part of the body, including the bones, muscles, fat, organs, and blood vessels.

			Some Terms to Remember
				Γ The property than can be measured
					.Radiodensity
						Refers to "the amount of radiation an object absorbs". Objects that absorb radiation well show up better on X-ray imaging.
				Γ The objects that possess the property 
					.Radiopaque
						Describes "an object or substance" that easily absorbs radiation (Γ therefore absorbs the radiation and does show up better). Radiopaque substances are "more readily visible" on X-ray.
					.Radiolucent
						Describes "an object or substance" that is easily penetrated by radiation (Γ therefore does not absorbs the radiation and does not show up) and "not as readily visible".
					--------------------------------------
					Radiolucent 
						(Increasing Radiodensity: from darker to lighter)
						("radiolucent Tyoes MKS1 No Official.png")
						Shading on X-ray Imaging ("Shading on X-ray Imaging.png")
						------------------------------------------
							Air/Gas – Black
							 Fat – Gray black
							  Soft tissue & Water
							   Water – Gray
							    Bone – White/Light gray
								 Metal - White
							      Radiopaque//Contrast Media – White outline

			How do X-rays Work?
				("02_04 How Do X-rays Work.mp4")

			Important Considerations of X-ray Imaging ("Considerations of X-ray Imaging0")
				-An object's density can play a role in how it shows up on an x-ray 
					-Materials denser than air and soft tissue will absorb more rays, and therefore, appear in greater contrast (gray or white) to the surrounding areas on an x-ray
				-The size of an object can also limit radiographic accuracy due to distortion
					Extremely small objects under 1 mm in size may be difficult to identify on an x-ray
					On the contrary, extremely large objects can be difficult to capture in one x-ray image
				-Foreign objects can sometimes blend in with the radiopacity of bone structures, which can easily be missed when only viewing the x-ray from one viewpoint
				-This is why it's important to take x-rays from multiple angles or viewpoints
		-------------------------------------- [MSK1_L01_MSK03.mp4]
		Does an X-Ray needs to occur?
			Patients often ask this question: -Do I need an X-Ray?-

				Case 1 (Harold): 44-year-old male with rapid onset of left hip pain 1 week ago
					-Harold comes as a direct access patient and reports a loss of 15 lbs. in the past month without intentionally trying, however he has also increased his running volume. Pain is 8/10 at night and he cannot find a comfortable position. In the morning he feels like he needs crutches because the pain limits him from putting weight on his leg and he brought crutches today “just in case.” In the past couple weeks he’s noticed increasingly more frequent urination and difficulty starting the flow of urine.

								(See [Q_WhenScreening])
								A/PROM are painful so it is non-contratile issue = Irrelevant only with this info alone.
									Would this change our treatment plan? No
										-Getting imaging is like putting a Red Flag that means that there is something you need to check out on them before you treat them. Being a non-contractile issue like the joint capsule will not change the treatment; so we not need to stop, get the X-ray, and come back for PT. (Γ)Maybe if we suspect it is a cartilage issue we need screenign? ¿?.
											(Γ)Passive Range of Motion (PROM) assessment is usually performed when a patient has limited Active Range of Motion (AROM). The goal of PROM is to evaluate the end-feel of the joint to determine what is limiting full range of motion. It is important to assess both AROM and PROM to understand the extent of the limitation and its underlying causes. PROM assessment helps in determining the presence of any restrictions in the joint capsule or muscles. Therefore, a patient with limited AROM and PROM "may" require a screening procedure to further evaluate the underlying issues causing the limited range of motion
								The loss of weight and pain at night may suggest cancer (a tumor)
									Then screening recommended
								The urination problem + pain at ROM
									Referral recommended
										Any change on bowel or blader, referral right away

										Kindney problem?

											It is decreasing in older population
											except for 25 to 45 y.o that it is skyrocketing

									He needs screening because the pain is non-mechanical

				Case 2 (Jenna): 15-year-old female ballet dancer with complaints of right anterior and medial knee pain that began gradually
					-Jenna reports pain for the past 4 weeks. She comes to you referred from her primary care physician and is accompanied by her mother. Jenna complains of 6/10 pain following dance class with 0-3/10 pain by the next morning. Her mom notes some concern that her daughter may not be eating enough as she has added an additional 2 classes each week. Denies any other remarkable past medical history. 
						-Range of motion within normal limits in all planes for hip, knee, and ankle except for pain at end-range knee flexion. 
						-Pain to palpation right medial facet of patella 
						-Hypermobile patellar mobility bilaterally 
						-Strength 5/5 except for: 4/5 right glute med, glute max, and hamstrings. 
						-Eccentric step-down test positive for medial knee pain
							--------------------------
							-This patient and her mother are concerned that she has had pain for a month and has not gotten imaging. The patient’s mom asks, “do you think we need to get an x-ray or MRI?”
							--------------------------
							From History/Patient Interview
								a.Timeline: pain for the past 4 weeks
								b.Irritability (middle): 6/10 pain following dance class
								c.Severity (low): 0-3/10 pain by the next morning
								d.mom concern daughter may not be eating enough/has added an additional 2 classes each week
								e.Denies any other remarkable past medical history
							From Physical Exam
								-f.Range of motion within normal limits in all planes for hip, knee, and ankle except for
									knee flexion: pain at end-range
										We are stretching a structure
											Could be contractile or non-contractile
												ACL?
												Meniscus?
								-g.Pain to palpation right medial facet of patella 
								-h.Hypermobile patellar mobility bilaterally 
								-i.Strength 5/5 except for:
									4/5
										right glute med
										glute max 
										hamstrings
								-j.Eccentric step-down test positive for
									medial knee pain

							1.Why do you think imaging is not necessary for this patient?
								-If it is a contractile or non-contractile, it does not change the course of the treatment; no imaging is needed, we can go ahead with PT treatment
									If the arguments is that we need imaging to confirm that what we are doing is correct for the patient then think: what else, besides imaging, can we do to confirm our hypothesis?
										Using "Outcomes Measures" to see if there is an improvement
											I.e.: If we beleive this a ACL issue, we will have to confirm this through Special Tests, then if it is positive, we need imaging before going to surgery
								-The pain increases after her class. So, she is still active; her injury is not stopping her to do anything. So, the severity scale is not that high
									-There is no mechanism of injury here. If there would be one (like a torn ACL, etc.), she would be in a different situation. This is only a gradual onset of adding two classes a week
									-Then, before more invasive testing, we will be more conservative and see if the pain decreases, even if she has a torn ACL (reducing the classes or even stopping them? ¿?)
								CONCLUSION:
									Not imaging recomended
							2.What specific findings make you think this?
								See question 1.
							3.Based on these answers, role play how you would have this conversation with Jenna.
								Maybe dancing is really importat for them for whatever reason and they are not going to stop until they see an imaging that confirms that everything is OK.
									-Explain that the imaging is not going to provide more that we alredy know, and that if she continues like that then we will face a real issue, then we will have to undertake imaging an PT treatment. In resume, delaying care will make this worse.
									-So, if we have tools from PT therapy, we have to try them, then through outcomes measures, see if the issue is resolving. If not, we will go for imaging; explain this.
							4.How could obtaining an image we do not need be harmful? ¿?

				.Your patient is a 23-year-old female 2 weeks s/p hip arthroscopic surgery. She has been on crutches since surgery touch-down weightbearing. 	Radiography [Also: "MSK1_L01_MSK03.mp4"]
		Basic Radiographic Examination
			Orienting Yourself to the Radiograph
				What view was taken?
					Anterior to Posterior (AP) (means from the front)?
					Posterior to Anterior (PA) (means from the back)?
					Lateral
					Oblique (less common)
				Patient’s right is on your left
				Region?
			The “ABC’s” of Radiographic Examination
				The ABCs of interpreting X-rays describe specific characteristics to consider when reading X-rays
					Alignment (and Assessment of Quality)
						Γ -I.e.: Using the standard knee radiograph, one can measure anatomic alignment (as distinguished from mechanical alignment), the angle subtended by the line of the femoral shaft as it intersects in the knee with the line of the tibial shaft).
						---------------------------------------------------
						Is the x-ray of good enough quality for assessment?
						Are there any abnormalities in anatomical alignment that suggest pathology?
							Things to consider:
								Architecture
									Size
									shape
									number of bones (extra? missing?)
									Deformities
									General alignment and anatomic appearance
								General contour
									Visually trace outlines of each bone
									Should be smooth and continuous - Cortical outline
									Sharp or incongruous – possible fracture or bony disease
									Spurring (brotant, un brot d'os)
								Relationships between articulating bones
									Dislocations, subluxations? - Displacement
									Spinal alignment
						Alignment Examples (Folder: "Alignment Examples MSK1")
							Degenerative Lumbar Scoliosis
							Advanced Rheumatoid Arthritis
							Bone Spur (brot) on Heel
							Adult vs. Child Hand & Wrist
							Dislocated Shoulder
							-----------------------------
							Alignment Examples to Examine ("SS3 - CNVS Imaging 1.16.24.pptx" & [01:12:33sec])
								Sliped Radial Head Epiphisys ("Sliped Radial Head Epiphisys.png")
									Lateral to Medial view?
									Neurovascular and motor control normal
									She complains of left wrist pain
									Deformity after having falled with her stretched hand
									X-Ray says

					Case 1 ("Avascular Necrosis.png")
						31 year old male presents to the Emergency Department with right distal quad/lateral hip & knee pain
							Patient Interview
								Began 1 month ago while running
								Reported sudden onset of “popping” in right leg with immediate severe pain
								Stopped running for several weeks
								Tried to run 1 week ago and developed right lateral hip/distal quad pain ("/"="and")
								Has run regularly since he was a teenager averaging 80-100 miles per week
							Exam Findings:
								Knee ROM WFL but flexion ↑s (increases) R hip pain
								Lumbar testing does not reproduce pain
									Standing for testing painful in R hip
								Hip AROM: 90 deg flex, 10 deg IR, 25 deg ER 
									limited by pain
								Resistive test R hip flexors painful and weak
								Palpation 
									painful at right ASIS, proximal hip flexor attachment, over lateral quads and distal ITB
						Possible differential diagnoses? (up to 3)
							Quad injury
							Stress fracture due to the excesive milage runned
							Impingement
							----------------------
							Real Result
								Avascular Necrosis
						Looking at the radigraph
							Right femoral head not as smooth as the left one
							Less space on the right b/w the femoral neck and acetabula
								-More signal (more white) around the femoral neck indicating more mass there due to the bone apoximation
							Less bone density (less opaque or white) on the right femur
							He ended with a total hip replacement 

					Soft Tissue
						-While Soft Tissue itself is not visible via radiography, x-rays may often reveal something about the state of soft tissue
							Normal - Soft tissue minimally distinguishable on radiograph
							After trauma/pathology - it may become distinguishable
								Examples may be the presence of
									swelling
									inflammation
									foreign bodies
									etc.

						1.MRI (soft tissue)
						2.N/A
						3.We know that there is a tear and we need the imaging. Also, surgery may be needed.
					Who may have early onset osteoporosis?
						1.Bone Density Scan
						2.X-Ray con show us bone density too but BDS will show more info. Also, the pt. will need to make some life changes
						3.N/A

						1.CT Scan. It will give the best of both worlds
						2.We know that there is a fracture and we need the imaging. Also, surgery may be needed.

						Has severe neck pain
