	Commonly Missed Fractures of the Spine, Upper and Lower Extremities
		-The below attachment describes a review of commonly missed high-risked fractures of the spine, and upper/lower extremities of the body. This document is for future reference only (it will not appear on course assessments unless these are highlighted elsewhere !!).
			("PT 2231_Mod 2_Missed Fractures.pdf")
				Spine
					C1-C2 fractures; C6-C7 fractures 
						Falls or motor vehicle accidents
							Radiographic clues to instability include segmental kyphosis, loss of lordosis, anterior soft-tissue swelling, antero/retrolisthesis; best imaged with CT
					Vertebral body fractures secondary to osteoporosis
						Increased incidence in postmenopausal females with age, with or without trauma
							Loss of vertebral body height that exceeds 20%
				Wrist
					Scaphoid fracture
						Fall on an outstretched hand
							Consider obtaining additional scaphoid views because routing radiographs often appear normal
					Triquetrum fracture
						Fall on an outstretched hand
							Triquetrum fractures are most easily seen on lateral view because this is the most dorsal carpal bone
					Galeazzi fracture (distal third of radius fracture with disruption of distal radioulnar joint)
						Fall on an outstretched hand with forearm pronation
							PA view: Ulnar styloid fracture may be seen with widening of distal radioulnar joint
							Lateral view: Ulna does not overlie radius and ulnar styloid is not aligned with dorsal triquetrum
					Distal radius Fx + carpal injury
						Fall on an outstretched hand
							Distal radius fractures sometimes associated with findings of scapholunate dissociation
				Elbow
					Monteggia Fx (proximal ulna fracture with radial head dislocation)
						Fall on an outstretched hand with rotational forces
							Obvious proximal ulna fracture
							Misalignment of radiocapitular line
							Cortical break in the radial head may be very subtle or absent in a nondisplaced Fx
					Radial head
						Fall on an outstretched hand
							Large anterior fat pad (Sail sign); any posterior fat pad sign
				Hip
					Femoral neck
						Direct trauma, as from a fall
							Note: Some patients can bear weight despite a Fx
							Look for cortical disruption or impacted hyperlucency
							See loss of smooth cortical transition from femoral neck to head as well as trabecular disruption
					Acetabulum
						Direct contact with femoral head, as in “dashboard" injuries
							-Anterior acetabular Fx is revealed by a break in the iliopubic line
							-Posterior acetabular fracture is shown by a break in the ilioschial line, looking behind the superimposed femoral head
							-Additional oblique views help for initially negative standard-view films
				Pelvis
					Sacrum
						Fall from height, motor vehicle accident
							Note subtle break in smooth sacral arcuate lines
							Pelvic outlet views improve visualization of the sacrum and rami
					Pelvic ring Fx
						Motor vehicle accidents, pedestrian accidents, fall from height
							Look for multiple injuries due to the inflexible ring structure of the pelvis; watch for rami fracture and sacroiliac dissociation
				Knee
					Tibial plateau; Tibial spine
						Valgus force with axial load, such as strike by car bumper
							Two views provide only 85% sensitivity; oblique views increase sensitivity
					Second fracture (small proximal lateral tibial avulsion Fx)
						Internal rotation and varus stress
							May be accompanied by other injuries:
								ACL tear (75%-100%), medial and lateral menisci tears (66%-70%, fibular head avulsion fracture
								MRI is needed to evaluate the ACL and possible injuries to menisci and other structures
					Patella
						Motor vehicle injuries, direct fall onto knee
							Assessed with axial patellofemoral “sunrise” view
							Lateral view is best to detect transverse fractures
					Maisonneuve fracture (proximal fibula fracture with distal tibiofibular disruption)
						Abduction and external rotation of the ankle
							Requires AP view of the knee as well as AP view of the ipsilateral ankle
				Foot
					Calcaneus
						Fall on the heels from a height
							A Boehler angle of less than 25° suggests a Fx
							Consider obtaining a “calcaneal view” (long axial view)
							Often requires CT imaging to assess fragments and intra-articular extension
					Talus
						Excessive dorsiflexion of the ankle
							Can find subtle cortical break on lateral view
					Thoracolumbar Fx + calcaneus Fx
						Jump from a height transmits force from heels to spine
							These two fractures have a 10% association rate
							Ensure both thoracolumbar and calcaneal studies are evaluated
		Potential fractures should be re-imaged if fracture is not detected on day of onset of symptoms.
		1-2 weeks post injury, a fracture can be detected since most of the acute swelling has subsided.

	MRI
		Identifying T1 and T2 Type MRIs
			Overview of MRI Weighted Scans
				-Best for identifying soft tissue structures, but it is limited by the time needed to produce an image and the high cost associated with the procedure
				----------------------------------------
				The most common MRI sequences are T1-weighted and T2-weighted scans. 
					T1 Images use shorter timing of radiofrequency pulse sequences to make images which highlight
						fat tissue
					T2 images require longer timing of radiofrequency pulse sequences and these highlight
						fat tissue
						water
				----------------------------------------
				Visible color of various substances and structures in T1 VS T2 weighted images MRI ("visible color T1 vs T2 weighted images.png")
					White (Hyperintense)
						T1 Weighted Images
							subacute hemorrhage
							melanin
							protein-rich fluid
							slowly flowing blood
							paramagnetic substances: gadolinium,
							manganese, copper
							calcification (rarely)
							laminar necrosis of cerebral infarction
						T2 Weighted Images
							increased water, as in
								edema
								tumor
								infarction
								inflammation
								infection
								subdural collection
							methemoglobin (extracellular) in subacute hemorrhage
					Gray
						T1 Weighted Images
							Gray Matter darker than white
								Γ More myelin in axons (fat) ¿?
						T2 Weighted Images
							White Matter darker than gray
								Γ More water on cell boy ¿?
					Dark
						T1 Weighted Images
							-Bone
							-Urine
							-CSF
							-increased water, as in edema, tumor, infarction. inflammation, infection, hemorrhage (hyperacute or chronic)
							-low proton density, calcification
						T2 Weighted Images
							-Bone
							-Fat
							-low proton density, calcification, fibrous tissue
							-paramagnetic substances: deoxyhemoglobin, methemoglobin (intracellular), iron, ferritin, hemosiderin, melanin
							-protein-rich fluid
						----------------------------------
						Radiology Mnemonic 
							MRI imaging weighting Mnemonic ("Radiology Mnemonic.jpg")
		Magnetic Resonance Imaging Comparison
			The following images show comparisons between a T1 VS T2-weighted MRI scan 
				("comparisons between a T1 vs. T2-weighted MRI scan.png")
					Note: Both were acquired at 3.0 T, with field of view of 240 by 240 mm and 3-mm-thick slices
				Image A:
					The T1 image can show clearly the difference between fluids and fat
						(note the white stripe of fat posterior to the spinal cord),
						fluids are black or hypointense, 
						muscle is in the middle (gray) and fat is the brightest
				Image B:
					The T2 image is “fat suppressed” and shows clearer visualization of discs and the fluid
						-Fat and CSF are hyperintense, but the response of fat is predictable and the computer can adjust
						-Muscle injury will appear bright because of swelling
		MRI Best Uses
			1. MRI is very sensitive for detecting changes and variations in bone marrow
				This is important in the diagnosis of
					bone tumors, 
					stress fractures, and 
					avascular necrosis
			2. MRI excels in the display of soft tissue detail
				It is widely used in the diagnosis of sports injuries because of its ability to
					differentiate between the different soft tissue and
					distinguish partial tears of tendons and ligaments from complete tears
			3. In some cases, MRI has replaced invasive diagnostic procedures such as arthroscopy, as in the detection of meniscal tears
			4. MRI is the best modality for evaluation of
				disk herniations and 
				other potential causes of nerve root impingement
			5. MRI can stage
				-neoplasms in bone and soft tissues as well as
				-evaluate the extent of tissue invasion, prior to surgery
			    -It is more sensitive than bone scan for detecting bone metastases, although bone scan is more effective as a screening technique
		(Γ)MRI VS Open MRI 
			In one you go in, in the other you do not go in

	Bone Scan vs. Bone Density Scan
		Bone Scan Test ("Bone Scan.gif")
			A bone scan is where a contrast agent is injected to a specific bone for bone uptake
			Pathological conditions usually show increased uptake
				There will be increased metabolic activity that demonstrate “hot spots”, which can be seen for
					Stress fractures
					avascular necrosis (AVN)
					metastatic disease, or 
					metabolic bone disease
		Bone Density Scan Test ("Bone Density Scan.jpg")
			Uses X-rays to measure how many grams of calcium and other bone minerals are in a specific bone.

	CT - Computed Tomography (CT Scan) ("Computed Tomography (CT Scan).png")
		A computed tomography (CT) scan is a narrow beam of x-rays rotated around the body
		Its signals are processed by computer to create sliced images of an area like an MRI
		It is an advanced digital enhancement of radiology
			It is more sensitive than plain radiograph
		It provides a greater visualization of soft tissue but cannot detect histological changes
		---------------------------------------------
		CT Scan Best Uses
			-is best for identifying subtle fractures and/or complex fractures.
			-is best for evaluating degenerative changes, such as spinal arthritic changes. 
			-may be the first imaging choice in serious trauma since multiple injuries to both osseous and soft tissue structures can be determined from one imaging series. 
			-excels in the evaluation of spinal stenosis, especially if performed as CT myelography.
			-Combined with a diskogram, it may give invaluable information on the condition of the intervertebral disk.
			-is the best modality for the evaluation of loose bodies in a joint.
			-is less time-consuming than MRI or ultrasound.
			-allows for accurate measurements of osseous alignment in any plane. 
			-is usually less expensive than MRI.
			-is less problematic for patients with claustrophobia.

	Ultrasound
		-This diagnostic tool creates cross-sectional images via sound waves reflected off tissue surfaces
		-A few studies have found ultrasound (US) to have a comparable diagnostic sensitivity and specificity to that of MRI
		-Cost-saving could be significant. However, US is more operator-dependent when compared to other imaging tools
			it cannot penetrate bone, and
			US waves cannot cross air interfaces in lungs or intestines
		---------------------------------------------------------
		Ultrasound Best Uses
			General Advantages
				Low Cost and portability
				No contraindications, orthopedic hardware not a problem
				Ability to visualize structures not limited by orthogonal planes
				Continuous modification of imaging based on findings
				Palpation, stress, testing, and application of resistance while imaging
			Advantages Over MRI
				Muscles - Architecture, imaging while testing with resistance
				Tendons - Fiber structure, degenerative changes, longitudinal tears
				Ligaments - Fiber structure, ability to stress test while imaging
				Cysts and bursae - Septations, debris not seen on MRI
			Disadvantages
				Joints; intra-articular structures
					Limited ability to show joint surfaces and intra-articular structures
				Bones 
					Can only show cortical outline of bone
				Scanning across lung fields
					Ultrasound does not cross air/tissue interfaces
			------------------------------------
			Knowledge Check
				Select all of the advantages of MRI. 
					✗is less problematic for patients with claustrophobia.
					✓is the best modality for evaluation of disk herniations and other potential causes of nerve root impingement. 
					✗is usually the least expensive imaging option.
					✓excels in the display of soft tissue detail. It is widely used in the diagnosis of sports injuries
				Select all of the advantages of CT scanning.
					✓-allows for accurate measurements of osseous alignment in any plane.  
					✓-is less problematic for patients with claustrophobia versus an MRI.
					✓-may be the first imaging choice in serious trauma since multiple injuries to both osseous and soft tissue structures can be determined from one imaging series.
					✗-is usually more expensive than MRI.
					✗-is the worst modality for the evaluation of loose bodies in a joint.

	Lower Quarter Diagnostic Images
		(Folder: "Lower Quarter Diagnostic Images MSK1_C2.5" & 
		"PT 2231_Lower Quarter Diagnostic Imaging_RBB.pptx")
		---------------------------------------------------
		Spondylolytic Pars Fracture
		L5-S1 Loss of Disc Height
		MRI Lateral View of Spine
			T1 vs T2 – T1 measures structures that rapidly give up energy early in the process of longitudinal remagnitization. This creates a fairly balanced image that provides a good picture of anatomic details. T2 measures energy late in the state of decay so is more selective in the structures it images. Water doesn’t readily give up energy so structures that contain a significant amount of water show up with higher signal intensity. This makes the T2 image valuable for detecting inflammation.
			--------------------------------------------------
			T1 Weighted Image
				radio frequency is caught early with tissues that recover magnetization such as fat create an area of high signal intensity. Water which recovers slowly will appear dark
			T2 Weighted Image
				radio frequency is caught late and tissues that are reluctant to give up energy are measured best. Water appears best
		Chronic Low Back and Left Lower Extremity Pain in an Elderly Woman
			The patient was a 79-year-old woman who presented to her primary care physician with complaints of constant low back and left lower extremity pain that had progressively worsened over the previous 6 months. The patient reported that her symptoms were insidious in onset and currently limited her ability to walk greater than 27 m, stand longer than 10 minutes, and sit longer than 5 minutes. Physical examination revealed an antalgic gait, limited lumbar flexion and extension range of motion that both exacerbated the patient’s symptoms, and weakness of the left L5 myotome as assessed with manual resistance to great-toe extension.
		Femoral Neck Stress Fracture
			The patient was a 19-year-old woman who recently completed a military basic training program. She was evaluated by a physical therapist in a direct-access capacity for a chief complaint of anterior right hip pain that limited her ability to run. Although there was no history of traumatic injury, her hip pain was insidious in onset and had gradually worsened over the previous 2 weeks. Prior to enlisting in the military, the patient had led a sedentary lifestyle. However, due to the requirements of her military training program, her activity level had gradually increased to running 24 to 32 km/wk over a 4-week period. Visual observation revealed that gait velocity was slowed and that stance phase for the right lower extremity was decreased. Right hip active and passive range of motion was painful and limited, and manual muscle testing of the right hip musculature revealed weakness and extreme pain.
		Hamstring Imaging
			Remember the T2 Image is going to show water the best so edema shows up well.
		FIGURE 1. Anterior-posterior (A) and lateral (B) radiographic views of the right knee
			The patient was a 32-year-old male who sustained a noncontact left knee injury during a quick pivot on a planted left foot, while playing flag football. He felt a pop in his knee, noted immediate swelling, and was unable to bear weight. He subsequently presented to the emergency department for evaluation.
		Tibial Spine Avulsion Fracture (AKA tibial spine comminuted avulsion fracture) (x2 images)
			The patient was a 32-year-old male who sustained a noncontact left knee injury during a quick pivot on a planted left foot, while playing flag football. He felt a pop in his knee, noted immediate swelling, and was unable to bear weight. He subsequently presented to the emergency department for evaluation.
		Foot and Ankle Pain in a Young Female Athlete
			The patient was a 14-year-old female with a 4-year history of left anterolateral foot and ankle pain of insidious onset that was first noticed while playing soccer. Following the initial onset of symptoms, the patient sprained her left ankle several times while playing basketball and volleyball. After a lack of progress with conservative measures for her left foot and ankle pain, which included a home exercise program and the use of an ankle stabilizing orthosis while playing sports, the patient was evaluated by an orthopedic surgeon. While conventional radiographs of the foot and ankle were normal, magnetic resonance imaging revealed generalized bone marrow edema in the navicular bone with irregularity along the lateral margin of the navicular bone and adjacent anterior margin of the anterior process of the calcaneus. These findings suggested possible sequelae of osteonecrosis or a partial coalition between the lateral margin of the navicular bone and anterior process of the calcaneus. A coalition is characterized by an abnormal union of 2 bones that can result in altered biomechanics. 
		Vertical Fracture Through the Body of the Talus
			The patient was a 35-year-old man who was evaluated by a physical therapist in a direct-access capacity for a chief complaint of right ankle pain and an inability to bear weight after sustaining an inversion injury to his right ankle while playing in a basketball game 1 day prior. Observation revealed erythema and moderate right ankle edema that was more pronounced at the lateral and posterior aspects of the ankle. The patient was unable to bear weight on his right lower extremity, and active range of motion of the right ankle was substantially decreased in all planes due to pain. On distal neurovascular examination, the right ankle was found to be warm, lighttouch sensation of the right foot and ankle region was intact, pulsations of posterior tibial and dorsalis pedis arteries were intact, and capillary refill in the toes was normal. Exquisite tenderness to palpation was noted at the anterior aspect of the talocrural joint.
		A Partial Tear of the Infraspinatus Muscle
			A29-year-old man experienced right shoulder pain and weakness following a forced abduction and external rotation (ER) motion performed to fend off an opposing player during an Australian football game. He was brought off the field and assessed by the club’s physical therapist and sports medicine physician. Active shoulder elevation and rotation were painful, and he was unable to externally rotate against resistance. Palpation revealed tenderness isolated to the infraspinatus muscle, focally worse through the medial aspect, with no rotator cuff musculotendinous junction tenderness. The following morning, the sports medicine physician referred the player for magnetic resonance imaging to exclude infraspinatus rupture, given the ER strength deficit.
		Rotator Cuff Tear Consequent to Glenohumeral Dislocation
			The patient was a 21-year-old collegiate running back who was tackled during a football game and sustained a posterior glenohumeral dislocation, which was reduced on the first attempt with traction performed by the athletic trainer on the field. The patient was referred to an orthopaedist and presented 3 weeks after the injury. The patient was unable to provide specifics regarding mechanism of injury (eg, position of upper extremity). On examination, the orthopaedist found no indications of neurological or vascular changes, but active flexion/abduction was limited to 80°, despite full passive motion. No instability signs were present with passive range of motion. Strength against manual resistance for most shoulder muscles was fair; however, the patient had a positive drop-arm test and an external rotation lag sign. Further imaging was ordered by the orthopaedist because of the rotator cuff weakness
		Cauda Equina Compression in the Absence of Neurologic Signs
			The patient was a 45-year-old man, referred from an interventional spine specialist to a hospital-based physical therapy clinic, with a 4-year history of chronic low back pain, intensifying insidiously over the previous 8 months. On physical examination, generalized, severe low back pain was increased with all motions, with no directional preference or abnormal neurologic signs. Radiographs from 5 months prior demonstrated preserved lumbar vertebral body heights and disc spaces, without obvious acute abnormality or deformity. The initial physical therapy diagnosis was nonspecific low back pain; treatment involved a home exercise program and pain education. The patient reported worsening symptoms at 2-week follow-up.
		Cervical Myelopathy in a Patient Referred for Lower Extremity Symptoms
			The patient was a 38-year-old male referred to physical therapy with complaints of right lower extremity radicular pain and left lower extremity weakness. He reported that his right lower extremity pain had gradually increased over a period of 4 weeks, with an insidious onset. He recently noticed left lower extremity weakness, stating that his leg “gave out” while playing badminton 1 week prior to referral to physical therapy. He stated that the next day, his right lower extremity pain increased to an intensity of 7/10. He also then reported difficulty flexing his left knee, feeling as though he had to drag his left lower extremity while walking. He denied any history of lumbar, thoracic, or cervical trauma. His remaining past medical history was noncontributory. Pertinent physical examination findings included positive clonus of bilateral ankles, left patellar tendon hyperreflexia/Hyper-reflexia (3+), and decreased right L5-S1 myotome function with increased spasticity of bilateral lower extremities (right, 1+; left, 2+ on the modified Ashworth scale). The therapist was unable to reproduce the patient’s symptoms with lumbar, thoracic, or cervical provocation testing. The patient demonstrated normal upper extremity motor function and reflexes.
		Avascular Necrosis of the Femoral Head (x2 images)

////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
"PT 2231_Mod 2_Bone Review.pptx" [MSK1_C02.3]
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Purpose of Bone
	Storage of Ca++ (In form of CaPO4)
	Protection of vital organs
	Support for body and mechanical basis for movement

Embryonic Development of Bone ("Embryonic Development of Bone.png")
	Week 6
		Mesoderm formed from mesenchymal cells
		Differentiation into connective tissue
			Ligament, muscle, tendon, fascia
			Cartilage, bone
		Cartilaginous matrix formed which will be the scaffold for bone growth
	Week 7
		Cartilage cells:
			Hypertrophy and form longitudinal rows
			Calcification of intercellular substance
			Vascular growth brings osteoblasts
			Proliferation of collagen, proteoglycans, calcium salts (CaPO4)

	Bone growth occurs via intramembranous ossification or endochondral ossification (chondrification)
		Intramembranous ossification
			Direct ossification of the mesynchyme
			Occurs during the embryonic period within the mesynchymal tissue, without prior cartilage formation
			Formation of the periosteum
		Enchondral ossification
			Mesenchyme is chondrified into a cartilaginous bone model
			Bone forms later by replacing the membranous model with calcified cartilage
			Occurs only in the presence of blood supply
			Advances the ends of the cartilaginous model
	
	Month 6
		Resorption of the central canal has occurred and the medullary cavity has formed
	Birth
		Secondary centers of ossification have formed
			Endochondral ossification 
			Distal femoral epiphysis (present)
			Other epiphyses (at various ages)
			Separated from the metaphysis by the epiphyseal plate
				Allows for growth in length of long bones
				Interstitial growth
			Increase in diameter (appositional growth) occurs by the deposition of osteoblasts on the interior surface of the diaphysis
	--------------------------------------------------
	Bone Growth and Remodeling
		Articular cartilage provides the framework for short and long bones
			Long – growth at the epiphysis
			Short – growth plate for entire bone
		Length
			Interstitial growth not possible alone. Length increases only by:
				Interstitial growth within the cartilage
				Endochondral ossification
			Epiphysial Plate Cartilage 
				Growth at the metaphysis and diaphysis of a long bone
					Interstitial growth of cartilage moves epiphysis further from metaphysis
					Calcification, death and replacement of cartilage (endochondral ossification)
		Width
			Appositional Growth:
				Activity of the osetoblasts
				Located in the deep layer of the periosteum
				Intramembranous ossification
			Osteoclastic resorption of the cortical bone results in increased medullary cavity size
		Four Zones of the epiphyseal plate
			1.Zone of resting cartilage
				Anchors the epiphyseal plate to the epiphysis
				Contains immature chondrocytes and delicate vessels
			2.Zone of young proliferation
				Site of the most active interstitial growth
			3.Zone of maturing cartilage
				Enlargement and maturation of the cartilage cells near the metaphysis
			4.Zone of calcifying cartilage
				Structurally the weakest zone
				Active boney deposition on the metaphysis
		Bone Growth and Remodeling
			Occurs because of the growth of long bones
			Occurs due to factors that demand removal of calcium from the bone
			Occurs because the Haversian systems are continually being eroded
		Bone Balance
			Describes the ratio of Osteoblasts VS Osteoclasts
				Bone balance is the result of osteoblastic VS osteoclastic activity
				Juvenile has a (+) bone balance
				Geriatric has a (-) bone balance
		Wolff’s Law (Law of Physical Stress)
			See "[Wolff_]"
	--------------------------------------------------
	Bone - Classification
		Long - Tubular (e.g. Humerus and Femur) have a body and 2 ends
		Short - Cuboidal (e.g. Carpals and Tarsals) found only in the foot or wrist
		Flat - Protective function (e.g. bones of cranial vault) help to form the walls of cavities
		Irregular - Bones of the face
		Sesamoid - Protect tendons from wearing (e.g. patella) (resembled sesame seeds)
	--------------------------------------------------
	Compact (Cortical) Bone
		Very dense, little space
		Highly organized lamellar network of fibers, packaged in osteons.
		Fibers in each layer or osteon oriented in different directions
	Cancellous (Trabecular) Bone
		Less dense, more space than compact
		More metabolically active
		Contain blood vessels, nerve fibers and fat
		Hemopoietic tissue
		Flat bones, metaphyseal regions of long bones
		More flexible
	--------------------------------------------------
	Mechanical Properties of Bone
		Must deal with various types of loading:
			Compression, Tension, Torsion, Shear
		Structure suited best for resisting compression loads
	Types of loads
		Bending Loads
		Torsion Loads
		Combined Loading
			Shear
			Compression
			Tension
	Role of Muscle in Redistribution of Stress on Bone


////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Bone Pathologies - Fracture pathologies [MSK1_C02.3]
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Fractures

Bone Pathologies
	Osteoporosis
		Means “porous bone”
		A pathological decrease in the density of the bone to the point where it actually looks like it has holes in it
		This greatly increases the risk of pathologic fractures
		-----------------------------
		Compression Fracture
			In the spine, osteoporosis often leads to a collapse of the vertebral body called a compression fracture
			This is quite common in older adults, especially older women
				and it is correlated to hormonal decreases in estrogen levels (particularly during menopause)
		Malignancy
			Osteosacroma
				It is the most common type of bone cancer, and it usually affects teenagers and young adults
				Tumors usually form at the ends of long bones, especially the femur, tibia, and humerus
				-----------------------------------------------------
				Two types of lesions are associated with osteosarcoma:
					May cause blastic lesions, in which the tumors fills the bone with extra cells
					May also cause lytic lesions, in which the tumor causes the destruction of bone material
			Multiple Myeloma ("01 1.2j Surviving Multiple Myeloma - Mayo Clinic.mp4")
				Multiple myeloma is another bone cancer, which affects the plasma cells in bone marrow
					-The cancer cells “crowd out” the bone marrow cells
					-Within the bone, the cancer cells signal the osteoclasts to break down bone at a high rate, so bone is destroyed and not rebuilt
					-This results in what are called “punched-out lesions”, which you can see in the X-rays below. It looks as though someone took a hole-puncher to the bones
	-----------------------------------------
	Knowledge Check
		Bone cancer that affects the plasma cells in bone marrow - Multiple Myeloma
		Pathological decrease in the density of the bone; means "porous bone" - Osteoporosis
		The most common form of bone cancers. Associated with blastic and lytic lesions - Osteosacroma

Rehabilitation for Total Hip Arthroplasty [MSK1_L07]
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
This goes beyond hip precautions seen in Patient Management
See also [MSK1_L07_m.StrainIntrv]
Most of the time you will see a posterior aproach for these type of interventions

Early Rehab Stage (first coupple of days in the hospital)
	< = less
	Strengthening of significantly weak (often <3/5) hip and knee musculature
		This is what you will see normally since they haven't been using the muscles
		Neuromuscular training is essential since they have a new, foreign body inside them
		What is a good start for this case? We want to start right away firing (from fire, activating) the mm.
			Assisted Isometrics
				You can use your hands, a towel, an elastic band, etc.
	Exercises for hip range of motion (w/in limits) PENDENT de millorar ¿?
		ABDuctors
		ADDuctors
			---------------------------
			Quads style
			For he knee: Put a plank undernith and make them them slide the legs
			For the hip: bike
				Make some passive stuff with them first before jumping to the bike and to see what they tolerate
	Flexibility of shortened structures (w/in limits)
		Depending on the approach we will select the stretchings
		In this case, hip flexors will be specially tight; we want to look into that
	Gait training
		What assisted device you would use early on (if they are up and walking)?
			Walkers for older patients, may be crutches for younger ones
		How do we progress this ones?
			Canes
		What parts of the gate you want to work on mainly?
			Weight shift since they have to start grabing a feel on the new part they have on them and trust it
				Short steps first and progress from there
Rehab Progression (if eveything is going well) - THA
	Progress hip ABD strengthening
		Clink shells exercises
			First, something on a mat
		Any movements on bans later on
		Side stteping
			With elastic band later on
	Progress hip EXT strengthening
		For neuromuscular training first:
			First, Hip flexor stretches in prone
			Later, Hip extensions
		Glut Bridges and progress to single leg, etc.
	CKC strengthening (functional)
		Mid squats and full squats later on
		Step ups in various directions
		Lunges
	Continue gait training to reduce abnormalities
		With antalgic walking, the struggles are the correct step lenght
		Marches in place

.When performing Varus and Valgus Testing at the Knee, testing with the knee slightly flexed should be performed: [MSK1_QValTest]
("06_07 Varus and Valgus Stress Tests.mp4")

b.Prior to testing without flexion
c.For Varus Testing but not Valgus Testing

Only when testing without flexion is normal [MSK1_Live08Knee 54:51sec] (!!)
	We first try in full exension
		Screw mechanism is applied in this position (knee locked) and
		A twisting of the Cruciates
	So, there is less motion in this position in general, for varus and valgus specially
		So, no much stress on MCL & LCL
	If you get provocation of symptoms in full extended, there is no need to go to flexion
	When we flex the knee, we are putting more laxity on the ligaments
		So, we can move more or put more stress on varus and valgus

	('STUDENT PRACTICAL RUBRIC IMM 2B 2023.docx') 
		MSK 1 – Immersion 2B Practical Exam Description
			15 minutes prior to their assigned time slot, students will access their assigned case (on Canvas)
			Cases will include a history and exam findings for ROM, Strength Testing, and Flexibility Testing only
			Based on this information, students should be able to 
				-I.Identify multiple logical differential diagnoses as potential hypotheses that will guide further testing
				-II.State specific characteristics for which they should observe
				-III.Describe appropriate conclusions regarding the given exam findings
				-IV.Identify and perform appropriate joint mobility testing technique(s) for this patient
				-V.Identify and perform multiple appropriate special tests to rule in/out previous hypotheses/differential diagnoses 
					V1.INSTRUCTORS may choose from any relevant special tests and will provide findings to these
				-VI.Palpate relevant structures for the patient case (again, instructors provide findings)
				-VII.State multiple impairments that would be a treatment priority
				-VIII.Describe and perform/instruct/prescribe 1-2 interventions to address the priority impairment(s)
		Exam Rubric
			Hypotheses & Rationale for Differential DX
				Lists at least TWO relevant diff dx after reading case info with no/minor cueing 
			Observation Considerations
				Describes multiple (>1) relevant characteristics to look for in patient case with no/minor cueing 
			Joint Mobility Assessment
				Identifies appropriate technique/direction/rationale 
				Gives appropriate instructions to patient prior to/during testing
				Uses appropriate hand placements
				Uses proper force
				Performs appropriate direction of force
				Absence of other unanticipated technique
			Special Tests ID & Performance
				Identifies TWO appropriate special tests
				Gives appropriate instructions to patient prior to/during testing
				Effectively performs psychomotor aspects of test
					Hand placements
					Technique & Body Mechanics
					Other aspects of psychomotor performance
			Palpation
				Identifies/Locates appropriate structures/landmarks for tenderness
				Palpates in an effective psychomotor manner
			Priority Impairments & Interventions
				Lists TWO priority impairments w/min cueing based on findings
				Lists ONE SPECIFIC & appropriate intervention for each impairment w/min cueing
			Performance of Interventions
				-Able to instruct/perform (1 or 2 as time permits) appropriate intervention(s) from those listed w/min cueing
					Proper instructions to patient prior to/during performance
					Proper psychometric performance/prescription of exercise
			Safety
				Failure to execute ANY skill in a safe manner will require remediation/retake
			----------------------------------------------------------------
			Hypotheses
			Observation
			ROM/MMT/Flex Interpretation
			Jt Mob Assessment
			Special Tests
			Palpation
			Impairments
			Interventions
			----------------------------------------------------------------
			Comments for the exam ¿?
				Basic exercises to adress the imparements that we are already indentifying at the immersion
				How to teach the patient what and how to do
					External cues are very usefull
				How to progress an exercise not only with weights
				What volume do we need for each exercise (not always 3x10)
					If pt. can tolerate exercises to recruit a muscle, there is never enough in this cases

	.Activity 1 - Manual Stretching, Self-Stretching, Neuromuscular Inhibition Stretching (Adjunct Ellie, Kin Sync 9, Ch. 7)
		A. Discuss specific examples of each
			Manual Stretching
				We want control over the stretching for the pt.
					Not to:
						-Overstretch (these could be painful, a recent surgery, post op with restrictions, etc)
						-Because pt. cannot do it itself
						-We want to achieve a more aggresive stretch
					Falta una rao; mirar en ANKI
		B. What are reasons to select each type over another type?
			This is practical for the pt. since it does not have to depend on pt.
			The reasons are: depending on the restrictions or injury
		C. Come up with one example of a patient situation
		in which it would be beneficial to prescribe or perform ONE of the three types of stretching and your rationale for this choice.
			-After individual work for a few minutes, describe your patient to the rest of the group
			-Others try to guess which type of stretching would be best for that patient and why

		.CASE x - The patient is a 55 y.o. female who sustained R elbow fx when falling from a ladder while changing light bulb 8 weeks ago
			Fractures were comminuted fractures of the proximal ulna and radius
			Underwent an open reduction internal fixation (O.R.I.F.) to repair
				Basically, this means fracture fragments were fixated with pins/screws
			Immobilized in a cast in a flexed postion x 8 weeks and had cast removed yesterday
			Radiographs indicate that fx's are stable and almost completely
			In addition to pain in her elbow she also has complaints of pain on the right side of her neck and Upper Traps
			She works as a custodian and participates in a Competitive Bowling League 2 nigts per week

			FINDINGS
				The following findings are evident on Exam
				.1st Posture:
					-Rounded shoulder posture (worse on R) (May be due to pectoral muscle tightness.) !! Needs Pectoral m. Manual Stretching AKA Stretching Intervention.
				.2nd Range of Motion:
					-1Ast. AROM: Full elbow flexion AROM
					-1Ast. AROM: Unable to actively extend the elbow past 90 degrees !! Needs AAROM on Elbow Extension. Conservative or in several sessions since (See 2And.) ¿?
						Contractile structures
							Needs stretching triceps before AROM or AAROM
								Regaining full ROM is before AROM
							They can perform also AROM on the available range; not necessary AAROM first
						Non-Contractile Estructures
							Join capsule too
					-2And. PROM: PROM slitghtty greater but very painful all around elbow with obvious muscle guarding. PROM Elbow Extension better but w/ Pain (high). !! Needs ,,, ¿? (See solution for 2And.)
					-1B&2Bnd. AROM/PROM: Pronation and supination AROM and PROM limited. !! Needs Manual Stretching first and AAROM second, AROM third for pronation and supination. ¿?
				.3th. Resistive testing (AKA MMT¿? or Selective tissue tension testing (resistive testing) of muscle to assess for muscle/tendon injuries):
					-Elbow flexion painful & weak !! AROM was full & no pain. Selective Tissue Tension test: elbow flexion painful & weak (no neurological problem). Due to the casting for 8 weeks (strophy).
					-Elbow extension weak & slightly painful when tested at 90 degrees. !! Same as elbow flexion. ",,,at 90 degrees" ¿?
				.Sensation:
					-Numbness/tingling in anterior-proximal-lateral forearm. !! Median n. & Ulnar n.
				.Palpation:
					-Distal biceps tendon painful to moderate pressure. !! Infalmation of thendon. AROM contraindicated. (A) Biceps: Forearm supination, Elbow Flexion especially when forearm is supinated, Short head adds stability to GH joint.

				See Live Session.
					1.List ALL of the patient's impairments
						Include anatomical region
					2.Which impairments are immediate treatment priorities?
					3.During initial visits, what general exercises would be included as part of this patient's treatment plan?
					4.Pick 2 non-exercise interventions to compliment this plan.
					5.How would the treatrnent plan change as the patient gains mobility?
					6.What specific stretching techniques/principles from this lecture might you incorporate?
						--------------------------------------------------
						1. See !! marks
						2.Regainig ROM (elasticity first: biceps, brachioradialis, supinator/pronator), the numbness/tingling (if this does not improve, then are facing a nerve entrapment)
						3. Do something always the first visit.
							Exercises to improve strech (upper triceps/levator scapulae. Contract-relax: triceps to allow biceps to relax and stretch) to the elbow: pulleys (after ultrasound), AAROM/AROM, ice or/and massage, heat to start things moving
						4. See 3
						5.Regaining the ROM (AROM); may be AAROM first. Starting being more aggresive with the stretches. Mechanical stretches
						6.
						After stretching we could start with isometrics contractions instead of AROM if they are too weak (isometrics contractions are part of neuromuscular inhibition techniques ¿?)

					By now, you should have some additional thoughts on the answers to these questions. Escoltar Live Session.

					.What are the reasons for performing stretching? Rounded shoulder posture (worse on R), unable to actively extend the elbow past 90 degrees, and pronation and supination AROM and PROM limited.

	.Kin Sync 8, Ch. 6?
		TECHNICALLY SPEAKING - What is the highest you can grade each of these individuals?
			-64 y.o. Female 5 weeks post total knee replacement lacks 20 deg of knee extension both actively and passively with firm end feel for both
				Quadriceps MMT grade?
			-44 y.o. Male has shoulder ABD AROM of 95 degrees and PROM of 120 degrees due to severe pain at the end of these ranges (end feel empty)
				Shoulder ABD MMT grade?
			-12 y.o. female with cerebral palsy has spasticity of the right biceps that limits active and passive elbow extension by 30%
				Triceps MMT grade?
				---------------------------------------------
				The fact that they have pain, does not mean thta they are week. If we have somebody with a recent surgery (not time to get weak). So, there is something esle preventing the movement and that is why we perform MMT.
				---------------------------------------------
				-None of these individuals can move thorugh full range against gavity (or otherwise)
				-So, thechnically we cannot grade them at
					3/5 - However they are not really that weak. What do we do about this? NO consistent standard. Different PTs socument different ways. See the recommentdarion
					2/5?
						----------------------------------------
						Course Recommendation:
							-If patient can take some resistance but cannot move thru full range for a reason other than weakness ...
								MMT= X/5 (3+, 4-, 4 etc. within the available range of motion
									I.e.: a pt. cannot move all the way but can take some resistance inside the available range
								OR if due to pain ...
									Document as Selective Tissue Tension Tesion
						---------------------------------------
						During Testing, Position Matters Active vs Passive Insufficiency
							-Passive insufficiency
								-When ROM limited because a muscle is stretched across 2 or more joints
									-Bend to wrist with a fist or with the jand relaxed. I.e.: pt. with a tight rectus femoris (2 joint mm) trying to make knee flexion and hip extension at the same time
									-That's why we have to think about position when measuring ROM
								Example: Passive wrist flexion
									w/hand in a fist
									w/ fingers relaxed
							-Active insufficiency (Some philosophy with AROM)
								-When a muscle contracts w/less tension b/c it is situated in a shortened position
									-Origin and insertion positioned closer together prior to contraction
								-Example: Force generated by hamstrings during hip extension
									w/knee extended
									w/knee flexed
						----------------------------------------
						MMT General Hip Extension __¿?__ Active Insufficiency in Action
							('2024-07-30 13_01_21-StrokesPlus.png')
								Here, we test both, the gluts and the hamstrings
								As long as you stabilize enough to prevent substitution, it does not matter if is ipsilateral or contralateral.
							('2024-07-30 13_02_13-StrokesPlus.png')
								Here, we isolate the gluteus maximum
								This achieves active insuficiency; we shorthen as much as we can the Hamstrings, then more load on the glut max

	.Ch. 09
		When observing scapular mechanics, consider:
			.Do the scapulae move in similar or different patterns?
			.Are movement patterns of the scapulae as expected or different than expected?
			.Do the scapulae move as much as they should?
			.Do they move more than they should?
		-Active Shoulder Motion should be assessed in a standing or seated position and typically includes observation and if necessary measurement of the following movements:
			.Shoulder Flexion
			.Shoulder ABDuction
			.Shoulder Scaption (Observed only)
			.Functional External Rotation (Observed only)
				-Reach over and behind head
			.Functional Internal Rotation (Observed only)
				-Reach behind and up back
			.Shoulder ER and IR in 90/90 Position (Observed only)
			.Shoulder Extension
				-May be observed and measured but not typically meaningful or helpful information

	.Breakout 1 - Selective Tissue Tension Testing (SINSS) (SINSS?, no serà STTT ¿?)
		1. Discuss interpretation of the four different possible outcomes of STTs
		2. Identify and discuss the role of SINNS associated with the case provided
			-------------------------------------------------
			``Hypothesis for Each of the Possible outcomes of STT (In relation to the muscle)
				Painless and Strong
				Painful and Strong
					-If it is strong, there are no tears on it
						However, something is wrong; a mininal injury
				Painful and Weak
					-It seems to be a more severe injury
				Painless and Weak
					-Atrophy
					-Neurological (nerve supply)
					-Total rupture of the tendon (it does not hurt), but there will be profound weakness
		-----------------------------------------------------
		Joseph the amazing PT is performing ROM and Manual Muscle Testing on a patient with a knee injury. After performing these tests, the patient reports his knee pain has increased from a 4/10 (initial severity) to an 7/10 (this is the irritability factor). Upon returning the next visit, the patient reports that the pain remained around a 7/10 for several hours before subsiding. Based on this information:
			-------------------------------------------------
			Other than numbers to indicate severity
				-Functional behaviour would be another factor that indicates severity
				-Are they able to work?
				-How are they sleeping? (pain at rest) it tell us about irritability too
				-Do they need to take medications to releaf the pain?
			-------------------------------------------------
			.Waldo the amazing PT is performing ROM ana Manual Muscle Testing on a patient with a knee Injury. After performing these tests, the patient reports his knee pain has increased from a 4/10 to an 7/10. Upon returning the next visit (at least 24 h. or more), the patient reports that the pain remained around a 7/1 0 for several hours before subsiding.
				Based on this information:
					-How would you classify the severity of the patient's symptoms (After activity, it would be irritability. We are talking here about severity)?
						What information supports this?
					-How would you classify the irritability of the patient's symptoms?
						What information supports this? (Because it remained several hours)
							It is a judgement call but moderate/moderate-high
					How would the patient's levels of severity and/or irritability influence your approach to examination and treatment?
						-Try to find out though ROM which is the movement that causes the irritability in order to avoid it, so we can continue our examination for the day at least
						-Let's say that every movement seems to cause pain.
							-You  would have to reduce the treatment to what you think is most important for the day and save the least important for the next visit. The same for the treatment plan.
							-Apply treatnment exercises very simple for the day.
								They can loose their confidence with you for the next visit, because of the pain they will have that day
				-----------------------------------------------
				How would determining that a patient has higher levels of severity and/or irritability influence your approach to examination and treatment?
					A. should be maximally thorough but initial treatments should be conservative
					B. Both examination and initial treatments should be somewhat conservative
					C. Examination should be conservative but initial treatments sheuld be maximally thorough
					D. Both examination and initial treatments should be maximally thorough so these can be reduced as soon as possible
					E. Examination should be conservative and no treatment should be performed until the next visit
						---------------------------------------------------------
						There will be profound weakness
						Always start some treatment if not derrimental
